# Detailed curriculum: 1.7 Finance basics and 2.3 Projects

Blueprint work file, Stage 2 Phase 1. Draft 1, 6 October 2026.

**Source of truth.** `docs/curriculum.md` for stages, topics, hours and exit tests. This file expands item 1.7 Finance basics (100 h, Stage 1, alongside 1.3 to 1.6) and item 2.3 Projects (400 h, Stage 2) into courses, levels, lessons, homework, topic exams, reading and verification, rebuilds both exit tests in the app, and writes one lesson in full.

**Built on.** `docs/design-research.md` sections 5, 8, 9 and 10 (lesson anatomy, curriculum architecture, teaching style, critiques); `docs/blueprint/work/widgets-finance-games.md` (the finance, projects and interview widget catalogue: its section 1 working outline, 1.7.1 to 1.7.9 and 2.3.0 to 2.3.5, is the outline this file replaces; its widget ids, modes, decisions and misconception register are used as written); `widgets-ml-research.md` (`sharpe-lab`, `overfit-lab`, `time-cv-lab`, `viva`, `portfolio-sort`, `factor-model-lab`, `bar-sampler`, `ml-workbench`); `widgets-prob-stats-ts.md`, `widgets-calc-linalg.md`, `widgets-programming.md` and `widgets-stage0.md` for the shared inputs; `CONTENT-FORMAT.md` revision 2 (ids, screen types, checks, tutor fields, drills, projects); `ASSESSMENT.md` draft 2 (the ladder, homework rules, paper kinds, exit parts, the project record and "finished", the track plan and its `projectOrder`); `AI-TUTOR.md` (Bertie's availability); `ARCHITECTURE.md` (what runs where); `canon-gap.md` revision 2 (the resource canon for 1.7 and 2.3 and proposals P1, P3, P6, P11 and P12, which overlap this file); the sibling curriculum files in `docs/curriculum/`, whose lessons this file cites and does not repeat; `spikes/README.md`; the approved prototype's course map (`prototype/js/data.js`); the owner's lesson-creator, maths-teaching, code-craft and project-forge skills, with `project-forge/references/engineering-floor.md` and `blueprint.md`.

**Tags.** [our choice] marks a design decision. [check] marks a resource detail (a chapter, section, lecture, module or problem number or title, a URL path, a date or a regulatory fact) that I recall but could not confirm from this environment; confirm it before the content cites it. [recalled] marks a fact from memory that I am confident of but did not verify here. [unverified] marks a platform behaviour not yet measured. [mode to add] marks a widget mode the catalogues do not list (open item 4).

**Answers.** Every number in sections 2 (hooks that state a number), 3, 4 and 7 was recomputed on 6 October 2026 with Python 3.11, NumPy 2.4.6, SciPy 1.17.1 and SymPy 1.14: exact `fractions` for every tree, parity, margin and ledger answer; SciPy `brentq` for yields, internal rates of return and implied volatilities; closed forms checked against a second method (finite differences against Greeks, trees against Black-Scholes, enumeration against formulas, simulation against expectations). The verification log at the end lists what was run.

**Currency in prose.** A dollar sign opens inline maths here, so prose writes "USD 100" or "100 dollars"; pounds use £. Prices in order books and option chains are written as bare numbers.

**At a glance.**

| Item | Hours | Courses | Levels | Lessons | Lesson hours | Homework sets | Topic exam | Estimates against hours |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1.7 Finance basics | 100 | 5 | 14 | 80 | 20.0 (20.0%) | 14 | `exm.fin`: 90 min, 12 questions, 100 marks; pass 70% with 70% on each of the three exit slots | 94.0 h (94.0%) |
| 2.3 Projects | 400 | 5 | 41 | 229 | 57.25 (14.3%) | 37 sets and 4 milestone packs | `exm.proj.methods`: 90 min, 11 questions, 100 marks, 30 of them a defence of the learner's own projects; pass 70% | 389.5 h (97.4%) |
| **Total** | 500 | 10 | 55 | 309 | 77.25 | 55 | 2 | 483.5 h |

"Estimates against hours" counts what the content schema can carry as estimates (lesson minutes, set and paper estimates, `Level.companion` hours, exit `estHours`, project milestone hours); redo time and practice papers are spent time only, as in the sibling files. Both items sit inside CI's 10% band (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.8).

**Exits.** `1.7.exit` is gated by `exm.fin` at 70% with `slotMin` 0.7 on its two explanation slots and its derivation slot, plus the capstone drill `drl.fin.exit-binomial` (section 4.1.3). `2.3.exit` is gated by three project records marked finished, each with tests and a results note (section 4.2.3).

---

## 0. Scope, decisions and conventions

### 0.1 Decisions

| # | Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Unit of lesson time | **15 minutes per lesson, quiz included**, the unit of every sibling file | 10 or 12 minutes | One unit across the path keeps hours tables comparable; design research 5.8 allows 10 to 15 minutes of screens, and the quiz is part of the sitting |
| 2 | 1.7 course structure | **Five courses**: Rates and Bonds (RB), Shares and Portfolios (EQ), Exchanges and the Order Book (MK), Forwards, Futures and Options (DV), Pricing Options (OP). They refine the approved prototype's four: its Markets and Instruments splits into RB and EQ; Market Microstructure becomes MK and moves before derivatives; Options and Payoffs becomes DV and gains forwards and futures; Pricing with Trees becomes OP and gains Black-Scholes, the Greeks and implied volatility | The prototype's four courses unchanged (68 lessons, no bonds, no futures); the widget catalogue's nine working courses (129 lessons, 32 h, 32% of the item) | The curriculum's cover line names bonds, interest, forwards, futures, Black-Scholes and the Greeks, which the prototype's map had no room for. Nine courses break the five-course cap and the lesson budget. The prototype was a navigation mock; its course names survive where they fit |
| 3 | 1.7 order | **The curriculum's resource order**: Khan Academy (interest, bonds, shares), then Varsity (stock markets, futures, options), then Hull (forwards to Greeks), with Harris beside MK. Course order RB, EQ, MK, DV, OP follows it | Derivatives first, because interviews ask about options most | "Use, in order" is the curriculum's instruction; RB's discounting is the foundation every later price stands on; MK carries the first exit part and needs only Stage 0 maths |
| 4 | 1.7 alongside 1.3 to 1.6 | **Skill gates, not item gates.** RB, EQ and MK need Stage 0 only (0.1 percentages and compound interest, 0.3 exponentials, logarithms and level C4 conditioning). DV needs RB1. OP1 needs 1.5 level V2 (expectation) and 1.1 (Python). OP2 and OP3 need 1.3 lessons 103 and 105 (the normal CDF series and Taylor in finance) and 1.5 levels V5 and V7 (the normal and the lognormal). The planner (`ASSESSMENT.md` 8.3) unlocks each course when its skills are learned | Unlocking all of 1.7 from the start of 1.3; holding 1.7 until 1.6 ends | "Alongside 1.3 to 1.6" in the curriculum; the widget catalogue's decision 14 ships RB to MK first because they need no calculus. Pace: 1.7 takes about 12% of Main, about 1.1 h a week at 15 h, so RB to MK fall during 1.3 and 1.4 and OP during 1.5 and 1.6, when its prerequisites arrive |
| 5 | Boundary with sibling files | **1.7 does not re-teach** returns, annualised volatility, drawdowns, adjusted prices in pandas and look-ahead (1.1 lessons 93 to 98); portfolio variance (1.5 lesson 96); the minimum-variance weight (1.3 lesson 128, 1.4 lesson 80); Taylor approximations of duration and delta (1.3 lesson 105); the lognormal mean (1.5 lesson 86); Breeden-Litzenberger (1.3 lesson 74); Bayes' rule (1.5 course H). It uses them, recaps each in one screen where a learner may arrive first, and spends its time on the finance | Re-teaching each where 1.7 needs it | 1.7 runs alongside, so some learners meet a 1.7 lesson before the sibling lesson. The recap screen and the skill gate (decision 4) cover that without duplicating content |
| 6 | Rates and conventions | **Periodic compounding in RB and EQ** (bond markets quote that way); **continuous compounding from DV on** (Hull's convention for derivatives); every rate on screen carries a convention chip ("5%, continuous"; "4%, annual, ACT/365") | One convention everywhere | Widget catalogue decision 4: the chip stops silent switching, the commonest source of wrong derivative prices in homework |
| 7 | Notation clash on $q$ | **$q$ is the risk-neutral probability** in trees (Shreve, the widget catalogue), **$\delta$ is the continuous dividend yield** (Hull writes $q$), and **$1 - p$ is written out** where 1.5 would write $q$ | Hull's $q$ for the dividend yield | Three meanings of one letter in one course is two too many. Every screen that cites Hull says "Hull writes $q$ for $\delta$". Request to the catalogue: rename `BinomialTreeConfig.q` to `divYield` (open item 4) |
| 8 | Normal CDF | **$N(\cdot)$ in 1.7**, as Hull, the Black-Scholes literature and interviewers write it, with a first-use note "$N$ is $\Phi$ from 1.5" | $\Phi$ throughout | The learner reads Hull beside the lessons and hears $N(d_1)$ at interview |
| 9 | Currency | **£ in RB and EQ1** (savings, mortgages, gilts); **USD from EQ2 on** (US shares, option chains, futures), written "USD" in prose | One currency | Personal finance lands in the learner's own currency; markets data and the free option chains of 2.3 are American (widget catalogue 1.1) |
| 10 | What games and simulations grade | **Decisions against exact expectations**, never realised P&L (widget catalogue decision 6) | Grading P&L | Good quoting loses money on some seeds |
| 11 | 2.3 structure | **Five courses**: Research Method (RM), shared by all projects; The Backtesting Engine (BT, project 1); Alpha Research and Portfolio Construction (AR, project 2); Options Analytics (OA, project 3); Market Data and Trading Systems (MS, projects 4 and 5). Each project course ends with a project level whose homework is the project's milestone pack | One course per project with method scattered inside them; six courses | The brief caps an item at five courses. Method (biases, inference, overfitting, reproducibility, the results note) is the same for every project and is what a sceptical reader checks first, so it gets its own course and comes first. Projects 4 and 5 share their infrastructure (the curriculum builds 5 on 1 and 4) |
| 12 | Lessons against projects built | **Every learner takes all five courses' lessons and builds three projects.** "Do three properly, not five thinly" governs building. A researcher is asked how tick data is stored; a developer is asked what an information coefficient is | Lessons only for the three projects built (about 160 lessons, 10% of the item) | Interviews test all five areas; every course is also a prerequisite of some 3.1 replication (Avellaneda and Stoikov needs MS, Jegadeesh and Titman needs AR) |
| 13 | Which three projects | **`ASSESSMENT.md`'s `projectOrder`**: researcher backtest, alpha, options; trader options, backtest, alpha; developer backtest, market-data (in C++, as the curriculum says), alpha. The trading bot (project 5) is an optional fourth on the developer track, built on projects 1 and 4, and is never one of the default three | The bot as a default project | The curriculum ranks the bot last ("retail technical analysis... carries no weight with a professional reader"). The trader track's "one project" against the stage exit's three stays open (`ASSESSMENT.md` 2.11) |
| 14 | How projects are run | **The owner's project-forge process**: each project's milestones are its three phases, Blueprint (`docs/PLAN.md` with the eleven sections and a threat class), Build (stages, each reviewed, tests inside the stage that creates the exposure) and README (dual register, `docs/DECISIONS.md`, the pre-push gate), plus the curriculum's one-page results note including what failed. Threat classes: backtester A, alpha A, options A, market data B, bot B | Free-form projects with a checklist at the end | The owner's own standard for a GitHub project, and the engineering floor is what separates a project that proves something from a vibe-coded one. Teaching it on the first project makes it a habit for 3.1 |
| 15 | The options dashboard | **A static site** built from cached data by a script and published on GitHub Pages; no server | A Streamlit or Dash server | A static build is threat class A, costs nothing to host, and a reader can open it from the README. A server is class C (input validation, authentication) for no gain in what the project proves |
| 16 | Market data in the app | **Synthetic only**, from `market-gen`, with known truths (true information coefficient, true volatility, planted biases and arbitrage); real data only in the learner's own repositories, cached to disk | Bundled price or option-chain snapshots | Widget catalogue decision 8: redistribution rights for free data are unclear, and known truths make items gradeable. The curriculum: "yfinance breaks often, so cache data to disk" |
| 17 | 2.3 lesson share | **229 lessons, 57.25 h, 14.3%**, just under the brief's 15 to 20% band | 240 lessons (15%) | Every lesson hour is an hour off three projects that already get 70 h each. The 229 cover every concept the five projects need (section 2.3); padding to 240 would add lessons without adding ideas |
| 18 | 2.3 topic exam | **A methods paper, `exm.proj.methods`**, 90 minutes, with a 30-mark section in which the learner defends their own finished projects in writing. It closes the topic with the exit; it is not an exit part | No topic exam, as `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.8 lists 2.3 | Method knowledge has to be examined independently of one's own code, and the defence section rehearses the interview question every project gets ("why should I believe this?"). Change request to `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.8 (open item 2); if declined, the paper runs as an ungated practice paper and nothing else changes |
| 19 | Boundary of 2.3's method course | **RM owns applied backtest statistics** (Sharpe uncertainty, the deflated Sharpe ratio, CSCV, the reality check, purged and combinatorial cross-validation, minimum track record). It recaps 1.6 lessons 19 and 38 to 42 and 2.2 lesson 21 in one screen each | Leaving them to 2.1's catalogue outline 2.1.9 | The developer track reaches 2.3 before 2.1 (`ASSESSMENT.md` plan). Request: 2.1.9 keeps its ML-specific lessons (leaderboards, model-selection protocol) and cites RM; a learner who has done either passes the other's level check (open item 3) |
| 20 | Overlap with canon-gap proposals | **Teach inside the budget now; move later without renumbering.** If P1 (research method, 35 h) is accepted, RM3 to RM5 and RM7 become its core and 2.3 returns their hours to building. If P6 (portfolio theory) is accepted, EQ2 and AR4 to AR5 become its preview. If P11 (financial econometrics) is accepted, RM8 moves there. If P12 (microstructure) is accepted, MK3 is its first level | Waiting for the owner's decision | Ids are immutable and display numbers come from position (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 4.1), so a move changes data, not learner records |
| 21 | Code runtimes | **1.7 drills use the standard library and NumPy and run on both targets**, because Pyodide with NumPy and pandas runs inside the Artifact (`spikes/README.md`, 6 October 2026, the owner's iPhone). **2.3 drills use NumPy and pandas on both targets**; drills that need SciPy, statsmodels or scikit-learn declare `needs` and hand off with a result code where the Artifact build lacks them (`ARCHITECTURE.md` decision 16, `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.5). C++ runs only on the learner's machine | MicroPython drills in the Artifact and NumPy on the web only, as the brief's platform note assumed | The brief says Pyodide cannot run in the Artifact; the spike measured that a repackaged Pyodide does, with NumPy and pandas, so this file follows the measurement, as every sibling file now does. Standard-library drills are still written to be `micropython-ok` where it costs nothing |
| 22 | Recreated, not copied | **Every Hull, Varsity, Harris or interview-book example is re-parameterised with our own numbers and solutions** | Using the books' worked examples | Copyright, and widget catalogue decision 9. Exercises are cited by chapter and number only |
| 23 | Sample lesson | **Lesson 1, "What is money later worth today?"** (`lsn.rates.present-value`), in full in section 7 | The order book lesson (exit part 1); the backtest event loop (2.3's first engine lesson) | Every price in both items is a present value: a bond, a forward, an option as a discounted expectation, a strategy's cost of capital. It is also where the first two errors of finance live: adding money paid at different dates, and multiplying by the growth factor instead of dividing |
| 24 | Display codes | **Two letters, as `stage1-statistics-ts.md` uses**: RB, EQ, MK, DV, OP for 1.7; RM, BT, AR, OA, MS for 2.3 | One letter (clashes with D, H, I, M, N, O, V, X and Z in the sibling files) | Display only; ids do not depend on them |
| 25 | Exit hours | **Charged to the exams block** of each item | A separate block | The curriculum's hours must hold everything |

### 0.2 What 1.7 and 2.3 inherit, and do not repeat

| Topic | Taught in | 1.7 or 2.3 does |
|---|---|---|
| Percentages, compound interest, reverse percentages | 0.1, 0.2 (GCSE) | Lesson 1 starts from compounding forwards and turns it round into discounting; lesson 2 adds conventions |
| Exponentials, $e$, logarithms, geometric series | 0.3 levels G1 to G5 (codes confirmed in `stage0-precalc-computing.md`) | Continuous compounding (2), perpetuities and annuities as geometric series (4), doubling times (2) |
| Conditioning on trees | 0.3 level C4 | Glosten-Milgrom (43) is a two-branch tree before 1.5 formalises Bayes |
| Returns, log returns, annualised volatility, drawdowns, adjusted prices, point-in-time joins, look-ahead, quantile bins | 1.1 lessons 88 and 91 to 98 | EQ1 uses adjusted prices without re-teaching pandas; RM and BT build backtest machinery on top of them |
| Git, tests, pytest, CI, SQL | 1.2 lessons 9 to 32 | Every project is a repository with CI; MS5 uses SQL upserts |
| The normal CDF series; Taylor in finance (duration, convexity, delta, gamma); Breeden-Litzenberger; $e$ as the compounding factor | 1.3 lessons 6, 74, 103, 105 | OP uses the normal CDF; RB3 and OP3 use the Taylor expansions as tools; OA4 uses Breeden-Litzenberger on data |
| Minimum variance; correlation matrices; Cholesky; PCA; shrinkage | 1.3 lesson 128; 1.4 lessons 79 to 82 and 95 | EQ2 interprets them as portfolio choices; AR4 builds risk models from them |
| Expectation, the normal, the lognormal, portfolio variance, conditional expectation, martingales, Kelly preview | 1.5 lessons 52, 69 to 72, 86, 96, 142, 154 | OP prices as discounted expectations; BT5 sizes with Kelly; OA7 reads skew |
| The Sharpe ratio's standard error by the delta method; FWER, Holm, FDR; forking paths; the expected best of $N$ noise strategies and the $t > 3$ hurdle | 1.6 lessons 19, 38 to 42 | RM3 and RM4 apply them to the learner's own trials, adding autocorrelation, the block bootstrap, the deflated Sharpe ratio, CSCV and the reality check |
| Regression, HC and Newey-West errors, the statsmodels table | 1.6 lessons 43 to 69 | RM8 runs Fama-MacBeth, clustered errors and event studies; RM2 regresses strategies on factors |
| Leakage in time series; EWMA and GARCH volatility; VaR backtests; volatility targeting; cointegration and pairs trading | 2.2 lessons 21 to 31, 39 to 42 | BT5 implements volatility targeting inside the engine; BT6 runs the 2.2 pairs rule inside it; RM5 extends leakage to purging and embargo |
| Time-ordered cross-validation, regularisation, gradient boosting | 2.1 (catalogue outline 2.1.3 to 2.1.7) | AR7 applies them to cross-sectional prediction; on the developer track, where 2.3 comes first, AR7's lessons carry their own one-screen recaps |
| C++ syntax, the standard library, testing in C++ | 1.9 | MS6 is performance engineering, not a C++ course; the developer's project 4 assumes 1.9 |

### 0.3 Conventions for every lesson

**Ids** follow `CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 4.1. Courses `crs.rates`, `crs.equity`, `crs.markets`, `crs.derivatives`, `crs.pricing` (1.7) and `crs.method`, `crs.backtest`, `crs.alpha`, `crs.options-analytics`, `crs.systems` (2.3). Levels `lvl.<course>.<slug>`, lessons `lsn.<course>.<slug>`, homework `set.<course>.<level-slug>`, exams `exm.fin` and `exm.proj.methods`, projects `prj.fin.pricing-lab`, `prj.proj.backtester`, `prj.proj.alpha`, `prj.proj.options`, `prj.proj.market-data`, `prj.proj.bot`. Libraries use the topic namespaces `fin` (1.7) and `proj` (2.3): `skl.fin.*`, `mc.fin.*`, `gen.fin.*`, `drl.fin.*` and `skl.proj.*` and so on. Lesson slugs inside 2.3's options and systems courses carry a sub-prefix for their level (`lsn.options-analytics.optdata-*`, `lsn.systems.feeds-*` and so on). The tables write the full ids; an earlier draft wrote them short (`lsn.feeds.*`) and left the compiler to expand them, which the curriculum index pass replaced so that every id here is the id the build will use (`docs/CURRICULUM.md` section 8). The `#` column is the lesson's number within its item; display numbers come from position and are never stored.

**Every lesson** runs the design research 8.3 sequence: a hook on a canvas; free manipulation; the name for what the learner just did; a ladder of 2 to 4 screens adding one constraint each; the trap; the rule stated as a description of the learner's own steps, with at least one derived line the learner completes; a transfer screen; the summary card; then a quiz of 4 items at 1 mark each (Mastered at 3 of 4, `ASSESSMENT.md` decision 26). From the owner's skills each lesson also carries the "why this exists" frame, one common trap, at least one edge case, a worked example with a decision point where the naive route fails, "derive, don't state", and a motivated leap wherever a creative step appears (`derive` screens carry `leap: naive, fails, pointsTo`). Finance adds two rules [our choice]: **every price is checked by a second route on screen** (replication against expectation, formula against tree, bump against Greek), and **every rate shows its convention chip**. 2.3 adds one: **every performance number appears with its uncertainty or its trial count**.

**Bertie** is available on lesson screens after the first Check, after each quiz item is graded, on homework after submission and on project milestones; never during `exm.fin`, `exm.proj.methods` or a viva's answer phase (`AI-TUTOR.md` 2.2 and 2.12). His finance guidance: never state a price or an arbitrage profit before the learner's Check; prefer "what would you need to hold today to have exactly this later?", "which way does the money flow, and when?" and "what could the strategy see at that moment?" to statements. On projects he reviews a `PLAN.md` or a results note only on request, quoting the engineering floor or the rubric, and never writes project code: the project is the learner's evidence.

**Widgets** use the ids of `widgets-finance-games.md` section 12 (`price-ladder`, `cashflow-timeline`, `position-editor`, `order-ticket`, `quote-entry`, `voice-capture`, `scratch-pad`, `compounding-lab`, `tvm-timeline`, `share-lab`, `margin-lab`, `portfolio-lab`, `bond-lab`, `yield-curve-lab`, `duration-lab`, `order-book`, `auction-lab`, `carry-arb`, `futures-mtm`, `hedge-lab`, `payoff-builder`, `option-bounds`, `parity-lab`, `option-chain`, `bs-explorer`, `greeks-lab`, `pnl-attribution`, `iv-solver`, `tape-lab`, `adverse-selection`, `dealer-sim`, `execution-lab`, `backtest-lab`, `sharpe-lab`, `signal-lab`, `vol-surface`, `pricer-compare`, `feed-sim`, `candle-builder`, `latency-lab`, `order-lifecycle`, `paper-trader`, `results-note`, `bet-lab`, `kelly-lab`), with its extensions of sibling widgets (`binomial-tree`, `gbm-lab`, `hedging-sim`, `drawdown-lab`, `time-series-lab`, `json-explorer`, `schema-builder`, `setup-checklist` project mode, `code-cell` with the `zq_fin` and `zq_market` preludes, `free-response` with voice and rubric, `multiplier-chain`, `series-explorer`, `convexity-chord`, `sketch-pad`, `dist-explorer`, `regression-lab`, `multiple-testing`, `node-link`), the ML catalogue's `sharpe-lab`, `overfit-lab`, `time-cv-lab`, `labelling-lab`, `bar-sampler`, `cluster-lab`, `ml-workbench`, `viva`, `portfolio-sort`, `factor-model-lab`, `pairs-lab`, and the shared inputs (`choice`, `numeric-input`, `expression-input`, `derivation-steps`, `value-table`, `region-select`, `token-tray`, `slider`, `matrix`, `card-sort`, `sim-runner`, `plot-2d`, `bench-lab`, `code-surface`). Two catalogues both specify `sharpe-lab` and `signal-lab`; this file uses the ML catalogue's specification, which is the fuller one, and asks the engine blueprint to merge them (open item 4).

**Quiz item types** (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.4), the letters of the sibling files: N numeric, X expression, C single choice, M multi-select, O order the steps, S sort or match, I interval, P point or curve on a canvas, A matrix or vector entry, T predict the output of code, K code with tests, G reach a target state in a simulator. Long-form items appear in homework and exams, never in quizzes. Single and multiple choice stay at or below 40% of each course's quiz items (design research 10); section 0.4 gives the counts.

**Level review.** Every level ends with a Level review of 8 items at difficulty 2 to 4, interleaved, with up to 30% from earlier levels of the same course (`ASSESSMENT.md` 2.4). The homework set follows (section 3).

**Columns.** Objective is one measurable sentence. Hook is the puzzle the lesson opens with, answer in brackets where it is a number. Trap is the misconception the trap screen is built around; Edge is the fringe case the lesson must show.

**Notation, 1.7** (design research 9.4 plus Hull): $S_0$, $S_t$, $S_T$ for the share price; $K$ strike; $T$ maturity in years; $r$ the risk-free rate (periodic in RB and EQ with its chip, continuous from DV on); $\delta$ the continuous dividend yield; $y$ a bond yield; $D_{\text{mac}}$, $D_{\text{mod}}$, DV01; $F_0$ the forward price; $u$, $d$, $q$ in trees; $\sigma$ annualised volatility; $N(\cdot)$ the standard normal CDF; $\Delta$, $\Gamma$, $\Theta$, $\nu$ (vega), $\rho$ (rho, never correlation in OP; correlation there is $\rho_{12}$).

**Notation, 2.3**: $r_t$ a period return; $\widehat{SR}$ the estimated Sharpe ratio, always labelled per period or annualised; $T$ the number of periods; $N$ the number of trials; $\gamma_3$ skewness and $\gamma_4$ kurtosis (not excess); IC the information coefficient, BR breadth, TC the transfer coefficient; $k = \ln(K/F)$ log-moneyness and $w = \sigma^2 T$ total implied variance; p50 and p99 for latency percentiles.

| # | Item | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Display codes RB to OP and RM to MS against the other files | Display only |
| 2 | `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.8 lists 2.3 among topics with no topic exam; decision 18 adds `exm.proj.methods` | Owner decision; if declined the paper is ungated practice |

| 4 | Widget requests: rename `BinomialTreeConfig.q` to `divYield` (decision 7); merge the two `sharpe-lab` and `signal-lab` specifications; modes to add: `bond-lab` `bootstrap-exam` (a timed variant without the knot handles), `order-book` `sequence` (replay a scripted order list, MK1 homework), `vol-surface` `event` (short-dated term structures with an event jump, lesson 150), `pricer-compare` `pde` (lesson 164), `latency-lab` `histogram-from-log` (MS4), `order-lifecycle` `reconcile-diff` (lesson 218) [mode to add] | Without them those lessons fall back to `plot-2d` with `sim-runner` |
| 5 | Lesson-id sub-prefixes inside `crs.systems` and `crs.options-analytics` (decision in 0.3) | **Closed**: the tables now carry the full ids (`lsn.systems.feeds-ticks`, `lsn.options-analytics.optdata-chain-anatomy`), as `CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 4.1 requires the course as the lesson namespace |

Request: `projectKind` accepts a list (`['backtest', 'alpha', 'options', 'market-data', 'bot']`) with `count: 3` and `distinct: true` | Without it, the exit needs a new instrument kind |

## 1. Item overview

### 1.1 Item 1.7 Finance basics (100 h)

#### 1.1.1 Purpose in a quant or ML career

- **You will be asked about markets.** The curriculum's reason for making 1.7 required: trader and researcher interviews ask how an order book works, why a spread exists, what a forward should cost, why put-call parity holds, what delta and gamma are, and why implied volatility is not flat. These are asked as reasoning, not recall.
- **Every price is a present value or a no-arbitrage argument.** Bonds discount cash flows; forwards are priced by carry; options by replication, which becomes a discounted expectation under risk-neutral probabilities. One idea, the law of one price, runs from lesson 1 to lesson 80, and every 2.3 project uses it.
- **Microstructure is where strategies die.** Backtests ignore the spread, queue position, adverse selection and impact; live trading does not. MK gives 2.3's cost models their meaning, and 3.1's Avellaneda and Stoikov replication its background.
- **Risk is measured in the Greeks and in duration.** A risk report is a table of first and second derivatives (delta, gamma, vega; DV01, convexity). OP and RB3 teach the learner to read one and to explain a day's P&L with it.
- **ML labs meet the same ideas under other names.** Discounting is the return in reinforcement learning; convexity and Jensen's inequality explain volatility drag and the value of options; the variance of an equally weighted portfolio is the variance of an ensemble's average; market impact is a cost function a policy must learn.

#### 1.1.2 Learning outcomes

| # | Outcome | Measured by |
|---|---|---|
| FN-O1 | Discount and compound cash flows under any stated convention; compute NPV, IRR (and say when it misleads), annuities, perpetuities and loan schedules; convert real and nominal rates | 85% on fresh difficulty-3 items; `exm.fin` Q1 |
| FN-O2 | Price fixed-coupon and zero-coupon bonds, solve for yield to maturity, bootstrap discount factors, derive forward rates, and handle accrued interest and credit spreads | 85% on difficulty-3 items; `exm.fin` Q2 |
| FN-O3 | Measure interest-rate risk with duration, DV01 and convexity, hedge it, and value a swap from discount factors | 85% on difficulty-3 items; long-form at 7 of 10 (homework RB3) |
| FN-O4 | Explain shares, issuance, dividends, splits, buybacks and indices; value a share by dividend discounting; compute long, short and margin P&L and margin-call prices | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| FN-O5 | Compute portfolio risk and Sharpe ratios, explain diversification's floor, estimate beta and alpha, and state what efficient markets do and do not claim | 85% on difficulty-3 items; long-form at 7 of 10 (homework EQ2) |
| FN-O6 | Explain a limit order book, the spread, market against limit orders, priority, order types, auctions and the trade cycle | `exm.fin` Q4 and Q5, the exit's explanation slots, at 7 of 10 each |
| FN-O7 | Measure trading costs (quoted, effective and realised spreads, impact) and explain why spreads exist (costs, inventory, adverse selection), including the Glosten-Milgrom quotes | 85% on difficulty-3 items; `exm.fin` Q6; long-form at 7 of 10 (homework MK3) |
| FN-O8 | Price forwards and futures by cash and carry with income, costs and foreign interest, value existing forwards, run a futures margin account and size hedges | 85% on difficulty-3 items; `exm.fin` Q8 |
| FN-O9 | Build option payoffs and strategies, decompose any piecewise-linear payoff into calls, derive the no-arbitrage bounds and put-call parity, and trade violations | `exm.fin` Q7, the exit's derivation slot, at 7 of 10 |
| FN-O10 | Price European and American options on binomial trees by replication and risk-neutral expectation, in code | `drl.fin.exit-binomial` passing every hidden test; `exm.fin` Q10 |
| FN-O11 | Use the Black-Scholes formula, read $N(d_1)$ and $N(d_2)$, compute and interpret the Greeks, explain a P&L by them and solve for implied volatility | 85% on difficulty-3 items; `exm.fin` Q11 |
| FN-O12 | Pass the 1.7 exit | `exm.fin` at 70% with the three exit slots at 70%; the binomial drill (section 4.1.3) |

#### 1.1.3 Prerequisites

- **Stage 0 complete**: percentages and compound interest (0.1, 0.2), exponentials, logarithms, geometric series and conditioning on trees (0.3).
- **1.1 Python** for the drills and the Pricing Lab project; **1.2 Tools** for its repository and tests.
- **Alongside, by course** (decision 4): RB, EQ and MK need nothing beyond Stage 0; DV needs RB1; OP1 needs 1.5 level V2; OP2 and OP3 need 1.3 lessons 103 and 105 and 1.5 levels V5 and V7.

**Feeds.** 1.9 (the C++ rewrite of the binomial pricer), 2.2 (the Roll model, VaR, volatility), 2.3 (every project), 3.1 (Avellaneda and Stoikov; Jegadeesh and Titman), 3.2 (Shreve I starts from the one-step tree), 3.3 (market-making games are Glosten-Milgrom played live; betting and Kelly).

#### 1.1.4 Hours budget (100 h)

| Block | What | Hours | Share |
|---|---|---|---|
| In-app lessons | 80 lessons at 15 minutes, quiz included | 20.0 | 20.0% |
| In-app practice and redo | 14 level reviews at about 15 minutes (3.5 h); generated practice (4.0 h); the Redo queue at 7 and 30 days (4.5 h, spent time only) | 12.0 | 12.0% |
| Homework | 4 sets of 8 items at about 1 hour 30 minutes (levels of 4 or 5 lessons: RB3, MK2, DV2, OP2; 6.0 h); 10 sets of 10 items at about 2 hours (20.0 h) | 26.0 | 26.0% |
| Exams | One practice paper (1.5 h, spent only); `exm.fin` (1.5 h); marking and review (1.0 h) | 4.0 | 4.0% |
| External reading and lectures | Watching and reading 22.0 h (section 5.1): Khan Academy Finance and Capital Markets videos (1.75); Zerodha Varsity modules 1, 4 and 5, targeted chapters (3.75); Hull chapters 1 to 5, 7, 10 to 13, 15, 19 and 20, targeted sections (8.25); Harris chapters on orders, market structures, order-driven markets, dealers, spreads and liquidity (3.0) and Roll (1984) (0.25); MIT 15.401 lectures on present value, fixed income, equities, portfolios, forwards and options (3.75); Yale ECON 252 (0.75); MIT 18.S096 (0.5). External problems 4.0 h: Hull end-of-chapter practice questions with the Student Solutions Manual | 26.0 | 26.0% |
| Projects or drills | The Pricing Lab project (10.0 h, section 3.2); the exit drill (0.5 h); 3 extra code drills of 30 minutes beyond homework (1.5 h) | 12.0 | 12.0% |
| **Total** | | **100.0** | 100% |

Solving time (everything except the 22.0 h of watching and reading and the non-task screens of lessons) is about 72 h, 72%, above the curriculum's 60% floor (rule 2). Estimates the schema carries: 94.0 h, everything but the 4.5 h of redo and the 1.5 h practice paper, which are spent time only.

### 1.2 Item 2.3 Projects (400 h)

#### 1.2.1 Purpose in a quant or ML career

- **Projects are the evidence a self-taught CV needs.** The curriculum is blunt that a self-taught CV rarely clears the screen at top firms; three finished, tested projects with honest notes are the strongest thing a learner can show. "Every project ships with a README, tests, and a one-page results note that includes what failed."
- **The job is not fooling yourself.** A researcher's value is the false discoveries they do not ship: look-ahead, survivorship, costs ignored, the best of 200 trials reported as if it were the only one. RM and BT turn the curriculum's "treat any simple rule that backtests to a Sharpe above 2 as a bug" into a method with numbers attached.
- **Real research work.** Project 2 (cross-sectional alpha) is the curriculum's "closest of the five to real research work": universes, signals, the information coefficient, neutralisation, risk models, turnover and costs. AR is what a quant researcher does on a Monday.
- **Options analytics is what desks look at.** Implied volatility surfaces, arbitrage checks, three pricers compared, Greeks across strikes: OA turns 1.7's formulas into numerics that survive real quotes.
- **Systems are where developers are judged.** Feed handlers, order books, latency percentiles, idempotent storage, order state machines and kill switches (MS) are interview material for developer roles and the infrastructure of every live strategy.
- **ML labs run the same discipline.** Held-out sets touched once, every trial logged, error bars on every metric, pinned environments and a write-up that reports what failed: RM is research method for any empirical field, and 2.1 and 3.1 reuse it.

#### 1.2.2 Learning outcomes

| # | Outcome | Measured by |
|---|---|---|
| PJ-O1 | State a falsifiable hypothesis with an economic mechanism, pre-register a test and a held-out period, and source, cache and quality-check point-in-time data | Long-form at 7 of 10 (homework RM1); the pre-registration milestone of project 2 |
| PJ-O2 | Compute and interpret Sharpe, Sortino, information ratio, drawdown, Calmar, turnover, capacity and factor-adjusted alpha | 85% on difficulty-3 items; `exm.proj.methods` Q1 |
| PJ-O3 | Quantify the uncertainty of a strategy's performance (standard errors with skew, kurtosis and autocorrelation; block bootstrap; minimum track record; random-entry nulls) | 85% on difficulty-3 items; `exm.proj.methods` Q2 |
| PJ-O4 | Correct for selection among many trials (trial ledger, expected maximum, deflated Sharpe ratio, CSCV and PBO, reality check, $t > 3$) | `exm.proj.methods` Q3; every backtest and alpha results note reports its trial count and deflated Sharpe ratio |
| PJ-O5 | Validate through time (walk-forward, purging and embargo, CPCV, one look at the held-out period) | 85% on difficulty-3 items; long-form at 7 of 10 (homework RM5) |
| PJ-O6 | Build reproducible research repositories (layout, pinned environments, seeds, cached and versioned data, numerical tests, CI and the pre-push gate) | Every project's milestones; `setup-checklist` paste-verify |
| PJ-O7 | Write a one-page results note and a dual-register README, report failures, and defend a project under questioning | Results notes at 7 of 10; `exm.proj.methods` section B at 7 of 10 per question |
| PJ-O8 | Design, implement and test an event-driven backtesting engine with correct accounting, fills, costs, impact, constraints, sizing and a held-out report | Project 1 finished (if built); homework BT1 to BT7 |
| PJ-O9 | Research a cross-sectional signal end to end: universe, construction, normalisation, neutralisation, IC and its decay, quantiles, Fama-MacBeth, the fundamental law, risk models, portfolio construction, turnover, costs and capacity | Project 2 finished (if built); homework AR1 to AR7 |
| PJ-O10 | Build an implied volatility surface from real quotes with arbitrage checks and an SVI fit, compare formula, tree, Monte Carlo and PDE pricers, and compute Greeks and P&L explains in practice | Project 3 finished (if built); homework OA1 to OA7 |
| PJ-O11 | Design a market data handler and trading system: feeds, book reconstruction, candles, clocks, latency percentiles, queues, storage, order lifecycle, reconciliation and risk checks | Project 4 or 5 finished (if built); homework MS1 to MS8 |
| PJ-O12 | Pass the 2.3 exit | Three project records finished (section 4.2.3) |

#### 1.2.3 Prerequisites

- **Stage 1 complete** (the stage gate), in particular 1.1 and 1.2 (repositories, tests, CI), 1.6 lessons 19 and 38 to 69 (Sharpe standard errors, multiple testing, regression), 1.7 (every course), 1.8 (complexity, heaps, hash maps for MS) and, on the developer track, 1.9.
- **On the researcher and trader tracks** 2.1 and 2.2 come first (`ASSESSMENT.md` plan). **On the developer track** 2.3 comes first; RM5 and AR7 then carry one-screen recaps of 2.1's cross-validation and 2.2's leakage lesson, and the planner offers 2.2 lessons 21 and 31 (leakage, volatility targeting) as a two-lesson bridge before RM5 [our choice].

**Feeds.** 2.1's capstone (the method of RM, the purged folds of RM5), 2.4 (Kaggle and IMC Prosperity reuse BT and MS), 3.1 (every replication is run with RM's protocol and written with RM7's note; Avellaneda and Stoikov reuses MS and MK; Jegadeesh and Titman reuses AR), 3.3 (the project round of a mock interview, `mock-interview` project mode).

#### 1.2.4 Hours budget (400 h)

| Block | What | Hours | Share |
|---|---|---|---|
| In-app lessons | 229 lessons at 15 minutes, quiz included | 57.25 | 14.3% |
| In-app practice and redo | 41 level reviews at about 15 minutes (10.25 h); generated practice (6.0 h); the Redo queue (8.5 h, spent only) | 24.75 | 6.2% |
| Homework | 37 sets of 6 items at about 1 hour 30 minutes (55.5 h). The four project levels' homework is their milestone pack, counted under projects | 55.5 | 13.9% |
| Exams | One practice methods paper (2.0 h, spent only, two 45-minute halves); `exm.proj.methods` (1.5 h); marking and review (1.5 h) | 5.0 | 1.25% |
| External reading and lectures | Watching and reading 39.5 h (section 5.2: López de Prado ch. 2, 3, 7 and 11 to 14; Chan; Grinold and Kahn ch. 6, 14 and 16; Hull ch. 20 and 21; Gatheral ch. 1 to 3 and 11; Glasserman ch. 1 to 4; Kleppmann and Riccomini; the method and anomaly papers; MIT 18.S096, 15.450 and 6.172 lectures; CMU 15-445 lectures on storage). External problems 8.0 h (Glasserman and Hull numerical exercises, CS:APP practice problems, paper replications of single tables) | 47.5 | 11.9% |
| Projects or drills | Three projects at 70 h each (210.0 h), each split into milestones across project-forge's three phases (section 3.4) | 210.0 | 52.5% |
| **Total** | | **400.0** | 100% |

Solving time (everything except 39.5 h of watching and reading and the non-task screens of lessons) is about 335 h, 84%. Estimates the schema carries: 389.5 h, everything but the 8.5 h of redo and the 2.0 h practice paper.

**Per project, 70 h** [our choice]: Blueprint 8 h (data plan, `PLAN.md`, pre-registration where the project tests a hypothesis), Build 48 h (four to six reviewed stages), README and results note 10 h, pre-push gate 2 h, defence rehearsal 2 h. The optional fourth project (the bot) is outside the 400 h.

## 2. Courses

### 2.0 How to read the tables

Each course opens with its purpose and spine, then one table per level. Each level is 4 to 7 lessons, then a Level review (8 items) and a homework set (section 3). Lesson rows give: number and id; title; a one-sentence measurable objective; the hook (answer in brackets); the key widgets by id with the mode in brackets; the trap and an edge case; and the quiz item types (4 items, letters as in section 0.3). A "Transfer" line after each table names where the level's ideas are used in quant or ML work.

### 2.1 Item 1.7 Finance basics: five courses, 14 levels, 80 lessons

#### Course RB: Rates and Bonds (`crs.rates`, 3 levels, 16 lessons)

Money has a price in time. The course turns compounding round into discounting, defines the discount factor as the price today of £1 later, and builds everything else on it: NPV and IRR, annuities and loans, real rates, bonds and their yields, the zero curve and forward rates, then interest-rate risk (duration, convexity, DV01 hedges) and swaps. Spine: Khan Academy Finance and Capital Markets, interest and bonds units; Hull chapter 4 (Interest Rates) and chapter 7 (Swaps) for RB2 and RB3; MIT 15.401 lectures on present value and fixed income as the lecture parallel. Currency: pounds sterling.

**Level RB1: Interest and present value** (`lvl.rates.time-value`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `lsn.rates.present-value` What is money later worth today? (section 7) | Price a future cash flow by discounting, $PV = C(1 + r)^{-t}$, justify it by the deposit that replicates it, and compare cash flows at different dates only after moving them to one date | A prize: £10,000 today or £10,800 in two years, when savings earn 4% a year. Which is worth more? (£10,800 is worth £9,985.21 today, so take the £10,000) | `cashflow-timeline`, `tvm-timeline` (pv, discount-factors), `compounding-lab` (simple), `value-table` | Trap: comparing £10,800 with £10,000 directly, or multiplying by $1.04^2$ to "discount" (`sum-without-discounting`, `discount-multiplies`). Edge: a negative rate makes the discount factor above 1; a cash flow paid today is not discounted | N, N, C, M |
| 2 | `lsn.rates.compounding-conventions` Rates and their conventions: APR, effective and continuous | Convert between a rate compounded $m$ times a year, its effective annual rate $(1 + \frac{r}{m})^m - 1$ and its continuous equivalent $m\ln(1 + \frac{r}{m})$, and estimate doubling times | Bank A pays 4.9% compounded monthly; bank B pays 5% compounded once a year. Which pays more? (A: 5.0116% effective) | `compounding-lab` (frequency, continuous, doubling, compare) | Trap: reading an APR as the effective rate (`apr-is-ear`); the rule of 72 taken as exact (`rule-72-exact`: at 8% it gives 9 years, the truth is 9.006). Edge: compounding ever more often approaches $e^r - 1$, not infinity (`frequency-unbounded`); 5% annual is 4.879% continuous | N, N, O, C |
| 3 | `lsn.rates.npv-irr` NPV, IRR and the decision rule | Compute a project's NPV, find its IRR by root-finding, and say when the IRR misleads (more than one sign change, projects of different scale) | Invest £1,000 now for £600 in each of the next two years, at a 10% cost of money? (NPV £41.32, IRR 13.07%: invest) | `tvm-timeline` (npv, irr), `plot-2d` | Trap: one IRR always exists and ranks projects (`irr-unique`): flows $-100, +230, -132$ have IRRs of 10% and 20%. Edge: a project with a higher IRR can have the lower NPV when the scales differ | N, N, C, M |
| 4 | `lsn.rates.annuities` Perpetuities, annuities and growth | Derive the perpetuity value $\frac{C}{r}$ by the shift trick, the growing perpetuity $\frac{C}{r - g}$, and the annuity as the difference of two perpetuities | An endowment pays £50,000 a year for ever, the first payment in a year, at 5%. Worth? (£1m; growing at 2% a year, £1,666,667) | `tvm-timeline` (perpetuity, annuity), `series-explorer` (annuity preset), `derivation-steps` | Trap: $\frac{C}{r + g}$ for a growing stream (`growing-perpetuity-sign`); discounting the first payment of an annuity due (`annuity-due-off-by-one`). Edge: $g \ge r$ gives no finite value: the model, not the asset, has broken | X, N, N, C |
| 5 | `lsn.rates.loans` Loans, mortgages and amortisation | Compute the level payment of an amortising loan, split each payment into interest and principal, and find the balance at any date | A £200,000 mortgage over 25 years at 4.5% a year, paid monthly (0.375% a month). What payment clears it? (£1,111.66) | `tvm-timeline` (amortise), `plot-2d` | Trap: halfway through the term, half the loan is repaid (after 12.5 years £127,357, 63.7%, is still owed). Edge: a payment below the first month's interest (£750) never repays anything | N, N, C, P |
| 6 | `lsn.rates.real-nominal` Real and nominal: inflation | Convert nominal to real rates with $(1 + n) = (1 + r)(1 + \pi)$, deflate future cash flows, and discount real flows at real rates | Savings earn 5% while prices rise 3%. How much more can you buy in a year? (1.94%, not 2%) | `compounding-lab` (real), `tvm-timeline` | Trap: real rate as the difference $n - \pi$ (`real-is-difference`), close only when both are small; at 50% inflation and 60% interest the gap is 3.3 points. Edge: discounting real flows at a nominal rate counts inflation twice; index-linked gilts pay real cash flows | N, X, C, N |

Transfer: lesson 1's discount factor is the price of a zero-coupon bond (RB2) and the $e^{-rT}$ in every option formula (OP); lesson 3's NPV rule is how a firm values a project and how 2.3 judges whether a strategy beats its cost of capital; lesson 4 values a share by its dividends (lesson 20).

Then the Level review and homework `set.rates.time-value`.

**Level RB2: Bonds and yields** (`lvl.rates.bonds`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | `lsn.rates.bond-cash-flows` A bond is a stream of cash flows | Price a fixed-coupon bond as the present value of its coupons and principal, and explain why price and yield move in opposite directions | A 3-year bond pays a 5% annual coupon on 100. Price at a 4% yield, then at 6%? (102.78; 97.33) | `bond-lab` (cashflows, price-yield), `cashflow-timeline` | Trap: price and yield rise together. Edge: a zero-coupon bond is the one-flow case; its price-yield curve is the steepest for its maturity | N, N, C, P |
| 8 | `lsn.rates.par-premium-discount` Par, premium, discount and the pull to par | Predict from coupon against yield whether a bond trades above or below par, and trace its price towards par as maturity approaches at a fixed yield | Three 10-year bonds yield 4%, with coupons of 3%, 4% and 6%. Which trade above 100? (the 6%: 116.22; the 4% at par; the 3% at 91.89) | `bond-lab` (price-yield, pull-to-par) | Trap: a premium bond is a worse investment, because it "loses" value to par (the coupons repay it). Edge: at a constant yield every bond's price moves smoothly to 100 at maturity between coupon dates | C, S, N, P |
| 9 | `lsn.rates.ytm` Yield to maturity by root-finding | Solve for the yield to maturity from a price by bisection or Newton's method, and state what the yield assumes | A 2-year 6% annual bond trades at 101.50. Yield? (5.19%) | `bond-lab` (ytm), `code-cell`, `plot-2d` | Trap: the yield is the return you will earn (only if every coupon is reinvested at that yield and the issuer pays). Edge: Newton's method from a bad start can overshoot; bisection on a bracket cannot fail | N, O, C, K |
| 10 | `lsn.rates.zeros-bootstrap` Zero-coupon bonds, discount factors and bootstrapping | Read discount factors from zero prices, bootstrap the next discount factor from a coupon bond, and price any bond off the curve | A 1-year zero trades at 97; a 2-year 5% annual bond at 101. What is the 2-year discount factor? (0.91571, a zero rate of 4.50%) | `yield-curve-lab` (discount-factors, bootstrap), `matrix` | Trap: discounting every cash flow at the bond's own yield (4.47%) and calling it the 2-year rate. Edge: the two agree only when the curve is flat; bootstrapping must go shortest first | N, N, A, C |
| 11 | `lsn.rates.forward-rates` Forward rates and the shape of the curve | Derive forward rates by no arbitrage, $(1 + z_2)^2 = (1 + z_1)(1 + f_{1,2})$, and read normal, flat and inverted curves | The 1-year zero rate is 3% and the 2-year 4%. What one-year rate, fixed today for next year, makes the two routes cost the same? (5.01%) | `yield-curve-lab` (forwards, shapes), `cashflow-timeline` | Trap: the forward rate is the market's forecast of next year's rate (it also holds a term premium). Edge: an inverted curve, as US 2-year above 10-year in 2023 [check dates], means forwards below spot rates, not a bad deal for lenders | N, X, C, C |
| 12 | `lsn.rates.accrued-credit` Clean and dirty prices, day counts and credit spreads | Compute accrued interest and the dirty price from a clean quote under a stated day count, and convert a credit spread into a default intensity with $s \approx \lambda(1 - R)$ | A company's bond yields 2% more than a gilt of the same maturity; bondholders expect to recover 40% on default. Implied annual default probability? (about 3.33%) | `bond-lab` (accrued, credit) | Trap: the dirty price's sawtooth read as the bond gaining and losing value; the spread read as the default probability itself. Edge: part of a real spread pays for illiquidity and tax, so $\lambda$ from spreads exceeds historical default rates | N, N, C, M |

Transfer: lesson 10's bootstrap is how every rates desk builds its curve, and its matching conditions are the splines of 1.3 lesson 29; lesson 11's forward rate is the forward price of lesson 47 for money; lesson 12's hazard rate is survival analysis, the same mathematics as customer churn in ML.

Then the Level review and homework `set.rates.bonds`.

**Level RB3: Interest-rate risk and swaps** (`lvl.rates.risk`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 13 | `lsn.rates.duration` Duration: a balance point and a slope | Compute Macaulay and modified duration and DV01, and use them to predict price changes for small yield moves | A 5-year zero yields 4% (annual). How much does it lose if the yield rises by 1 basis point? (0.0395 per 100 face: price 82.19, modified duration 4.81) | `duration-lab` (fulcrum, tangent) | Trap: a higher coupon means a longer duration (it is shorter: more value arrives early). Edge: a zero's Macaulay duration is its maturity; a floating-rate note's is the time to its next reset | N, N, C, P |
| 14 | `lsn.rates.convexity` Convexity: when the tangent lies | Add the convexity term, $\frac{\Delta P}{P} \approx -D_{\text{mod}}\Delta y + \frac12C\Delta y^2$, and judge when it matters | A 30-year zero yields 4%; yields fall by 2 points. Duration predicts +57.7%. What really happens? (+79.1%; with convexity +74.9%) | `duration-lab` (convexity), `convexity-chord` | Trap: convexity is a risk to the holder (a long bond gains more than duration says when yields fall and loses less when they rise). Edge: callable bonds and mortgages have negative convexity | N, N, C, C |
| 15 | `lsn.rates.dv01-hedging` Hedging with DV01 and immunisation | Build a DV01-neutral hedge between two bonds, and immunise a liability by matching value and duration | You hold £10m of a 10-year gilt with DV01 0.080 per 100; you hedge with a 2-year gilt with DV01 0.019 per 100. How much 2-year do you sell? (£42.1m) | `duration-lab` (immunise, compare), `yield-curve-lab` (shapes) | Trap: hedging with equal notional. Edge: a DV01 hedge protects against parallel moves only; a steepening of the curve leaves it exposed | N, N, C, M |
| 16 | `lsn.rates.swaps` Interest rate swaps and the overnight benchmarks | Value a fixed-for-floating swap as a fixed-coupon bond minus a floating-rate bond, find the par swap rate from discount factors, and name the floating benchmarks (SONIA, SOFR) | Discount factors are 0.97, 0.94 and 0.91 for years 1 to 3. What fixed rate makes a 3-year annual swap worth zero today? (3.19%) | `yield-curve-lab` (swap), `cashflow-timeline` | Trap: the floating leg needs a forecast of every future rate (it is worth par at each reset, so its value is $1 - d_3$ per unit). Edge: LIBOR's replacement by overnight rates (SONIA from the end of 2021, SOFR after June 2023 [recalled, check]) means floating coupons are compounded in arrears | N, X, C, N |

Transfer: lesson 13's DV01 is the first line of every rates risk report; lesson 14's convexity is the same second-order term as an option's gamma (lesson 75), and 1.3 lesson 105 is its calculus; lesson 16's swap curve discounts every derivative cash flow in OP.

Then the Level review and homework `set.rates.risk`.

#### Course EQ: Shares and Portfolios (`crs.equity`, 2 levels, 12 lessons)

What a share is, how it pays its owner, how its returns behave when compounded, and how shares combine into portfolios whose risk is less than the sum of their parts. It closes with the CAPM, factors and what efficient markets claim, the vocabulary 2.3's alpha research is built on. Spine: Khan Academy, stocks and bonds unit; Zerodha Varsity module 1 (Introduction to Stock Markets); MIT 15.401 lectures on equities and portfolio theory. Currency: pounds in EQ1, USD from EQ2.

**Level EQ1: Shares and how they pay** (`lvl.equity.shares`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 17 | `lsn.equity.what-is-a-share` Ownership, market capitalisation and issuance | Compute market capitalisation and a holder's stake, and the effect of issuing new shares on the stake's percentage and its value | A firm has 10m shares at £20. It sells 2m new shares at £20 to raise £40m. Does your 1% stake lose value? (Its share falls to 0.833% but its value stays £2m) | `share-lab` (ownership, issue) | Trap: dilution always destroys value (only issuing below fair value does). Edge: shares with different voting rights; a rights issue at a discount to all holders leaves each holder's wealth unchanged | N, N, C, M |
| 18 | `lsn.equity.dividends-buybacks` Dividends, buybacks and the ex-date | Predict the price drop on the ex-dividend date, and show that a buyback at a fair price leaves the value of each remaining share unchanged | A share at £50 goes ex a £2 dividend tomorrow. Expected opening price, with no news? (£48) | `share-lab` (dividend, buyback) | Trap: a dividend is free money on top of the share's value (`dividend-is-free-money`). Edge: a buyback above fair value transfers wealth from those who stay to those who sell | N, C, N, M |
| 19 | `lsn.equity.splits-total-return` Splits, adjusted prices and total return | Build adjustment factors for splits and dividends, and compute total return against price return | A chart shows a share falling from £300 to £100 overnight with no news. A crash? (A 3-for-1 split: nothing changed) | `share-lab` (split, total-return), `drawdown-lab` (returns) | Trap: computing returns from raw closes (`raw-close-returns`); a split creating or destroying value (`split-changes-value`). Edge: a "buy below £5" rule run on back-adjusted prices trades at levels that never existed (RM lesson 5) | N, N, C, S |
| 20 | `lsn.equity.dividend-discount` What a share is worth: the dividend discount model | Value a share as the present value of its dividends, $P_0 = \frac{D_1}{r - g}$, and back out the growth or the return a price implies | Next year's dividend is £3, growing 4% a year for ever; investors require 8%. Value? (£75. At a price of £100 the implied growth is 5%) | `tvm-timeline` (perpetuity), `slider` | Trap: using this year's dividend $D_0$ in place of $D_1$; $g \ge r$. Edge: a fall in the required return from 8% to 7% raises the value by a third, which is why share prices react so strongly to interest rates | N, N, C, X |
| 21 | `lsn.equity.indices` Price-weighted and capitalisation-weighted indices | Compute both kinds of index from their constituents, adjust a divisor for a split, and say which constituents dominate each | Three shares are priced £10, £50 and £200, with 1,000m, 100m and 10m shares. Weights in each kind of index? (price-weighted 3.8%, 19.2%, 76.9%; cap-weighted 58.8%, 29.4%, 11.8%) | `share-lab` (index) | Trap: a price-weighted index weights companies by size (`index-weights-confused`). Edge: a split in a price-weighted index changes the divisor, and the split share's weight falls | N, N, C, S |
| 22 | `lsn.equity.long-short-margin` Long, short and margin | Compute long and short P&L, the price that triggers a margin call, and the effect of gearing on return and risk | You buy £10,000 of shares with £5,000 of your own; maintenance margin is 30%. How far can the price fall before a call? (28.6%) | `margin-lab` (long-short, margin, short, squeeze) | Trap: a short's loss is bounded like a long's (`short-loss-bounded`); the call comes at zero equity (`call-at-zero-equity`); gearing adds return but not risk (`gearing-adds-return-not-risk`). Edge: borrow fees, recall and a short squeeze can force a close at the worst time | N, N, C, M |

Transfer: lesson 19's adjustment factors are the first thing a backtest gets wrong (BT lesson 52); lesson 20 is a perpetuity (lesson 4) and the duration of equities; lesson 22's margin call is the forced selling that turns a fall into a crash.

Then the Level review and homework `set.equity.shares`.

**Level EQ2: Risk, return and the market** (`lvl.equity.portfolios`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 23 | `lsn.equity.volatility-drag` Arithmetic and geometric returns: volatility drag | Compute arithmetic and geometric mean returns and use $g \approx \mu - \frac12\sigma^2$ | A share rises 50% and then falls 50%. Where are you? (down 25%, although the average return is 0%) | `multiplier-chain` (returns), `gbm-lab` (1.7), `convexity-chord` (drag) | Trap: a zero arithmetic mean means you break even. Edge: a fund that resets to twice the daily move of an index decays in a volatile, flat market | N, N, C, X |
| 24 | `lsn.equity.diversification` Two assets: diversification and correlation | Compute a two-asset portfolio's mean and volatility, and find the minimum-variance weight (recapping 1.5 lesson 96 and 1.3 lesson 128 in one screen) | Two shares each have 20% volatility and correlation 0.2. Volatility of a 50/50 portfolio? (15.5%) | `portfolio-lab` (two-asset), `lagrange-explorer` (minimum-variance preset) | Trap: a portfolio's volatility is the weighted average of its parts' volatilities (true only at correlation 1). Edge: at correlation $-1$ some mix has zero risk | N, N, P, C |
| 25 | `lsn.equity.many-assets` Many assets: the systematic floor | Derive the variance of an equally weighted portfolio of $n$ assets with common $\sigma$ and $\rho$, $\sigma^2\left(\rho + \frac{1 - \rho}{n}\right)$, and its floor $\sigma\sqrt\rho$ | 100 shares, each with 30% volatility and pairwise correlation 0.3. Portfolio volatility? (16.6%, against a floor of 16.4% however many you add) | `portfolio-lab` (n-asset), `derivation-steps` | Trap: enough shares remove all risk. Edge: the same formula is the variance of an ensemble's average prediction, and why correlated models add little (2.1) | N, X, C, N |
| 26 | `lsn.equity.sharpe-cml` The Sharpe ratio and the best risky portfolio | Compute Sharpe ratios, show that mixing a risky portfolio with cash traces a straight line, and pick the risky portfolio with the steepest line | Fund A returns 10% with 15% volatility; fund B 7% with 8%; cash pays 3%. You may borrow and lend. Which fund do you hold? (B: Sharpe 0.50 against 0.47) | `portfolio-lab` (frontier, cml) | Trap: the fund with the highest return is best. Edge: when borrowing costs more than lending, the line bends and the best fund depends on how much risk you want | N, P, C, N |
| 27 | `lsn.equity.capm-beta` Beta, the CAPM and alpha | Estimate beta by regressing excess returns on the market's, split variance into systematic and idiosyncratic parts, and read alpha as the intercept | A share moves 1.5% for each 1% the market moves; the market premium is 5% and cash pays 3%. CAPM expected return? (10.5%) | `portfolio-lab` (beta), `regression-lab` (beta preset) | Trap: a high-volatility share must have a high beta (beta measures only the part that moves with the market). Edge: the security market line is empirically flatter than the CAPM says, the basis of "betting against beta" | N, N, C, M |
| 28 | `lsn.equity.factors-efficiency` Factors and efficient markets | State the Fama-French factors (market, size, value; profitability and investment; plus momentum), interpret a factor regression, and state the efficient markets hypothesis with the joint-hypothesis problem | A fund beat the market by 3% a year for five years. After regressing on market, size and value, its alpha is 0. Was it skilful? | `regression-lab`, `card-sort`, `factor-model-lab` | Trap: beating the index is alpha. Edge: the joint-hypothesis problem: any test of efficiency is also a test of the model of expected returns used | C, M, S, N |

Transfer: lesson 25 is 2.3's breadth and its fundamental law in disguise; lesson 26's Sharpe ratio is every strategy's headline number (RM2); lessons 27 and 28 are the regression every 2.3 results note runs to show its alpha is not beta (RM lesson 12).

Then the Level review and homework `set.equity.portfolios`.

#### Course MK: Exchanges and the Order Book (`crs.markets`, 3 levels, 17 lessons)

How trading actually happens: the continuous double auction, the limit order book, the spread, market against limit orders, priority, order types, auctions, venues and the post-trade chain; then why spreads exist (processing costs, inventory, adverse selection) and what trading costs. The course carries the first part of the 1.7 exit and ends with its rehearsal. Spine: Zerodha Varsity module 1 for the mechanics; Harris, Trading and Exchanges, for orders, order-driven markets, dealers, spreads and liquidity. Prices are in ticks of 0.01 unless stated.

**Level MK1: The limit order book** (`lvl.markets.order-book`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 29 | `lsn.markets.continuous-auction` Why exchanges exist: the continuous double auction | Describe a continuous double auction and predict when and at what price an arriving order trades | Bids rest at 99 and 98; offers at 101 and 102. A seller arrives willing to sell at 98. What happens? (It trades at 99, the resting bid's price, with whoever bid 99 first) | `order-book` (explore), `price-ladder`, `order-ticket` | Trap: the trade happens at the arriving order's limit price. Edge: an arriving order that crosses several levels trades at each resting price in turn | S, N, C, G |
| 30 | `lsn.markets.book-spread` The book: best bid, best ask, spread and mid | Read the best bid and ask, the spread in ticks and basis points, the mid and the depth at each level | Bids 100.00 × 300 and 99.99 × 500; asks 100.02 × 200 and 100.03 × 400. Spread? (2 ticks, 2.0 basis points of the mid 100.01) | `order-book` (place, spread), `price-ladder` | Trap: the quoted spread is the cost of any order (only of a small one). Edge: a one-sided book has no spread and no mid | N, N, C, P |
| 31 | `lsn.markets.market-orders-walk` Market orders cross the spread and walk the book | Compute the average fill price and the slippage of a market order against the visible book | Asks: 100.02 × 200, 100.03 × 400, 100.05 × 1,000. Market buy of 1,000 shares: average price? (100.036, 2.6 basis points above the mid) | `order-book` (walk, depth) | Trap: a market order fills at the best ask. Edge: hidden and iceberg orders can improve the fill; quotes can vanish before the order arrives | N, N, C, P |
| 32 | `lsn.markets.priority` Price-time priority and queue position | Apply price-time priority to decide who fills, and explain why raising an order's size or changing its price loses its place | Three bids at 99: A for 300 (first), B for 200, C for 500. A market sell of 400 arrives. Who fills? (A 300, B 100) | `order-book` (queue), `order-ticket` | Trap: larger orders fill first, or fills are shared pro rata. Edge: some futures markets do match pro rata; reducing an order's size keeps its place on most venues [check per venue] | O, N, C, M |
| 33 | `lsn.markets.market-vs-limit` Market against limit orders: price against certainty | Compare the expected cost of a market order (the half-spread now) with a limit order (no spread, but the risk of no fill and of fills that come when the price moves against you), and choose for a stated goal | You must buy 100 shares today. Pay 100.02 now, or bid 100.00 and fill 60% of the time, chasing at 100.10 on average when you do not? (Limit costs 100.04 on average: the market order is cheaper here) | `order-book` (race), `sim-runner` | Trap: limit orders are always cheaper because they save the spread. Edge: adverse selection: a resting bid fills most often just before the price falls | N, C, M, O |
| 34 | `lsn.markets.order-types` Order types and instructions | Predict the book and the fills for stop, stop-limit, immediate-or-cancel, fill-or-kill, post-only and iceberg orders | A stop sell at 95 rests while the price gaps from 96 to 93 at the open. Fill price? (about 93 or lower: a triggered stop becomes a market order) | `order-book` (types), `order-ticket` | Trap: a stop guarantees its price. Edge: a stop-limit can fail to fill at all in a gap; a post-only order that would cross is cancelled or repriced | S, C, N, M |

Transfer: lesson 31's walk is the slippage model of BT3; lesson 32's queue position is why speed has value (lesson 38) and why a backtest that fills every touch is optimistic (BT lesson 60); lesson 33 is the first execution decision a trading system makes.

Then the Level review and homework `set.markets.order-book`.

**Level MK2: Auctions, venues and the trade cycle** (`lvl.markets.venues`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 35 | `lsn.markets.auctions` Opening and closing auctions | Find the uncrossing price that maximises the volume traded in a call auction, and apply the imbalance tie-break | Before the open: buy 800 at 10.05, 1,200 at 10.03, 1,000 at 10.02, 1,500 at 10.00; sell 700 at 9.98, 1,100 at 10.01, 900 at 10.03, 1,300 at 10.05. One price for all trades: which maximises volume? (10.03, 2,000 shares) | `auction-lab`, `plot-2d` (supply and demand steps) | Trap: the midpoint of the best bid and best offer (10.015). Edge: ties on volume are broken by the smallest imbalance, then by a reference price [rule set labelled "typical", widget catalogue open question 7] | N, N, C, P |
| 36 | `lsn.markets.fees-tick` Maker-taker fees, rebates and tick size | Compute the all-in cost of taking and of making liquidity with fees and rebates, and predict how a tick-size change moves spreads and queues | The spread is 1 cent; takers pay 0.30 cents a share and makers receive 0.20. Cost of crossing against the gain from being filled, both relative to the mid? (taking costs 0.80 cents; making earns 0.70 cents) | `order-book` (fees, tick) | Trap: rebates make providing liquidity free money (adverse selection costs more). Edge: when the tick is large relative to the price, the spread is pinned at one tick and queues grow long | N, N, C, C |
| 37 | `lsn.markets.clearing-settlement` After the trade: clearing, the central counterparty and settlement | Order the post-trade steps, explain novation and netting by a central counterparty, and state settlement cycles | You buy shares on Monday. When do you own them, and what happens if the seller fails on Tuesday? (US shares settle T+1 since 28 May 2024; the UK plans T+1 from 11 October 2027 [check]) | `card-sort`, `node-link` | Trap: the exchange or the seller is your counterparty after clearing (the central counterparty is). Edge: netting turns many gross obligations into a few net ones, which is why a CCP failure would be systemic | O, C, M, S |
| 38 | `lsn.markets.market-structure` Fragmentation, dark pools, best execution and speed | Match venue types (lit exchanges, multilateral trading facilities, dark pools, internalisers, request for quote) to the traders they serve, explain best execution, and describe latency races on stale quotes | A pension fund wants to sell 2% of a day's volume without moving the price; a quote on venue A updates 50 microseconds after venue B. Where does each trade, and who profits from the delay? | `card-sort`, `order-book` (tick, resilience), `free-response` | Trap: dark pools are illegal or hidden markets (they are regulated venues where large orders avoid signalling, at the cost of uncertain fills). Edge: frequent batch auctions are a proposed cure for latency races | S, C, M, O |

Transfer: lesson 35 is the closing auction where index funds trade and where 2.3's backtests should assume their fills; lesson 36's fees enter every cost model in BT3; lesson 38's latency race is what 2.3's feed handler measures.

Then the Level review and homework `set.markets.venues`.

**Level MK3: Why spreads exist, and what trading costs** (`lvl.markets.microstructure`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 39 | `lsn.markets.who-trades` Who trades and why; what liquidity is | Classify traders in Harris's terms (utilitarian, profit-motivated, informed, uninformed, dealers) and measure liquidity by width, depth, immediacy and resilience | A pension fund rebalancing, a hedge fund with news, a market maker, an index fund at the close and a day trader all arrive. Who is happy to pay the spread, and why? | `card-sort`, `order-book` (resilience) | Trap: every trader wants the same thing from a market. Edge: a market can be deep but slow to recover (low resilience) after a large order | S, M, C, O |
| 40 | `lsn.markets.tape-spreads` The tape: trade signs, effective and realised spreads | Sign trades by the quote rule and the tick rule, and compute the effective spread $2\lvert p - m\rvert$, the realised spread $2d(p - m_{t+\Delta})$ and price impact | A buy prints at 100.04 when the mid is 100.01; five minutes later the mid is 100.05. Effective spread, realised spread, impact? (0.06; $-0.02$; 0.08) | `tape-lab` (classify, spreads) | Trap: a realised spread cannot be negative (it is when the price keeps moving the trader's way: the liquidity provider lost). Edge: effective equals realised plus impact, so a dealer's take is what is left after impact | N, N, S, C |
| 41 | `lsn.markets.roll` Bid-ask bounce and Roll's estimator | Derive Roll's estimate $s = 2\sqrt{-\mathrm{Cov}(\Delta p_t, \Delta p_{t-1})}$ from trades bouncing between bid and ask | With no news, trades bounce between 99.98 and 100.02; the autocovariance of successive price changes is $-0.0004$. Spread? (0.04) | `tape-lab` (roll), `derivation-steps` | Trap: negative autocorrelation in trade prices is a mean-reversion opportunity (it is the bounce, and costs a spread to trade). Edge: when the measured autocovariance is positive the estimator is undefined | X, N, C, N |
| 42 | `lsn.markets.inventory` Dealers and inventory: why quotes skew | Explain the inventory component of the spread and set skewed quotes that shed an unwanted position | You are a dealer long 5,000 shares, quoting 99.98 bid and 100.02 offer, and you want to sell. Which quote moves, and which way? (both down: a lower offer to sell, a lower bid to buy less) | `dealer-sim` (inventory) | Trap: widening both sides symmetrically sheds inventory. Edge: Avellaneda and Stoikov's model (3.1) sets the skew from inventory, risk aversion and volatility | C, N, P, M |
| 43 | `lsn.markets.adverse-selection` Adverse selection: the Glosten-Milgrom spread | Compute zero-profit bid and ask as conditional expectations of the value given a sell and given a buy, when some traders are informed | The value is 100 or 110, equally likely; 30% of traders know which and trade the right way; the rest buy or sell at random. Zero-profit quotes? (bid 103.5, ask 106.5) | `adverse-selection` (glosten-milgrom, sequence), `tree-diagram` | Trap: the spread pays only for processing costs. Edge: with the prior at one half the spread is the informed share times the value range ($0.3 \times 10 = 3$); quotes move after each trade as beliefs update | N, N, C, P |
| 44 | `lsn.markets.impact-execution` Market impact and execution benchmarks | Estimate impact by the square-root law, $Y\sigma\sqrt{Q/V}$, and score an execution against arrival price, VWAP and implementation shortfall | You sell 1% of a day's volume, then 9%. How much more impact per share does the second order pay? (three times as much: $\sqrt 9 = 3$) | `execution-lab` (impact, schedule, shortfall) | Trap: impact is proportional to size. Edge: beating VWAP is easy for an order that is itself most of the volume; implementation shortfall counts the cost of not trading | N, N, C, M |
| 45 | `lsn.markets.exit-explain` Exit rehearsal: explain the book, the spread, and market against limit orders | Explain, aloud or in writing, a limit order book, the bid-ask spread and market against limit orders, scoring 7 of 10 or more on the exit rubric | An interviewer asks: "Walk me through what happens, in order, when you send a market order to buy 1,000 shares." | `free-response` (voice, rubric), `voice-capture`, `viva`, `order-book` | Trap: definitions without mechanism (who the counterparty is, what sets each fill's price, why the spread exists). Edge: the answer changes in an auction or a one-sided book; a strong answer says so | O, C, M, S |

Transfer: lesson 40's spreads are what BT3 charges and what MS measures from the feed; lesson 43 is 3.3's market-making game played with a formula, and Bayes' rule (1.5 course H) at work; lesson 44's square-root law sets every strategy's capacity (AR lesson 121).

Then the Level review and homework `set.markets.microstructure`.

#### Course DV: Forwards, Futures and Options (`crs.derivatives`, 3 levels, 18 lessons)

Contracts whose value depends on a price later. Forwards and futures are priced by cash and carry, valued as they age, settled daily and used to hedge; options are rights, whose payoffs combine into strategies and decompose into calls, and whose prices are boxed in by no-arbitrage bounds and tied together by put-call parity. The course carries the derivation part of the 1.7 exit. Spine: Zerodha Varsity modules 4 (Futures Trading), 5 (Options Theory for Professional Trading) and 6 (Option Strategies); Hull chapters 1 to 3, 5 and 10 to 12. Rates are continuous from here on (decision 6).

**Level DV1: Forwards and futures** (`lvl.derivatives.forwards-futures`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 46 | `lsn.derivatives.forward-payoff` A forward: the obligation to trade later | Write the long and short forward payoffs $S_T - K$ and $K - S_T$, and explain why entering a forward costs nothing | You agree today to buy an ounce of gold in a year at USD 2,000. Gold ends at 2,300, or at 1,800. Your P&L? ($+300$; $-200$) | `payoff-builder` (forward), `position-editor` | Trap: you pay the forward price when you enter. Edge: the long and short payoffs sum to zero: a forward moves risk, it does not create value | N, P, C, C |
| 47 | `lsn.derivatives.cash-and-carry` Pricing by no arbitrage: cash and carry | Derive $F_0 = S_0e^{rT}$ by replication, and build the arbitrage when a quote differs, in both directions | A share at 100 pays no dividends; $r = 5\%$ continuous; the 1-year forward is quoted at 108. Free money? (Fair is 105.13: sell the forward, borrow 100, buy the share; lock in 2.87 at expiry) | `carry-arb` (build, arbitrage), `cashflow-timeline` | Trap: the forward price is the market's forecast of the future price (`futures-forecast`). Edge: if you cannot short the asset, the reverse trade fails and the forward can sit below fair value (commodities, hard-to-borrow shares) | N, O, C, M |
| 48 | `lsn.derivatives.carry-income` Income, storage, convenience yield and currency forwards | Extend cash and carry to $F_0 = (S_0 - I)e^{rT}$, $S_0e^{(r - \delta)T}$, storage costs and convenience yield, and derive covered interest parity | Sterling is at 1.25 dollars; sterling rates are 5% and dollar rates 4%, both continuous. One-year forward rate? (1.2376 dollars per pound) | `carry-arb` (income, fx), `derivation-steps` | Trap: the higher-rate currency trades at a forward premium (it trades at a discount). Edge: a convenience yield explains commodity forwards below the carry price, where the reverse trade cannot be done | X, N, C, N |
| 49 | `lsn.derivatives.value-existing-forward` Valuing a forward you already hold | Value an existing forward as $(F_t - K)e^{-r(T - t)}$ by replacing it with an offsetting new contract | You are long a forward struck at 100 with 6 months left; today's 6-month forward price is 104 and $r = 5\%$. Value? (3.90) | `carry-arb` (value), `cashflow-timeline` | Trap: the value is $F_t - K$, undiscounted. Edge: a forward's value moves with rates even when the spot price does not | N, X, C, N |
| 50 | `lsn.derivatives.futures-mtm` Futures: daily settlement and margin | Compute daily variation margin, track a margin account, and decide when a call is made and how much it is | Long 1 gold contract of 100 ounces at 2,000; initial margin USD 10,000, maintenance 7,500. Settlements: 1,990, 1,970, 1,985. Margin call?
