(Yes, after day 2: the balance is 7,000; deposit 3,000 to restore 10,000) | `futures-mtm` (daily, margin, gearing) | Trap: the call comes when the balance hits zero, or tops up only to maintenance. Edge: futures and forwards prices differ slightly when interest rates are random and correlated with the asset | N, N, C, M |
| 51 | `lsn.derivatives.basis` Basis, convergence, contango and backwardation | Explain convergence of futures to spot at expiry, and read contango and backwardation from carry and convenience yield | Oil futures: 1 month 80, 6 months 76. Is the market forecasting a fall in oil? | `futures-mtm` (basis, term) | Trap: the shape of the futures curve is a forecast. Edge: a long position rolled through a contango curve loses the roll yield even if spot is unchanged | C, C, N, M |
| 52 | `lsn.derivatives.hedging-futures` Hedging with futures: the minimum-variance ratio and beta hedges | Compute $h^* = \rho\frac{\sigma_S}{\sigma_F}$, the number of contracts, and the contracts that change a portfolio's beta | An airline hedges jet fuel with heating-oil futures: fuel volatility 3%, futures 4%, correlation 0.9. Hedge ratio? (0.675). A USD 10m portfolio with beta 1.2 and index futures worth 250,000 each: contracts to remove beta? (48) | `hedge-lab` (basic, min-var, beta) | Trap: a hedge ratio of 1 is always best. Edge: basis risk remains after any hedge; the minimum-variance ratio is the regression slope (1.6) | N, N, P, C |

Transfer: lesson 47 is the law of one price that every later price rests on; lesson 48's covered interest parity is checked to the decimal by FX desks; lesson 52's regression hedge is the beta neutralisation of 2.3 (AR lesson 101).

Then the Level review and homework `set.derivatives.forwards-futures`.

**Level DV2: Option payoffs and strategies** (`lvl.derivatives.payoffs`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 53 | `lsn.derivatives.calls-puts` Calls and puts: rights, not obligations | Draw long and short call and put payoffs, separate payoff from profit, and find break-even prices | A call struck at 100 costs 5. The share ends at 103. Exercise? Profit? (Exercise: payoff 3, profit $-2$; break-even 105) | `payoff-builder` (single, profit, sketch-first), `sketch-pad` (piecewise) | Trap: do not exercise below the break-even (exercising still recovers 3 of the 5 paid). Edge: the short put's loss is bounded by the strike; the short call's is not | P, N, C, N |
| 54 | `lsn.derivatives.moneyness-chain` Moneyness, intrinsic and time value, and reading a chain | Split option prices into intrinsic and time value, classify moneyness, and read the mid, spread and liquidity from a chain | The share is at 102. The 100 call trades at 4.50 and the 105 call at 1.80. Intrinsic and time value of each? (2 and 2.50; 0 and 1.80) | `option-chain` (read), `payoff-builder` (value-today) | Trap: an option is never worth less than its intrinsic value (a deep in-the-money European put can be, when rates are positive). Edge: open interest and volume, not price, tell you which strikes trade | N, S, C, N |
| 55 | `lsn.derivatives.spreads-volatility` Spreads, straddles and butterflies | Build vertical spreads, straddles, strangles and butterflies, and read each one's maximum gain, maximum loss and the view it expresses | You expect a big move after earnings but not its direction. Build a position; with a 100 straddle costing 8, where does it break even? (92 and 108) | `payoff-builder` (legs) | Trap: a straddle bets on direction (it bets on the size of the move). Edge: a butterfly is a bet that the price lands near one strike, and its cost is small exactly because that is unlikely | P, N, S, C |
| 56 | `lsn.derivatives.covered-protective` Shares plus options: covered calls, protective puts and collars | Build payoffs of shares combined with options and recognise their equivalents (a covered call is a short put plus a bond) | You own a share at 100 and sell a 110 call for 3. The share ends at 130. Your total? (113: the upside above 110 was sold) | `payoff-builder`, `position-editor` | Trap: a covered call is free income. Edge: a collar (long put, short call) can cost nothing and still change the risk completely | P, N, C, M |
| 57 | `lsn.derivatives.static-replication` Any kinked payoff is a sum of calls | Decompose a piecewise-linear payoff into a bond, a forward and calls at each kink, with quantities equal to the slope changes | Target: 0 below 90, rising to 10 at 100, flat above. Fewest legs? (long the 90 call, short the 100 call) | `payoff-builder` (match), `matrix` | Trap: matching the payoff's values at a few prices instead of its slopes. Edge: a smooth payoff needs calls at every strike, which is Breeden and Litzenberger's density (1.3 lesson 74, OA4) | P, A, N, C |

Transfer: lesson 54's chain is the raw material of project 3; lesson 57's decomposition prices any payoff from calls, and turns a strip of options into the variance swap behind the VIX (OA lesson 152).

Then the Level review and homework `set.derivatives.payoffs`.

**Level DV3: No-arbitrage bounds and put-call parity** (`lvl.derivatives.parity`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 58 | `lsn.derivatives.bounds` No-arbitrage bounds on option prices | Derive $\max(S_0 - Ke^{-rT}, 0) \le C \le S_0$ and $\max(Ke^{-rT} - S_0, 0) \le P \le Ke^{-rT}$, and build the arbitrage when a quote breaks one | A share at 100; a European call struck at 90, one year, $r = 5\%$, quoted at 12. Arbitrage? (The bound is 14.39: buy the call, short the share, invest 88; you keep at least 2.51 at expiry) | `option-bounds` (spot), `position-editor` | Trap: the lower bound is $S_0 - K$, undiscounted. Edge: dividends lower the call's bound to $S_0 - PV(D) - Ke^{-rT}$ | N, O, C, I |
| 59 | `lsn.derivatives.american-call` Never exercise an American call early on a share without dividends | Show an American call on a non-dividend share is worth at least $S_0 - Ke^{-rT} > S_0 - K$, so it is never exercised early, and say how dividends change that | A share at 150, a call struck at 100, a year to run, $r = 5\%$. Exercise now for 50? (No: the call is worth at least 54.88; sell it, or short the share) | `option-bounds` (american) | Trap: deep in-the-money calls should be exercised (`american-call-early`). Edge: just before a large dividend, early exercise can pay; American puts can be exercised early at any time | C, N, C, M |
| 60 | `lsn.derivatives.parity-overlay` Put-call parity: two portfolios, one payoff | Show by payoff tables that a call plus a bond paying $K$ and a put plus a share have identical payoffs, so must have equal prices | Slide the final price across the chart: portfolio A is a call plus a bond paying $K$, portfolio B a put plus a share. What do you notice? (The two payoff lines coincide at every price) | `parity-lab` (overlay), `payoff-builder` | Trap: parity needs a model or a volatility (it needs neither). Edge: parity fails for American options, which satisfy only an inequality (homework DV3) | C, N, P, S |
| 61 | `lsn.derivatives.parity-arbitrage` Deriving parity, and trading violations | Derive $C - P = S_0 - Ke^{-rT}$ (and with dividends), and build conversion and reversal trades against violations | Share 100, strike 100, one year, $r = 5\%$: call 10.45, put 4.50. Arbitrage? ($C - P = 5.95$ against $S_0 - Ke^{-rT} = 4.88$: sell the call, buy the put and the share, borrow 95.12, and take 1.07 today) | `parity-lab` (derive, arbitrage), `derivation-steps` | Trap: trading the violation in the wrong direction. Edge: with bid and ask quotes the parity line becomes a band, and most apparent violations sit inside it | X, O, N, C |
| 62 | `lsn.derivatives.synthetics-box` Synthetics, the box spread and the implied forward | Build a synthetic share and a synthetic put, price a box spread as a loan, and read the implied forward from $C - P$ | A 90/110 box pays exactly 20 at expiry. Its price with $r = 5\%$ and one year? (19.02) | `parity-lab` (synthetic, box, forward) | Trap: a box spread is riskless whatever the exercise style (with American options the short legs can be exercised early). Edge: the box's price gives the market's interest rate, the cleanest one there is | N, P, C, N |
| 63 | `lsn.derivatives.strike-convexity` Strike convexity, and the exit derivation | Prove that call prices fall and are convex in the strike (a butterfly costs at least zero), and derive put-call parity cold for the exit | Calls struck at 90, 100 and 110 trade at 14, 8 and 1. Free money? (The butterfly costs $14 - 16 + 1 = -1$: you are paid to take a position that cannot lose) | `option-bounds` (strikes), `derivation-steps` (exit rehearsal, no hints) | Trap: call prices are linear in the strike. Edge: convexity in the strike is a non-negative risk-neutral density (OA4) | N, O, X, C |

Transfer: lesson 61 is the exit's derivation and the check every options data cleaner runs (OA lesson 140); lesson 62's implied forward is how project 3 removes dividends and rates from a chain; lesson 63's convexity test is OA4's butterfly arbitrage check.

Then the Level review and homework `set.derivatives.parity`.

#### Course OP: Pricing Options (`crs.pricing`, 3 levels, 17 lessons)

Replication in a one-step tree, then risk-neutral probabilities, backward induction and American exercise, then the binomial pricer in code (the exit drill); Black-Scholes as the tree's limit, read as two digital claims; then the Greeks, a day's P&L explained by them, delta hedging, implied volatility and the smile. Spine: Hull chapters 13 (Binomial Trees), 15 (The Black-Scholes-Merton Model), 19 (The Greek Letters) and 20 (Volatility Smiles); Shreve I chapter 1 as a stretch. OP2 and OP3 open after 1.5 levels V5 and V7 and 1.3 lessons 103 and 105 (decision 4).

**Level OP1: Binomial trees** (`lvl.pricing.binomial`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 64 | `lsn.pricing.one-step-replication` One step: copy the option with shares and a bond | Solve the two equations for the share holding $\Delta$ and the bond holding that copy an option in both states of a one-step tree | A share at 100 moves to 120 or 90; a call struck at 100; zero interest for the step. Price? ($\Delta = \frac23$, borrow 60: price 6.67) | `binomial-tree` (replicate), `position-editor`, `matrix` | Trap: the price is the expected payoff under the real chances (`real-probability-prices`). Edge: the replicating portfolio exists for any two-state payoff, which is the whole idea | N, N, A, C |
| 65 | `lsn.pricing.risk-neutral` Risk-neutral probability: the real probability does not matter | Derive $q = \frac{e^{r\Delta t} - d}{u - d}$, price as a discounted $q$-expectation, and prove that no arbitrage needs $d < e^{r\Delta t} < u$ | In the same tree, one trader thinks the up move has probability 0.9 and another 0.1. Do they disagree on the call's price? (No: $q = \frac13$ for both, price 6.67) | `binomial-tree` (q derive, arbitrage) | Trap: $q$ is anyone's belief about the future. Edge: if $u \le e^{r\Delta t}$ the share is dominated by the bond and an arbitrage appears | N, X, C, M |
| 66 | `lsn.pricing.backward-induction` Two steps and backward induction | Price a European option on a two-step tree by backward induction and by summing over paths, and check the two agree | Share 100, $u = 1.1$, $d = 0.9$, zero interest, call struck at 100, two steps. Price? ($q = \frac12$; only the top leaf pays 21; price 5.25) | `binomial-tree` (price, state-prices) | Trap: discounting the expected payoff by one step instead of two (`discount-once-at-end`). Edge: in a recombining tree, $N$ steps have $N + 1$ end nodes but $2^N$ paths | N, N, C, N |
| 67 | `lsn.pricing.delta-hedge-tree` Delta through the tree: dynamic replication | Compute $\Delta$ at each node and show the self-financing rebalancing reproduces the payoff on every path | In the two-step tree, how many shares does the hedger hold now, and after an up move? (0.525; then 0.9545) | `binomial-tree` (delta), `value-table` | Trap: delta is fixed at the start (`delta-is-constant`). Edge: in the down node the call is worthless on both branches, so delta is 0 there | N, N, C, O |
| 68 | `lsn.pricing.crr-calibration` Calibrating the tree: Cox-Ross-Rubinstein, dividends and convergence | Set $u = e^{\sigma\sqrt{\Delta t}}$ and $d = \frac1u$, include a dividend yield through $e^{(r - \delta)\Delta t}$, and watch prices converge with an odd-even wobble as $N$ grows | Volatility 20%, one year, four steps: what is $u$? (1.1052) | `binomial-tree` (crr, dividend, converge) | Trap: $q$ computed from $\sigma$ directly (`q-from-sigma-directly`); more steps always means closer to the formula (the error alternates). Edge: with too few steps and high rates, $q$ can leave $[0, 1]$ | N, N, C, P |
| 69 | `lsn.pricing.american-put` American puts and early exercise | Price an American put by comparing exercise and continuation values at every node, and mark the nodes where exercise is optimal | Share 40, put struck at 42, $u = 1.25$, $d = 0.8$, growth $e^{0.04}$ per step, two steps. American and European prices? (4.93 and 4.19; exercise at the down node, where 10 beats 8.35) | `binomial-tree` (american), `region-select` | Trap: exercise only at expiry, as for calls (`american-call-early` in reverse). Edge: the exercise boundary is a share price below which you exercise; it rises towards the strike as expiry nears | N, M, C, N |
| 70 | `lsn.pricing.binomial-code` The binomial pricer in one array (exit preparation) | Write a vectorised European pricer that overwrites one array by backward induction and passes hidden tests against Black-Scholes and exact trees | A tree with 1,000 steps has 501,501 nodes. How many numbers must your code hold at once? (1,001) | `code-cell` (`zq_fin` prelude), `binomial-tree` (vector) | Trap: storing the whole tree, or discounting once at the end with the wrong number of steps. Edge: put-call parity holds exactly in any tree, which is a free test | K, T, N, C |

Transfer: lesson 64 is the one-period model of Shreve I and of 0.2 lesson 54, and the reason a price is not a forecast; lesson 69's exercise decision is optimal stopping (1.5 lesson 151); lesson 70 is the code 1.9 rewrites in C++.

Then the Level review and homework `set.pricing.binomial`.

**Level OP2: Black-Scholes** (`lvl.pricing.black-scholes`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 71 | `lsn.pricing.lognormal` Prices as lognormal: the median below the mean | State the lognormal model of $S_T$ and compute its mean $S_0e^{\mu T}$, its median $S_0e^{(\mu - \frac12\sigma^2)T}$ and $P(S_T > K)$ (recapping 1.5 lesson 86) | Drift 5% and volatility 40% a year. Does the median path end above where it started? (No: the median is 0.970 of the start) | `gbm-lab` (1.7), `dist-explorer` (lognormal) | Trap: the expected price is the typical price. Edge: higher volatility lowers the median and leaves the mean unchanged | N, N, C, X |
| 72 | `lsn.pricing.tree-to-formula` From the tree to the Black-Scholes formula | Obtain $C = S_0N(d_1) - Ke^{-rT}N(d_2)$ as the limit of the CRR price, and check the formula against a tree with many steps | Share and strike 100, $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$, one year. Trees with 500 and 501 steps give 10.4466 and 10.4541. The formula? (10.4506) | `binomial-tree` (converge), `bs-explorer` | Trap: $d_1$ and $d_2$ swapped, or $\sigma$ entered as 20. Edge: as $\sigma\sqrt T \to 0$ the price tends to the discounted intrinsic value of the forward | N, X, C, N |
| 73 | `lsn.pricing.reading-bs` Reading the formula: two digital claims | Decompose the call into an asset-or-nothing claim minus a cash-or-nothing claim, and read $N(d_2)$ as the risk-neutral probability of exercise and $N(d_1)$ as delta | For that at-the-money call, $d_1 = 0.35$ and $d_2 = 0.15$. Risk-neutral chance it finishes in the money? (0.560; the two pieces are 63.68 and 53.23) | `bs-explorer` (decompose, density), `dist-explorer` (lognormal) | Trap: $N(d_2)$ is the real-world probability of exercise. Edge: a digital call's price is $e^{-rT}N(d_2)$, the slope of the call price in the strike | N, C, M, N |
| 74 | `lsn.pricing.inputs-atm` How the inputs move the price, and the at-the-money shortcut | Predict the signs of the price's sensitivities to $S$, $K$, $\sigma$, $T$, $r$ and $\delta$ for calls and puts, and price at-the-money options with $C \approx 0.4\sigma\sqrt TS$ | An at-the-money 3-month call on a USD 50 share with 30% volatility: estimate it in your head. (0.4 × 0.3 × 0.5 × 50 = 3.00; the formula with zero rates gives 2.99) | `bs-explorer` (inputs, atm, put), `speed-drill` (options deck) | Trap: higher volatility makes a put cheaper because the share might rise (both rise with volatility). Edge: a European call on a dividend share can lose value as time to expiry grows | S, N, C, N |

Transfer: lesson 72 is the bridge 3.2 crosses with stochastic calculus; lesson 73's digital is the binary contract of 1.5 lesson 17; lesson 74's shortcut is a 3.3 mental-arithmetic deck.

Then the Level review and homework `set.pricing.black-scholes`.

**Level OP3: Greeks, hedging and implied volatility** (`lvl.pricing.greeks`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 75 | `lsn.pricing.delta-gamma` Delta and gamma: slope and curvature | Compute delta $N(d_1)$ and gamma, locate gamma's peak near the money and close to expiry, and size a delta hedge | You sold 100 call contracts of 100 shares each, delta 0.6. Shares to buy to hedge? (6,000) | `greeks-lab` (delta, gamma) | Trap: delta stays put as the price moves (`delta-is-constant`). Edge: an at-the-money option's gamma grows without limit as expiry approaches | N, P, C, N |
| 76 | `lsn.pricing.theta-vega-rho` Theta, vega and rho: time decay and volatility | Compute and interpret theta, vega and rho, sketch the price curve at several expiries, and explain where time value decays fastest | A one-year at-the-money call (share 100, volatility 20%, $r = 5\%$) has vega 37.5 per unit of volatility. Volatility rises 2 points: change in price? (+0.75) | `greeks-lab` (theta, vega, rho), `bs-explorer` (slices), `sketch-pad` | Trap: vega quoted per 1.00 read as per point; time value decays at a constant rate. Edge: deep in-the-money European puts can have positive theta | N, N, C, S |
| 77 | `lsn.pricing.position-greeks` Greeks of a position, and a day's P&L explained | Add Greeks across legs, make a straddle delta-neutral, and explain a day's P&L as delta, gamma, theta and vega terms plus a residual | A book has delta 0, gamma 50 per USD 1, theta $-40$ a day. The share moves USD 2. Day's P&L? ($\frac12 \times 50 \times 4 - 40 = +60$) | `greeks-lab` (portfolio), `pnl-attribution` (one-day), `position-editor` | Trap: delta-neutral means riskless. Edge: a large residual means a missing term (a move in the smile, vanna, volga) or a jump | N, N, C, M |
| 78 | `lsn.pricing.delta-hedging` Delta hedging one path: gamma pays for theta | Simulate daily delta hedging of a sold option and show the hedged P&L depends on realised against implied volatility | You sell a call at 20% implied volatility and hedge daily; the share's realised volatility turns out 30%. Profit or loss? (Loss: the gamma you are short costs more than the theta you collect) | `hedging-sim` (one-path), `pnl-attribution` (path) | Trap: a hedged option has no risk. Edge: hedging less often adds noise, not bias, to the hedged P&L | C, N, P, C |
| 79 | `lsn.pricing.implied-vol` Implied volatility | Solve for implied volatility by bisection or Newton's method, and explain why traders quote volatility, not price | Share and strike 100, one year, zero rates: a call trades at 8. Implied volatility? (20.1%) | `iv-solver` (drag, bounds, algorithm) | Trap: implied volatility is an accurate forecast of future volatility (it carries a risk premium). Edge: a price below intrinsic value has no implied volatility at all | N, O, C, N |
| 80 | `lsn.pricing.smile` The smile: where Black-Scholes fails | Compute implied volatilities across strikes, describe the equity skew, and name the assumptions it contradicts | Six-month options on a share at 100 ($r = 3\%$): the 80 put at 1.231, 90 put 2.451, 100 call 6.649, 110 call 2.122, 120 call 0.487. Solve each for volatility. Equal? (No: 30%, 25%, 21%, 18%, 17%) | `iv-solver` (smile), `option-chain` (smile) | Trap: unequal implied volatilities are an arbitrage, or prove the formula useless (it becomes a quoting language). Edge: the skew steepened after the 1987 crash and has stayed since | C, M, N, S |

Transfer: lesson 77's P&L explain is a trader's daily report and the method of OA lesson 170; lesson 78's realised-against-implied P&L is the variance risk premium (OA7); lesson 80 is where project 3 begins.

Then the Level review and homework `set.pricing.greeks`.

**Coverage of the curriculum's 1.7 lines.** Shares: 17 to 22. Bonds: 7 to 16. Interest and compounding: 1 to 6. Forwards and futures: 46 to 52. Option properties: 53 to 63. Binomial trees: 64 to 70. Black-Scholes: 71 to 74. Greeks: 75 to 78. Market microstructure (Harris): 29 to 45. The exit: the limit order book, spread and market against limit orders in 29 to 34, 40 and 45; put-call parity in 60, 61 and 63; the binomial pricer in Python in 70 and the exit drill. Added for a top programme: real rates, loans and NPV (3, 5, 6), forward rates, credit spreads, duration, convexity and swaps (11 to 16), dividend discounting, indices and margin (20 to 22), portfolio theory, CAPM, factors and efficient markets (23 to 28), auctions, fees, clearing and market structure (35 to 38), Roll, Glosten-Milgrom, inventory and impact (41 to 44), basis and hedging (51, 52), static replication (57), implied volatility and the smile (79, 80). Not covered, by decision (widget catalogue 1.4): fundamental analysis of financial statements (beyond the accounting lags of AR7), value at risk (2.2 lessons 30 and 31), credit derivatives.

### 2.2 Item 2.3 Projects: five courses, 41 levels, 229 lessons

Every course runs on synthetic data from `market-gen` with known truths (decision 16), so an item can say "the true information coefficient was 0.02" and grade the learner's estimate against its sampling error. The learner's real projects run on real data on their own machine; each project level connects the two through `setup-checklist` (project mode), downloadable starter suites, pasted outputs and `results-note`.

#### Course RM: Research Method (`crs.method`, 8 levels, 47 lessons)

The science of not fooling yourself, applied to strategies. The course opens with what a sceptical reader checks, then hypotheses, data and its traps; how to measure performance; how uncertain one strategy's numbers are; how selection among many trials inflates them; how to validate through time; how to make research reproducible; how to write and defend it; and the empirical-finance tools (sorts, Fama-MacBeth, clustered errors, event studies, spanning) every alpha project and 3.1 replication uses. Spine: López de Prado, Advances in Financial Machine Learning, chapters 7 and 11 to 14; Arnott, Harvey and Markowitz (2019) as the checklist; the method papers of section 5.2. It recaps 1.6 lessons 19 and 38 to 42 and 2.2 lesson 21 in one screen each (decision 19).

**Level RM1: Questions and data** (`lvl.method.questions-data`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `lsn.method.what-readers-look-for` What a sceptical reader looks for | Rank research summaries by credibility against a checklist: hypothesis stated first, costs included, a held-out period, trials counted, failures reported | Five one-paragraph summaries, from "Sharpe 3.1, no costs, full sample" to "Sharpe 0.7 after costs, 0.5 held out, 40 variants tried". Rank them as a hiring manager would | `card-sort`, `results-note` (read-only) | Trap: the highest Sharpe is the most impressive. Edge: a modest number with its uncertainty and its failures is the strongest signal a self-taught candidate can send | O, M, C, S |
| 2 | `lsn.method.hypothesis-first` Hypothesis first: mechanism and pre-registration | Write a falsifiable hypothesis with an economic mechanism (a risk premium, a behavioural bias, a structural constraint), a test and a decision rule fixed before seeing data | "Shares that went up keep going up." Turn it into a claim that data could prove wrong | `token-tray` (hypothesis builder), `free-response` | Trap: writing the hypothesis after the backtest. Edge: a claim with no mechanism can still be true, but you then need far more evidence (RM4) | S, C, S, O |
| 3 | `lsn.method.data-sources` Where data comes from, and what you may do with it | Choose a free source for each project (yfinance, Nasdaq Data Link (formerly Quandl), Kenneth French's library, Open Source Asset Pricing, FRED, Deribit's public API), state its limits and licence, and cache raw downloads with a manifest | The night before your deadline the price library stops working. What do you wish you had done a month ago? | `card-sort`, `setup-checklist` (project) | Trap: committing downloaded data to a public repository (most terms forbid redistribution). Edge: free price data usually omits delisted shares (lesson 4) | S, M, C, O |
| 4 | `lsn.method.survivorship-point-in-time` Survivorship and point-in-time universes | Measure survivorship bias by comparing today's survivors with a point-in-time universe that keeps delisted names and their delisting returns | An equal-weighted portfolio of today's index members, backtested over 20 years, beats the index by 2% a year. Why? | `backtest-lab` (bias: universe) | Trap: dropping delisted shares, or giving them a zero final return. Edge: survivorship also hides in "shares with 10 years of data" filters | N, C, O, S |
| 5 | `lsn.method.corporate-actions` Corporate actions, adjustments and restated data | Apply split and dividend adjustments correctly, and detect the look-ahead created by back-adjusted price levels and restated fundamentals | A back-adjusted series shows a price of 0.83 dollars in 2005; the share really traded near 25. Is anything wrong for a rule that buys below 5 dollars? (Yes: use unadjusted prices for level filters) | `share-lab` (split), `value-table` | Trap: adjusted prices are right for every purpose (right for returns, wrong for levels). Edge: fundamentals restated later must be used as first reported | N, C, S, M |
| 6 | `lsn.method.data-quality` Data quality checks before any result | Write checks for stale prices, outliers, gaps, duplicate timestamps and calendar or time-zone mismatches, and decide for each whether to fix, flag or drop | A share "returned" 900% in one day in your data. Bug or event? | `code-cell` (pandas checks), `time-series-lab` | Trap: winsorising or dropping rows silently, which can create survivorship or look-ahead. Edge: some extreme days are real (a takeover); the check must flag, and a person must decide | K, S, C, O |

Transfer: lesson 2's pre-registration is required for project 2 (AR8) and every 3.1 replication; lessons 4 and 5 are the two biases a 3.1 replication most often finds in a published result.

Then the Level review and homework `set.method.questions-data`.

**Level RM2: Measuring performance** (`lvl.method.performance`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | `lsn.method.excess-returns` Excess returns and annualisation | Compute excess returns, annualise means and volatilities with their assumptions stated, and aggregate simple returns across assets and log returns across time | A long-short strategy earns 0.04% a day with daily volatility 0.6%. Annual Sharpe ratio? (1.06) | `sharpe-lab` (compute, annualise) | Trap: subtracting the cash rate from a self-financing long-short return (its excess return is already the return). Edge: $\sqrt{252}$ assumes independent days (lesson 15) | N, N, C, M |
| 8 | `lsn.method.risk-adjusted` Sharpe, Sortino, information ratio and tracking error | Compute each ratio and state the question it answers | Two funds have the same Sharpe ratio; one loses rarely but heavily. Which ratio sees the difference? | `sharpe-lab`, `drawdown-lab` (compare) | Trap: Sortino's downside deviation computed over the losing days only (it is over all days, with gains set to zero). Edge: an information ratio against the wrong benchmark measures nothing | N, N, S, C |
| 9 | `lsn.method.drawdowns` Drawdowns, Calmar and time under water | Compute maximum drawdown, the Calmar ratio and drawdown duration, and explain why maximum drawdown grows with the length of the record | Two strategies both have Sharpe 1; one has 3 years of history, the other 15. Whose worst drawdown will look bigger? (The longer record's) | `drawdown-lab` (Sharpe, Calmar), `sim-runner` | Trap: comparing Calmar ratios measured over different lengths. Edge: time under water matters to an investor as much as depth | N, N, C, P |
| 10 | `lsn.method.turnover-capacity` Turnover, holding period and cost drag | Compute turnover from weight changes, the cost drag it implies, and the average holding period | A book trades 40% of its value a day (counting buys and sells) at 5 basis points per unit traded. Annual cost drag? (504 basis points) | `signal-lab` (turnover), `backtest-lab` (costs) | Trap: counting one side of each trade. Edge: halving turnover rarely halves the signal, which is why smoothing often pays (AR lesson 110) | N, N, C, S |
| 11 | `lsn.method.hit-rate-skew` Hit rate, payoff ratio and skew | Compute expectancy from hit rate and payoff ratio, and explain why a high hit rate can hide a negative expectancy and a fat left tail | A strategy wins 90% of trades by USD 100 and loses 10% by USD 1,000. Expected P&L per trade? ($-10$) | `bet-lab` (ev), `dist-explorer` | Trap: a high hit rate means a good strategy. Edge: selling options has exactly this shape (OA lesson 174) | N, N, C, C |
| 12 | `lsn.method.benchmarks-beta` Alpha or beta? Regressing on factors | Regress strategy returns on market and factor returns, read alpha and its $t$-statistic, and split performance into factor and residual parts | A strategy returned 8% a year with beta 1.2, while the market returned 7% and cash 2%. Alpha? (0) | `regression-lab` (beta preset), `factor-model-lab` | Trap: return above the index is alpha. Edge: a strategy with zero alpha against the market may still be cheap exposure worth having | N, N, C, S |

Transfer: every results note in 2.3 and 3.1 reports these numbers with the conventions fixed here; lesson 12 is the first question an interviewer asks about any backtest.

Then the Level review and homework `set.method.performance`.

**Level RM3: Is it real? Inference for one strategy** (`lvl.method.inference`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 13 | `lsn.method.sharpe-standard-error` How noisy is a Sharpe ratio? | Compute $\mathrm{SE}(\widehat{SR}) \approx \sqrt{(1 + \frac12\widehat{SR}^2)/T}$ in per-period units, annualise it, and give an interval (recapping 1.6 lesson 19) | Three years of daily returns, annual Sharpe 1.0. A 95% interval? (about $-0.13$ to 2.13: the standard error is 0.58) | `sharpe-lab` (sampling, years) | Trap: a Sharpe of 1 over three years is proven. Edge: the units: per-day and annual Sharpe ratios differ by $\sqrt{252}$, and so do their standard errors | N, N, C, P |
| 14 | `lsn.method.minimum-track-record` Skew, kurtosis and the minimum track record | Compute the probabilistic Sharpe ratio and the minimum track record length with skewness and kurtosis | How many years of daily data before you can be 95% sure that a strategy with annual Sharpe 0.5 has a true Sharpe above 0? (about 10.8) | `sharpe-lab` (PSR, years) | Trap: ignoring negative skew, which raises the bar. Edge: fat tails matter most for high-Sharpe strategies, through the $\widehat{SR}^2$ term | N, N, C, X |
| 15 | `lsn.method.autocorrelation-hac` Autocorrelated returns: annualisation and HAC errors | Correct an annualised Sharpe ratio for autocorrelation with Lo's $\eta(q)$, and compute Newey-West standard errors for a mean of overlapping returns | Monthly returns of a smoothed, illiquid fund have autocorrelations $0.3^k$. Does $\sqrt{12}$ overstate its annual Sharpe? (Yes, by about a third: $\eta(12) = 2.61$ against 3.46) | `sharpe-lab` (annualise), `time-series-lab` | Trap: overlapping monthly returns built from daily data treated as independent. Edge: negative autocorrelation makes $\sqrt{12}$ understate | N, C, N, M |
| 16 | `lsn.method.block-bootstrap` The block bootstrap for a Sharpe ratio | Build an interval for a Sharpe ratio by the stationary bootstrap and explain why resampling single days understates the uncertainty of dependent returns | Resampling single days of a volatility-clustered series gives a narrow interval. Is it honest? (No: it destroys the clusters) | `sampling-lab` (bootstrap), `code-cell` | Trap: the plain bootstrap for time series. Edge: the block length is a choice to report and vary | C, K, N, S |
| 17 | `lsn.method.random-entry-null` Does it beat chance? Nulls with the same turnover | Build a null distribution from random entries (or circularly shifted signals) that match holding period and exposure, and compute an empirical p-value | A moving-average rule earns Sharpe 0.6; of 1,000 random-entry strategies with the same holding period, 120 beat it. p-value? (121 of 1,001, about 0.12) | `paper-trader` (null), `backtest-lab` | Trap: testing against zero when a long-biased rule rides a rising market. Edge: add one to numerator and denominator so the p-value is never 0 | N, C, M, S |
| 18 | `lsn.method.regime-stability` Sub-periods and stability | Compare sub-period performance against its sampling error and decide between decay and noise | A strategy's Sharpe by decade: 1.4, 0.2, $-0.3$. Decay or noise? (Each decade's standard error is about 0.32, so 1.4 against $-0.3$ is 3.8 standard errors apart: decay) | `sharpe-lab`, `backtest-lab` (holdout) | Trap: one great decade proves a strategy. Edge: a fall after publication is expected (lesson 30) | N, C, O, S |

Transfer: lessons 13 to 17 are the uncertainty section of every results note; lesson 15 is why hedge-fund indices of illiquid assets look safer than they are; lesson 17 is the null every project 5 bot is judged against.

Then the Level review and homework `set.method.inference`.

**Level RM4: Many trials, one winner** (`lvl.method.selection`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 19 | `lsn.method.trial-ledger` Counting what you tried: the trial ledger | Log every configuration tested, estimate the number of effectively independent trials by clustering correlated variants, and report it with the result | You tried 5 look-back windows, 4 holding periods and 3 universes. How many trials do you report? (60, and the effective number after clustering) | `ml-workbench` (ledger), `cluster-lab` | Trap: reporting only the final specification. Edge: trials you only looked at in a chart count too | N, C, M, S |
| 20 | `lsn.method.expected-max-sharpe` The best of $N$ noise strategies | Compute the expected maximum Sharpe ratio of $N$ independent noise strategies, about $\mathrm{SE} \times [(1 - \gamma)\Phi^{-1}(1 - \frac1N) + \gamma\Phi^{-1}(1 - \frac1{Ne})]$, and compare a found result with it (recapping 1.6 lesson 42) | You test 100 noise strategies on 5 years of daily data. Expected best annual Sharpe? (about 1.13: 2.53 standard errors of 0.45) | `overfit-lab` (zoo) | Trap: the winner's held-out Sharpe will match its in-sample Sharpe. Edge: correlated trials inflate less than independent ones, which is why the effective count matters | N, C, P, N |
| 21 | `lsn.method.deflated-sharpe` The deflated Sharpe ratio | Compute the deflated Sharpe ratio from the Sharpe ratio, sample length, skewness, kurtosis, the number of trials and the variance of the trials' Sharpe ratios | Your best of 50 trials has annual Sharpe 1.5 over 4 years of daily data, skew $-0.5$, kurtosis 5; the trials' annual Sharpe ratios have variance 0.25. Significant? (The deflated Sharpe ratio is 0.76, below 0.95: no, although the plain PSR is 0.998) | `sharpe-lab` (deflated) | Trap: $N$ taken as the number of final candidates. Edge: the hurdle grows with the dispersion of the trials, so wild trials cost more | N, N, C, M |
| 22 | `lsn.method.pbo-cscv` The probability of backtest overfitting | Compute PBO by combinatorially symmetric cross-validation from a performance matrix | 16 blocks give 12,870 ways to split them in half; in 8,500 the in-sample winner ranks below the median out of sample. PBO? (0.66) | `overfit-lab` (CSCV) | Trap: one train-test split is enough evidence. Edge: PBO near 0.5 means selection added nothing; above 0.5 it did harm | N, O, C, N |
| 23 | `lsn.method.reality-check` White's reality check and the $t > 3$ hurdle | Test whether the best of many strategies beats a benchmark with a bootstrap of the maximum, and apply Harvey, Liu and Zhu's hurdle and haircut | The best of 200 strategies has $t = 2.8$. Significant? (No: Bonferroni needs about 3.66; Harvey, Liu and Zhu's hurdle is about 3) | `multiple-testing` (strategy zoo), `sharpe-lab` (haircut) | Trap: no correction, or Bonferroni on highly correlated strategies (too strict). Edge: Hansen's SPA test removes the power lost to bad strategies in the set | C, N, M, S |
| 24 | `lsn.method.parameter-plateaus` Parameter sweeps: plateaus against spikes | Read a parameter heat map, prefer plateaus to spikes, and fix the selection rule before running the sweep | A moving-average grid on a random walk: the best cell has Sharpe 1.4, its neighbours $-0.2$. Real? (No: a spike on noise) | `overfit-lab` (sweep) | Trap: picking the best cell. Edge: a genuine plateau can still be a single overfit choice made one level up (the universe, the period) | C, P, O, S |

Transfer: every 2.3 backtest and alpha note reports its trial count and deflated Sharpe ratio (outcome PJ-O4); lessons 20 and 21 are the same arithmetic as test-set reuse in ML benchmarks.

Then the Level review and homework `set.method.selection`.

**Level RM5: Validation through time** (`lvl.method.validation`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 25 | `lsn.method.time-splits` Training, validation and test in time | Lay out time-ordered splits with one final test, and explain why shuffled splits leak (recapping 2.2 lesson 21) | Shuffled 5-fold cross-validation gives an $R^2$ of 0.08 on next-day returns; time-ordered folds give 0.00. Which is right? (The time-ordered one) | `time-cv-lab`, `backtest-lab` (holdout) | Trap: shuffled cross-validation on time series. Edge: even time-ordered folds leak when labels overlap (lesson 27) | C, O, M, S |
| 26 | `lsn.method.walk-forward` Walk-forward: rolling and expanding windows | Implement walk-forward refits with rolling or expanding windows, and count the out-of-sample observations they give | Ten years of data, train on 3, test on 1, step 1 year. How many out-of-sample years? (7) | `time-cv-lab`, `backtest-lab` (holdout) | Trap: tuning on the walk-forward results and still calling them out of sample. Edge: an expanding window adapts more slowly to regime change | N, O, C, S |
| 27 | `lsn.method.purge-embargo` Overlapping labels: purging and embargo | Purge training samples whose label windows overlap the test fold, and add an embargo after it | Labels are 20-day forward returns; the test fold is days 500 to 600. Which training days must go? (days 480 to 499, whose labels reach into the fold, and an embargo after day 600) | `time-cv-lab` (purge), `labelling-lab` | Trap: purging before the test fold only. Edge: the embargo's length should cover the serial dependence of the features, not only the labels | N, S, C, M |
| 28 | `lsn.method.cpcv` Combinatorial purged cross-validation | Count the splits and backtest paths of CPCV with $N$ groups and $k$ test groups, and read a distribution of out-of-sample Sharpe ratios | Six groups, two held out at a time. How many splits and how many full backtest paths? (15 splits; 5 paths) | `time-cv-lab` (cpcv) | Trap: treating the paths as independent samples. Edge: CPCV gives a distribution, but all of it comes from one history | N, N, C, S |
| 29 | `lsn.method.one-look` The held-out period: one look, written down | Fix the held-out period and the decision rule before research, and explain why a second look makes it validation data | The held-out Sharpe is 0.3. You tweak the rule and re-test: 0.9. Which number goes in the note? (0.3, with the tweak reported as a new trial) | `backtest-lab` (holdout lock), `results-note` | Trap: re-using the test set. Edge: the lock is a commitment device, so the app records when it was opened | C, M, O, S |
| 30 | `lsn.method.out-of-sample-decay` Out-of-sample decay and post-publication returns | Shrink a backtest Sharpe towards what live trading delivers, using the evidence on published anomalies | A published anomaly earned 0.8% a month in its paper. Plan on what? (McLean and Pontiff found returns about 26% lower out of sample and 58% lower after publication [recalled]) | `sharpe-lab`, `card-sort` | Trap: live performance equals the backtest. Edge: part of the decay is arbitrage by readers, which is evidence the effect was real | N, C, O, S |

Transfer: RM5 is the validation section of every project's `PLAN.md` and of the 2.1 capstone; lesson 27's purge is what `ml-workbench`'s audit strip enforces.

Then the Level review and homework `set.method.validation`.

**Level RM6: Reproducible research engineering** (`lvl.method.reproducible`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 31 | `lsn.method.repo-layout` A research repository's layout | Lay out a repository (a `src` package, `tests`, `data/raw` ignored by Git, `data/processed`, `notebooks` for exploration only, `reports`) and say what is committed | Your headline figure comes from a notebook called `final_v7_REAL.ipynb`. What is wrong? | `setup-checklist` (project), `card-sort` | Trap: logic living in notebooks that nothing tests. Edge: small synthetic fixtures belong in the repository; downloaded data does not | S, M, C, O |
| 32 | `lsn.method.environments-seeds` Pinned environments, lockfiles and seeds | Pin dependencies with a lockfile, seed every random source, and record versions in each run's output | A colleague re-runs your backtest and gets Sharpe 0.71, not 0.74. Name three causes | `setup-checklist`, `code-cell` | Trap: seeding NumPy's global generator only, while a library uses its own. Edge: floating-point results can differ across machines in the last digits, so tests need tolerances (lesson 34) | M, O, K, S |
| 33 | `lsn.method.caching-data` Caching and versioning data | Cache raw downloads immutably with content hashes and a manifest, store processed data as Parquet, and trace every result to a data version | Your data vendor revised a price after you downloaded it. Which version produced your figure? | `code-cell`, `json-explorer` | Trap: overwriting raw files in place. Edge: a manifest of hashes lets a reader verify your data without you redistributing it | C, M, K, S |
| 34 | `lsn.method.testing-numerical` Testing numerical research code | Write invariant, golden-file and property-based tests with stated tolerances (recapping 1.2 lessons 21 and 24) | A test of your Sharpe function fails on CI with 1.0000000002 against 1.0. Fix the test or the code? (The test: compare with a tolerance) | `code-cell` (pytest), `setup-checklist` | Trap: exact float equality; tests that recompute the expected value with the code under test. Edge: a golden file must be regenerated deliberately, with the reason in the commit | K, C, O, S |
| 35 | `lsn.method.ci-gate` Continuous integration and the pre-push gate | Set up CI that runs tests and lint, and run project-forge's pre-push gate: a secrets scan over the whole history, a dependency audit, a clean-clone test run, a licence | An API key sat in a commit for an hour before you deleted it. Is it safe now? (No: history is permanent; rotate the key) | `setup-checklist`, `card-sort` | Trap: deleting a secret in a later commit. Edge: a green CI badge on a public repository is itself a hiring signal (engineering floor) | O, C, M, S |
| 36 | `lsn.method.config-experiments` Configuration and experiment records | Separate configuration from code, and record each run's configuration, commit, data hash and metrics | Three weeks later you cannot say which settings produced the chart in your note | `ml-workbench` (ledger), `json-explorer` | Trap: parameters hard-coded across files. Edge: the run record doubles as the trial ledger of lesson 19 | S, M, O, K |

Transfer: RM6 is the engineering floor (`engineering-floor.md`) applied to research; the same habits are what ML labs mean by reproducible experiments.

Then the Level review and homework `set.method.reproducible`.

**Level RM7: Writing and defending results** (`lvl.method.writing`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 37 | `lsn.method.results-note` The one-page results note | Write a results note with question, data, method, results, what failed, limitations and next steps, each number matching the metrics file | Two notes on one backtest: one leads with "Sharpe 2.1"; the other with "0.8 after costs, one variant failed". Which earns the interview? | `results-note` (guided) | Trap: hiding failures, or burying costs in a footnote. Edge: a note longer than a page is a note nobody reads; `results-note` flags 500 words | O, M, C, S |
| 38 | `lsn.method.honest-charts` Honest charts for performance | Draw equity on a log scale with a drawdown panel, a benchmark, costs and uncertainty, and spot misleading choices | A linear-scale equity chart makes the last five years look like the whole story. Redraw it | `plot-2d`, `drawdown-lab` | Trap: linear scales for compounded wealth; start dates chosen after a crash. Edge: a chart of a held-out period should mark where it begins | C, O, P, S |
| 39 | `lsn.method.negative-results` Reporting what failed | Write a failure section naming each failed variant, why it failed and what it rules out | "Momentum with a one-month skip failed after costs." Useful or embarrassing? (Useful: it bounds the cost at which the idea works) | `results-note`, `free-response` | Trap: vague failures ("some variants underperformed"). Edge: a failure caused by a bug is not a research result; fix it and report the fix | M, C, S, O |
| 40 | `lsn.method.readme-dual-register` The README in two registers | Write a README a layman can follow and an interviewer can probe line by line, every claim backed in `docs/` (project-forge Phase 3) | The README says "fast backtester". What does the interviewer ask next? | `free-response`, `card-sort` | Trap: claims without evidence (benchmarks, tests). Edge: a limitations section raises credibility | S, M, S, O |
| 41 | `lsn.method.defence` Defending a project under questioning | Answer the standard follow-ups (what would make this wrong, how much was in sample, costs, capacity, what failed) concisely and with numbers | "Your Sharpe is 1.2. Why should I believe it?" | `viva`, `mock-interview` (project) | Trap: defensiveness, or claims without numbers. Edge: "I don't know, and here is how I would find out" scores better than a guess | C, M, S, O |

Transfer: RM7 is how every project is finished (`ASSESSMENT.md` 11.4) and the project round of a 3.3 mock interview.

Then the Level review and homework `set.method.writing`.

**Level RM8: The empirical finance toolkit** (`lvl.method.empirical`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 42 | `lsn.method.portfolio-sorts` Portfolio sorts and long-short spreads | Form quantile portfolios on a characteristic and test the high-minus-low return (recapping 1.1 lesson 91) | 500 shares sorted into quintiles by past return: Q5 minus Q1 averages 0.9% a month with monthly standard deviation 4.5% over 240 months. $t$? (3.10) | `portfolio-sort`, `signal-lab` (quantiles) | Trap: equal weights let microcaps dominate. Edge: breakpoints from large shares only (NYSE breakpoints) are the convention [recalled] | N, C, S, N |
| 43 | `lsn.method.fama-macbeth` Fama-MacBeth regressions | Run a cross-sectional regression each period and test the mean of the slopes with its standard error, with Newey-West lags where slopes are autocorrelated | 120 monthly slopes on momentum average 0.4% with standard deviation 2%. $t$? (2.19) | `regression-lab`, `code-cell` | Trap: pooled regression $t$-statistics on panel data. Edge: errors-in-variables when the regressors are estimated betas | N, O, C, M |
| 44 | `lsn.method.clustered-errors` Panel data and clustered standard errors | Explain why errors correlated across shares on the same date inflate $t$-statistics, and compute errors clustered by date | A pooled regression on 500 shares over 120 months reports $t = 8$; clustered by month, $t = 2.1$. Which do you believe? (The clustered one) | `regression-lab` | Trap: $N \times T$ observations treated as independent. Edge: clustering by firm and by date together when both dependences exist | C, N, O, S |
| 45 | `lsn.method.event-study` Event studies | Compute abnormal returns against a market model, cumulative abnormal returns around an event, and a test statistic across events | Over 400 earnings announcements the average abnormal return on the day is 0.25%, with standard deviation 2% across events. $t$? (2.5) | `time-series-lab`, `regression-lab`, `plot-2d` | Trap: an estimation window that overlaps the event. Edge: events that cluster in calendar time are not independent | N, O, C, M |
| 46 | `lsn.method.spanning` Does a new factor add anything? | Regress a new factor on existing ones and judge it by the intercept's $t$-statistic, and explain why a factor with a high Sharpe ratio can be redundant | A new "quality" factor has Sharpe 0.6 but loads 0.7 on an existing profitability factor; its intercept is insignificant. Keep it? | `factor-model-lab` | Trap: judging a factor by its own Sharpe. Edge: a factor redundant in one sample may not be in another; report both | N, C, O, S |
| 47 | `lsn.method.causality-confounding` Correlation, confounding and natural experiments | Identify confounders in a finance claim and name the designs that address them (difference-in-differences, index-inclusion discontinuities) | Firms that buy back shares outperform afterwards. Do buybacks cause the outperformance? | `card-sort`, `free-response` | Trap: causal language from a predictive regression. Edge: a prediction can be useful for trading even when it is not causal | C, M, S, O |

Transfer: RM8 is the toolkit of 3.1's Jegadeesh and Titman and Fama and French replications, and canon-gap P11's starting point (decision 20).

Then the Level review and homework `set.method.empirical`.

#### Course BT: The Backtesting Engine (`crs.backtest`, 8 levels, 44 lessons)

Project 1, the curriculum's first-ranked project: an event-driven backtesting engine with "transaction costs, slippage, a check against look-ahead, and a held-out period", reporting "Sharpe, Calmar and maximum drawdown". The course builds it as a reader would audit it: what a backtest is, portfolio accounting, fills and costs, biases switched on and off, sizing and risk, strategies to test the engine on, architecture and tests, then the project. Spine: López de Prado chapters 11 to 14; Chan, Quantitative Trading, chapters on backtesting and execution; Harris for fills; the 1.7 MK course for costs.

**Level BT1: What a backtest is** (`lvl.backtest.basics`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 48 | `lsn.backtest.event-loop` A backtest replays history with only the past | Step an event loop bar by bar and name what the strategy may know at each moment | At 16:00 on Monday the strategy sees the close. When can it trade, and at what price? (At Tuesday's open, at the open's price, at the earliest) | `backtest-lab` (step) | Trap: trading at the close that produced the signal. Edge: a closing-auction order placed before the close is possible, but then the signal cannot use the close | O, C, S, S |
| 49 | `lsn.backtest.vectorised-vs-event` Vectorised against event-driven backtests | Implement a vectorised backtest with a lagged position and compare it with an event loop, naming what each can and cannot model | A vectorised and an event-driven backtest of one rule disagree by 0.3 in Sharpe. Which do you trust, and how do you find out? | `code-cell`, `backtest-lab` | Trap: vectorised backtests cannot have look-ahead (one missing lag gives it). Edge: path-dependent rules (stops, limits on cash) need the event loop; zipline itself is unmaintained, so a library engine means zipline-reloaded, and project 1 builds its own | K, C, M, T |
| 50 | `lsn.backtest.timing` Signal, decision and execution times | Map the timestamps of signal, decision and execution and credit each position with the right return | A signal from today's close is traded at tomorrow's open. Which return does the position earn first: open to open, close to close, or open to close? (tomorrow's open to close, then close to close) | `backtest-lab` (timing) | Trap: crediting the close-to-close return to a position entered at the next open. Edge: in a daily bar the open is a different market from the close (auction against continuous trading) | C, N, O, S |
| 51 | `lsn.backtest.data-model` Bars, calendars and alignment | Align several assets on a trading calendar, handle missing bars and holidays, and forward-fill only where it is valid | One share has no bar on a day the others trade. What does the portfolio hold, and at what mark? | `time-series-lab`, `value-table` | Trap: forward-filling a price across a delisting or a suspension. Edge: markets in different countries share few holidays | C, M, S, K |
| 52 | `lsn.backtest.corporate-actions-engine` Dividends and splits inside the engine | Process splits as quantity changes and dividends as cash on their dates, so an unadjusted price feed gives correct P&L | You hold 100 shares at USD 50 through a 2-for-1 split and then a USD 1 dividend per new share. Change in equity from the actions alone? (0: 200 shares, +200 cash, price down 1 a share) | `share-lab`, `backtest-lab` | Trap: using adjusted prices and also paying the dividends (counted twice). Edge: a short position pays the dividend | N, C, S, N |
| 53 | `lsn.backtest.benchmark` Buy-and-hold: the engine's first test | Reproduce an index's total return with the engine's buy-and-hold, as a golden test, and compare strategies with a beta-matched benchmark | Your engine's buy-and-hold of an index lags the index's total return by 1.3% a year. Where is the leak? (dividends, cash drag or costs) | `backtest-lab`, `drawdown-lab` | Trap: a benchmark without dividends. Edge: a strategy with beta 0.5 should be compared with half the index plus cash | N, M, C, K |

Transfer: lesson 48's rule ("what could the strategy see at that moment?") is the question behind every leakage audit in 2.1 and every 3.1 replication.

Then the Level review and homework `set.backtest.basics`.

**Level BT2: Portfolio accounting** (`lvl.backtest.accounting`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 54 | `lsn.backtest.cash-positions-equity` Cash, positions and equity | Maintain cash, positions and equity through trades and marks, keeping equity equal to cash plus the marked positions | Start with USD 100,000; buy 300 shares at 100 with a USD 5 fee; the price moves to 102. Cash and equity? (69,995; 100,595) | `value-table` (blotter), `backtest-lab` | Trap: forgetting fees in cash. Edge: money in minor units (integer cents) keeps the books exact (widget catalogue decision 3) | N, N, C, K |
| 55 | `lsn.backtest.realised-unrealised` Realised and unrealised P&L | Compute realised and unrealised P&L under first-in-first-out and average cost, and show the total is the same | Buy 100 at 10, buy 100 at 12, sell 100 at 13, with the price still 13. Realised P&L under each method? (FIFO 300, average cost 200; total 400 either way) | `value-table` (blotter) | Trap: the method changes total P&L (it only moves it between realised and unrealised). Edge: tax rules may force one method; the engine should support both | N, N, C, M |
| 56 | `lsn.backtest.shorts-financing` Shorts, financing and borrow fees | Account for short proceeds, borrow fees, interest on cash and dividends owed on shorts | You short USD 1m of a share for a year: interest on the proceeds 4%, borrow fee 0.5%, dividend yield 2%. Net carry? (+1.5%, USD 15,000) | `margin-lab` (short), `backtest-lab` | Trap: forgetting the dividends a short owes. Edge: hard-to-borrow fees can exceed 20% a year and can be recalled | N, C, S, N |
| 57 | `lsn.backtest.weights-to-orders` From target weights to orders | Convert target weights into share orders from current equity and prices, with lot rounding and minimum trade sizes | Equity USD 1m, target 2.5% in a USD 347 share. Order? (72 shares, a weight of 2.498%) | `code-cell`, `position-editor` | Trap: sizing from yesterday's equity before today's marks. Edge: rounding error is a tracking error that grows as the account shrinks | N, K, C, M |
| 58 | `lsn.backtest.gearing-margin` Gross, net and margin limits | Compute gross and net exposure of a long-short book and enforce margin limits in the engine | Long USD 1.5m and short USD 1.2m on USD 1m of equity. Gross and net? (gross 2.7 times, net 0.3 times) | `margin-lab`, `backtest-lab` (sizing) | Trap: net exposure measures risk (a market-neutral book can still be geared and lose heavily). Edge: brokers' margin rules differ for long and short legs | N, N, C, S |
| 59 | `lsn.backtest.invariants` Conservation laws as tests | Write invariant tests (equity reconciles; no fill before its signal; positions equal the sum of fills; cash changes by trade value plus fees) that run on every backtest | Your engine's equity drifts from cash plus positions by USD 0.01 a day. Rounding or a bug? (A bug, if money is in integer cents) | `code-cell` (pytest), `setup-checklist` | Trap: floats for cash, then tolerances that hide bugs. Edge: the invariant must hold after every event, not only at the end | K, M, C, S |

Transfer: BT2's ledger is the same accounting a trading system's position keeper runs (MS7), and the invariants are 2.3's first starter-suite tests.

Then the Level review and homework `set.backtest.accounting`.

**Level BT3: Fills, costs and constraints** (`lvl.backtest.costs`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 60 | `lsn.backtest.fill-models` Fill models | Implement next-open, VWAP and limit-order fill models and state the bias of each | Your limit buy rests at 99.50; the day's low is exactly 99.50. Filled? (Not necessarily: queue position, MK lesson 32) | `backtest-lab` (timing), `order-book` (queue) | Trap: a touch is a fill. Edge: requiring the price to trade through the limit is conservative; a queue model is better still | C, O, N, S |
| 61 | `lsn.backtest.spread-commission` Spread and commission costs | Charge commission plus half the spread per unit traded and compute the cost drag of a turnover | A USD 10m book trades 30% of its value a day; half-spread 3 basis points, commission 1. Annual cost? (302 basis points, about USD 302,400) | `backtest-lab` (costs) | Trap: confusing cost per side with cost per round trip. Edge: spreads widen in stressed markets, exactly when strategies trade most | N, N, C, M |
| 62 | `lsn.backtest.impact-model` Market impact in a backtest | Add a square-root impact cost $Y\sigma\sqrt{q/V}$ with participation caps, and see capacity fall out of the backtest | Daily volatility 2%, $Y = 1$, and you trade 4% of average daily volume. Impact? (0.4%, 40 basis points) | `execution-lab` (impact), `backtest-lab` (costs) | Trap: linear impact, or none. Edge: impact is negligible for a personal account and dominant for a fund, so state the account size the backtest assumes | N, N, C, P |
| 63 | `lsn.backtest.volume-constraints` Volume, liquidity and borrow constraints | Cap trades at a share of volume, carry unfilled orders forward, and enforce borrow availability | The strategy wants 20% of a small company's daily volume. What does a realistic engine do? (Fill a capped share and carry the rest) | `backtest-lab`, `order-lifecycle` | Trap: full fills in thin names. Edge: carried orders change the strategy's realised turnover and costs | C, M, N, S |
| 64 | `lsn.backtest.break-even-cost` Cost sensitivity and the break-even cost | Sweep the cost assumption, find the break-even cost per trade, and compare it with realistic costs for the universe | Gross Sharpe 1.2, break-even cost 6 basis points. Large-cap costs run 2 to 5, small caps 20 or more. Verdict? (Viable in large caps only, and barely) | `backtest-lab` (costs), `slider` | Trap: reporting gross results. Edge: the break-even cost is the most honest single number in a backtest report | N, C, P, S |
| 65 | `lsn.backtest.funding-cash` Cash drag, interest and fees | Credit interest on idle cash, charge financing on borrowing, and charge fees when comparing with an investable benchmark | A strategy holds 30% cash on average; cash earns 4% and shares 9%. Drag against being fully invested? (1.5% a year) | `backtest-lab` | Trap: cash earning zero in a history where rates were 5%. Edge: interest on cash was near zero from 2009 to 2021, which flatters cash-heavy strategies in that period | N, N, C, M |

Transfer: BT3 is where 1.7's MK course becomes code; the break-even cost (lesson 64) is a required line of project 1's results note.

Then the Level review and homework `set.backtest.costs`.

**Level BT4: Biases you can switch on** (`lvl.backtest.biases`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 66 | `lsn.backtest.look-ahead` Look-ahead inside an engine | Find and fix look-ahead from shift errors, full-sample normalisation and same-bar fills, predicting each toggle's effect first | A signal is z-scored with the mean and standard deviation of the whole sample. Look-ahead? (Yes: the future sets today's score) | `backtest-lab` (bias), `time-series-lab` (shift) | Trap: full-sample statistics inside features. Edge: the "shuffle the future" test catches any look-ahead (lesson 85) | C, O, S, K |
| 67 | `lsn.backtest.survivorship-engine` Survivorship and delisting in the engine | Run a point-in-time universe with delisting returns and measure the bias | Two runs of one strategy differ by 2.1% a year; one uses today's universe. Which is wrong? | `backtest-lab` (bias) | Trap: missing delisting returns treated as zero, or the share silently dropped. Edge: delisting for a takeover is good news, for bankruptcy bad | N, C, M, S |
| 68 | `lsn.backtest.publication-lag` Publication lags and restatements | Lag fundamental data by its release date and use values as first reported | Results for the year to 31 December come out on 20 February. When may the strategy use them? (from 21 February) | `time-series-lab`, `value-table` | Trap: using the period-end date (recapping 1.1 lesson 88's point-in-time join). Edge: some vendors backfill history with restated numbers | C, N, O, S |
| 69 | `lsn.backtest.asynchronous-closes` Non-synchronous prices and time zones | Detect stale and asynchronous closing prices across markets and the false predictability they create | A rule uses the US close to trade Japan the next day and looks excellent. Why? (Tokyo closed hours before New York; the US close carries news Japan prices next morning) | `time-series-lab`, `sim-runner` | Trap: every market closes at the same moment. Edge: illiquid shares' stale closes create the same effect inside one market | C, M, S, N |
| 70 | `lsn.backtest.in-sample-parameters` Parameters chosen on the period they are judged on | Separate parameter selection from evaluation by fitting inside walk-forward windows | You chose the look-back that maximised Sharpe over 2000 to 2020 and report the 2000 to 2020 Sharpe. Problem? | `backtest-lab` (holdout), `overfit-lab` | Trap: tuning on the evaluation period. Edge: defaults "from the literature" were themselves chosen in sample | C, O, S, S |
| 71 | `lsn.backtest.bug-hunt` The bug hunt: a Sharpe above 2 is a bug until proved otherwise | Find the planted defect in each of three too-good backtests | Three equity curves with Sharpe 3.4, 2.8 and 2.2. Find the bug in each | `backtest-lab` (hunt), `code-surface` | Trap: accepting a high Sharpe because the code looks right. Edge: some real strategies (market making, short-horizon) do have high Sharpe ratios, with capacity to match | M, C, S, O |

Transfer: BT4 is the bias audit of the curriculum's 2.3 rule and canon-gap P1's exit (five planted defects, find four); every one of these biases has a twin in ML (lesson 66 is target leakage).

Then the Level review and homework `set.backtest.biases`.

**Level BT5: Sizing and risk control** (`lvl.backtest.sizing`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 72 | `lsn.backtest.vol-targeting` Volatility targeting inside the engine | Size positions as target volatility over forecast volatility with lagged forecasts and caps, and measure the effect on Sharpe and drawdown (recapping 2.2 lesson 31) | Target 10% volatility; the forecast is 25%. Position weight? (0.4) | `backtest-lab` (sizing) | Trap: sizing today's position with today's realised volatility. Edge: caps matter when forecast volatility collapses | N, C, S, P |
| 73 | `lsn.backtest.kelly` Kelly and fractional Kelly for strategies | Compute the continuous Kelly fraction $\frac{\mu - r}{\sigma^2}$ and explain why half Kelly is used when $\mu$ is estimated | Excess return 6%, volatility 15%. Kelly gearing? (2.67 times) | `kelly-lab` (continuous, fractional) | Trap: full Kelly with an estimated edge. Edge: twice Kelly grows at about zero (1.5 lesson 154) | N, N, C, M |
| 74 | `lsn.backtest.stops` Stop-losses: what they do | Test stop-loss rules against a no-stop baseline and explain that stops reshape the return distribution and help only when returns trend | Adding a 5% stop raises the hit rate and lowers the Sharpe. How? | `backtest-lab`, `sim-runner` | Trap: stops always reduce risk. Edge: stops are path-dependent and need the event loop (lesson 49) | C, O, N, S |
| 75 | `lsn.backtest.drawdown-control` Drawdown controls and gearing limits | Implement a drawdown-based de-risking rule and evaluate its cost in slower recovery | Halve exposure after a 10% drawdown: does maximum drawdown fall, and what does it cost? | `drawdown-lab`, `backtest-lab` | Trap: a rule that looks good on one historical path. Edge: run it on simulated paths too (BT6, lesson 82) | C, N, M, P |
| 76 | `lsn.backtest.rebalancing-frequency` Rebalancing frequency against cost | Choose a rebalancing frequency and no-trade bands by trading tracking error against cost | A slow signal rebalanced daily, weekly or monthly: which wins after costs? | `backtest-lab` (costs), `signal-lab` (turnover) | Trap: daily is always better. Edge: no-trade bands beat calendar rebalancing at the same turnover | C, N, P, S |
| 77 | `lsn.backtest.portfolio-risk` Portfolio risk: correlation, concentration and stress | Compute portfolio volatility from a covariance matrix, measure concentration, and stress-test against a historical episode | Ten positions of 10% each, all technology shares. How diversified is that? | `portfolio-lab` (n-asset), `covariance-lab` | Trap: the number of positions measures diversification. Edge: correlations rise in crises, so stress tests use crisis correlations | N, C, M, N |

Transfer: BT5's sizing rules are the bridge from a signal to a strategy, and Kelly reappears in 3.3's betting games.

Then the Level review and homework `set.backtest.sizing`.

**Level BT6: Strategies to test the engine on** (`lvl.backtest.strategies`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 78 | `lsn.backtest.time-series-momentum` Time-series momentum | Implement the sign of an asset's past 12-month excess return with volatility scaling across a set of futures or ETFs (Moskowitz, Ooi and Pedersen) and evaluate it with RM's tools | Does an asset's own past year predict its next month? | `backtest-lab`, `time-series-lab` | Trap: choosing the look-back in sample. Edge: trend following has done well in long crises, which is its main appeal | C, K, N, S |
| 79 | `lsn.backtest.pairs-in-engine` Mean reversion: the pairs rule inside the engine | Run 2.2 lesson 42's pairs rule in the engine with formation and trading periods, short costs and capacity | The 2.2 pairs rule made 12% a year before borrow fees. With them, and with next-open fills? | `pairs-lab`, `backtest-lab` | Trap: estimating the hedge ratio on the trading period. Edge: the spread can stop mean-reverting (a merger, a delisting) | N, C, M, K |
| 80 | `lsn.backtest.carry` Carry | Compute carry (interest differentials, futures roll yield) as a signal, and read its negative skew | Borrow yen at 0% and lend Australian dollars at 4%. What is the catch? (sudden crashes when the trade unwinds) | `backtest-lab`, `dist-explorer` | Trap: carry is free money because uncovered interest parity fails. Edge: its crashes cluster, so its Sharpe ratio overstates its safety (lesson 11) | N, C, O, S |
| 81 | `lsn.backtest.technical-rules` Indicator rules as a null hypothesis | Implement the curriculum's project 5 rules (EMA crossover, RSI and Supertrend) and test them against random entries with matched turnover | An RSI rule wins 62% of its trades in a bull market. Edge? | `paper-trader` (indicators, null), `backtest-lab` | Trap: hit rate as evidence. Edge: in a rising market a random long-only rule also wins most trades | N, C, M, S |
| 82 | `lsn.backtest.overfit-on-purpose` A deliberately overfit strategy | Overfit a seven-parameter rule on purpose and watch the engine's held-out section and deflated Sharpe ratio catch it | A seven-parameter rule has in-sample Sharpe 2.6. Predict its held-out Sharpe before you unlock it | `overfit-lab` (sweep), `backtest-lab` (holdout) | Trap: complexity as sophistication. Edge: the engine's report should make this failure visible without the author noticing it | C, N, P, S |

Transfer: lesson 78 is a 3.1 replication candidate; lesson 81 is the curriculum's verdict on project 5's indicator rules, demonstrated rather than asserted.

Then the Level review and homework `set.backtest.strategies`.

**Level BT7: Architecture, tests and speed** (`lvl.backtest.architecture`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 83 | `lsn.backtest.components` Components and boundaries | Design the engine as data handler, strategy, portfolio, execution model and reporting with typed interfaces, naming what crosses each boundary | Where do transaction costs belong: in the strategy or in the execution model? (execution: the strategy states intent, execution prices it) | `backtest-lab` (architecture), `node-link` | Trap: the strategy computing its own fills. Edge: the same strategy interface should run in the backtest and in the paper trader (MS7) | S, M, C, O |
| 84 | `lsn.backtest.events-determinism` Event ordering and determinism | Order same-timestamp events deterministically and make a backtest reproducible to the last digit | Two orders with the same timestamp, processed in a different order, give different P&L. Fix? (a stable tie-break by sequence number) | `node-link`, `code-cell` | Trap: relying on dictionary or set order, or on thread timing. Edge: reproducibility across machines needs pinned libraries too (RM lesson 32) | O, C, S, K |
| 85 | `lsn.backtest.test-pyramid` Testing a backtester | Build the test suite: unit tests for accounting, invariants on every run, a golden backtest, and property-based tests on random price paths | What single test would catch a look-ahead bug in any strategy? (Change the future prices: no decision before the change may move) | `code-cell` (pytest), `setup-checklist` | Trap: testing only the happy path. Edge: property tests need shrinking to give a readable failing case | K, M, C, S |
| 86 | `lsn.backtest.performance` Making it fast: profile, then vectorise | Profile an engine, vectorise the hot loop, and state the complexity of each stage | 500 shares, 20 years of days: 2.52m bars. The engine takes 40 minutes. Where does the time go? | `bench-lab`, `code-cell` | Trap: optimising before profiling. Edge: an event loop in pure Python runs at roughly a million simple events a second, so 2.5m bars should take seconds, not minutes [our estimate; measured in the lesson on the learner's device] | N, C, K, S |
| 87 | `lsn.backtest.report` The backtest report | Generate a standard report (metrics with standard errors, log-scale equity and drawdown, costs, break-even cost, held-out section, trial count, deflated Sharpe ratio) from a metrics file | What is missing from this one-page report? | `results-note`, `drawdown-lab` | Trap: a report without its held-out section and trial count. Edge: the report should be generated by code, never edited by hand | M, S, C, O |

Transfer: BT7's architecture is a project-forge component tree; the shuffle-the-future test (lesson 85) is the strongest single test in the course.

Then the Level review and homework `set.backtest.architecture`.

**Level BT8: Project 1, the backtesting engine** (`lvl.backtest.project`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 88 | `lsn.backtest.project-blueprint` Phase 1: the blueprint | Write `docs/PLAN.md` for the engine with all eleven project-forge sections (threat class A, failure behaviour for every file read and download, a test strategy per component) and survive the adversarial pass | A draft `PLAN.md` with five gaps: find them (no test strategy for the execution model; no failure behaviour for a missing price file; costs undefined; no held-out period; no data licence line) | `setup-checklist` (project), `free-response`, `card-sort` | Trap: a plan with no test strategy for some component. Edge: class A still needs the failure-mode, dependency and publication blocks | M, S, O, S |
| 89 | `lsn.backtest.project-build` Phase 2: stages and the starter suite | Plan the build as stages each sized to one review, run the starter suite, and paste results for verification | Which stage comes first: the data handler, the metrics, or the strategy? (the data handler and accounting, with their invariant tests) | `setup-checklist` (project) | Trap: a final "add tests" stage. Edge: the starter suite tests interfaces, so the learner's design must match its published signatures | O, C, M, S |
| 90 | `lsn.backtest.project-note` Phase 3: the README and the results note | Write the dual-register README and the results note (with break-even cost, trial count, deflated Sharpe ratio and the held-out result), consistent with the metrics file | Your note says Sharpe 0.9; the metrics file says 0.86. What does `results-note` do? (flags the mismatch) | `results-note` | Trap: numbers typed by hand. Edge: a held-out result worse than in-sample is normal and must be reported as it is | M, S, S, O |
| 91 | `lsn.backtest.project-defence` The defence | Answer a viva on your own engine (biases checked, fill model, cost model, what failed) at 7 of 10 | "How do you know there is no look-ahead in your engine?" | `viva`, `mock-interview` (project) | Trap: answering for a strategy when asked about the engine. Edge: naming what the engine cannot model is a strong answer | C, M, S, O |

Then the project's milestone pack (section 3.4.1) in place of a homework set.

#### Course AR: Alpha Research and Portfolio Construction (`crs.alpha`, 8 levels, 45 lessons)

Project 2, "the closest of the five to real research work": cross-sectional momentum and mean reversion across a few hundred shares, reporting "information coefficient, turnover and Sharpe after costs". The course goes from universes and signal construction through cleaning and neutralising, measuring predictive power, factor and risk models, and portfolio construction, to the honest-research checks that decide whether anything survived, then signals beyond price and the project. Spine: Grinold and Kahn, Active Portfolio Management, chapters 6 (the fundamental law), 14 (portfolio construction) and 16 (transaction costs) [check chapter titles]; Jegadeesh and Titman (1993); Isichenko, Quantitative Portfolio Management; data from Kenneth French's library and Open Source Asset Pricing.

**Level AR1: Universes and signals** (`lvl.alpha.signals`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 92 | `lsn.alpha.cross-section-vs-time-series` Cross-sectional against time-series signals | Tell a signal that ranks assets against each other from one that times a single asset, and say what each bets on | Every share fell 10% this month. Does a dollar-neutral cross-sectional momentum book lose money because of it? (Not from the fall itself: it is long some and short others) | `signal-lab` (universe) | Trap: a cross-sectional signal is a bet on the market. Edge: a long-only version of the same signal is mostly a market bet | C, S, S, O |
| 93 | `lsn.alpha.universe` Building a point-in-time universe | Build a universe of a few hundred liquid shares with filters on price, average daily volume and market capitalisation, each computed from past data only | You filter "the 500 largest companies" for 2010 using today's market capitalisations. What goes wrong? (2010's universe is chosen with 2024's winners) | `signal-lab` (universe), `backtest-lab` | Trap: today's index members for every past date. Edge: filters on liquidity change the signal's results more than most signal tweaks, so they are pre-registered | C, M, N, S |
| 94 | `lsn.alpha.momentum` Momentum: twelve minus one | Compute 12-1 momentum from monthly prices and explain the skipped month | A share was 50 twelve months ago, 60 a month ago and 57 today. Its 12-1 momentum? (20%: last month's fall is skipped) | `signal-lab` (construct), `code-cell` | Trap: including the most recent month, which carries the reversal of lesson 95. Edge: momentum measured in residual returns crashes less [recalled: Blitz, Huij and Martens 2011] | N, K, C, S |
| 95 | `lsn.alpha.reversal` Short-term reversal and liquidity provision | Compute one-week and one-month reversal and explain it as payment for providing liquidity, earned at high turnover | Last week's losers beat last week's winners by 0.5% this week. After costs? (Depends on spreads: in small caps it usually vanishes) | `signal-lab` (construct, turnover) | Trap: gross reversal returns in small caps taken at face value. Edge: reversal is strongest where liquidity is scarcest, which is where trading it costs most | N, C, M, N |
