| 96 | `lsn.alpha.classic-anomalies` Value, quality, low volatility and size | Define book-to-market, profitability, low-volatility and size signals with their publication lags, and state what each is thought to capture | Low-volatility shares have earned about as much as high-volatility shares. Why is that a puzzle for the CAPM? | `card-sort`, `signal-lab` | Trap: using a market capitalisation from the same date as a book value released months later. Edge: the size effect has been weak since its publication [recalled] | S, C, S, O |
| 97 | `lsn.alpha.open-data` Free data for cross-sectional research | Assemble price and signal data from free sources and document their limits | Free daily prices have no delisted shares. What does that do to a momentum backtest? (losers that died vanish, so the short leg looks worse and long-only looks better) | `card-sort`, `setup-checklist` (project) | Trap: survivorship from free price data. Edge: Open Source Asset Pricing provides signal portfolios built on the full universe, which the learner can use as a check [check access terms] | M, C, S, O |

Transfer: the universe of lesson 93 is the first section of project 2's pre-registration; lessons 94 and 95 are the signals the curriculum names and the subject of the Jegadeesh and Titman replication in 3.1.

Then the Level review and homework `set.alpha.signals`.

**Level AR2: Cleaning and neutralising signals** (`lvl.alpha.neutralise`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 98 | `lsn.alpha.normalise` Ranks, z-scores and winsorising | Normalise a raw signal by rank, by z-score and by winsorising, and choose for a fat-tailed signal | One share's raw signal is 50 standard deviations from the mean. What weight does z-scoring give it, and what does ranking give it? | `signal-lab` (normalise) | Trap: z-scores dominated by one outlier. Edge: ranks throw away magnitude, which matters when the signal's size carries information | N, C, S, P |
| 99 | `lsn.alpha.missing-values` Missing values in a cross-section | Handle missing signal values (exclude, sector median, zero after normalisation) without look-ahead and without shrinking the universe towards large companies | 30% of shares have no analyst coverage. Drop them, or fill? | `signal-lab`, `code-cell` | Trap: filling with values from the future, or dropping, which biases towards large companies. Edge: "missing" can be the signal (no coverage is itself informative) | C, M, K, S |
| 100 | `lsn.alpha.sector-neutral` Sector and industry neutralisation | Demean a signal within sectors and show that the unintended sector bet vanishes | Your value signal is long banks and short technology. Is that the bet you meant? | `signal-lab` (neutralise) | Trap: neutralising returns instead of the signal. Edge: very small sectors make within-sector ranks noisy; merge them first | N, C, S, A |
| 101 | `lsn.alpha.beta-size-neutral` Beta and size neutrality by regression | Residualise a signal on beta and size by cross-sectional regression and check the portfolio's exposures are zero | A dollar-neutral portfolio has beta 0.4. How can that be? (It is long high-beta shares and short low-beta ones) | `regression-lab`, `signal-lab` | Trap: dollar-neutral means market-neutral. Edge: neutralising on a noisy beta estimate leaves residual exposure | N, C, M, X |
| 102 | `lsn.alpha.combining-signals` Combining signals | Combine standardised signals by equal weights, IC weights or regression, and compute the gain from low correlation | Two signals each have IC 0.03 and are correlated 0.2. IC of their equal-weighted sum, assuming independent forecast errors? (about 0.039) | `signal-lab`, `covariance-lab` | Trap: adding correlated signals as if they were independent. Edge: IC weights estimated in sample overfit; equal weights are a strong baseline (lesson 132) | N, X, C, S |
| 103 | `lsn.alpha.formulaic` Formulaic signals and the delay rule | Build signals from operators (rank, time-series delta, time-series mean) with a one-period delay enforced, and spot leaks | `rank(-ts_delta(close, 5))` traded at the same close it was computed from. Leak? (Yes) | `signal-lab` (expression) | Trap: trading on the bar the signal used. Edge: Kakushadze's 101 formulaic alphas are examples of construction, not a menu of edges | T, C, M, K |

Transfer: AR2 is the "neutralise" step that separates an alpha from a factor bet, the same idea as removing confounders in ML features.

Then the Level review and homework `set.alpha.neutralise`.

**Level AR3: Measuring predictive power** (`lvl.alpha.measure`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 104 | `lsn.alpha.ic` The information coefficient | Compute Pearson and rank IC between a signal and next-period returns on one date | Five shares ranked 1 to 5 by signal have return ranks 2, 1, 4, 3, 5. Rank IC? (0.8) | `signal-lab` (ic), `value-table` | Trap: IC against same-period returns (look-ahead). Edge: Pearson IC is dominated by outlier returns; rank IC is the default | N, N, C, S |
| 105 | `lsn.alpha.ic-series` IC through time: mean, ICIR and $t$ | Compute the mean IC, its standard deviation, the IC information ratio and the $t$-statistic, with a correction for overlapping horizons | Mean monthly IC 0.03, standard deviation 0.12, over 120 months. $t$? (2.74) | `signal-lab` (ic-series) | Trap: overlapping 3-month returns sampled monthly treated as independent. Edge: an IC of 0.03 is typical of a good signal; 0.10 is suspicious | N, N, C, M |
| 106 | `lsn.alpha.ic-decay` IC decay and the holding period | Plot IC by horizon and choose a holding period and rebalancing frequency | IC at 1 day 0.04, at 5 days 0.03, at 20 days 0.01. Which horizon do you trade? | `signal-lab` (decay) | Trap: the horizon with the highest IC is best (costs scale with rebalancing). Edge: a signal whose IC rises with horizon is a slow signal; trade it slowly | C, P, N, S |
| 107 | `lsn.alpha.quantiles` Quantile portfolios and monotonicity | Compute quantile portfolio returns, check monotonicity and compute the long-short spread | Quintile returns: 0.5, 0.9, 0.4, 0.8, 1.2% a month. A good signal? (The spread is positive but the middle is not ordered: much of it may be one tail) | `portfolio-sort`, `signal-lab` (quantiles) | Trap: only the spread matters. Edge: a signal that works only in the short tail may be untradeable (lesson 125) | O, C, N, M |
| 108 | `lsn.alpha.ic-to-returns` From IC to expected returns | Relate IC to forecast returns, $\alpha = \mathrm{IC} \times \sigma \times z$, and to a signal-weighted portfolio's return | IC 0.05, residual volatility 30% a year, a share with a z-score of 1. Its forecast alpha? (1.5% a year) | `signal-lab`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: reading the IC as a return. Edge: the same formula says forecasts for volatile shares should be larger in return terms | N, X, C, S |
| 109 | `lsn.alpha.fundamental-law` The fundamental law of active management | Compute $\mathrm{IR} \approx \mathrm{IC}\sqrt{\mathrm{BR}}$, add the transfer coefficient, and explain breadth as independent bets | IC 0.02 on 500 independent bets a year. Information ratio? (0.45) | `signal-lab` (breadth) | Trap: breadth is the number of shares (`breadth-is-number-of-stocks`; correlated bets add less). Edge: constraints lower the transfer coefficient, and with it the realised ratio | N, N, C, M |
| 110 | `lsn.alpha.turnover-signal` Signal turnover and smoothing | Measure a signal's autocorrelation and turnover, and smooth it to trade IC against cost | Gross alpha 6% at a two-sided turnover of 20 times a year and 10 basis points per unit traded. Smoothing cuts turnover to 8 and alpha to 5.1%. Better? (Net 4.3% against 4.0%: yes) | `signal-lab` (turnover, net) | Trap: maximising gross IC. Edge: smoothing changes the effective horizon, so re-measure IC decay | N, C, P, S |

Transfer: lesson 104's IC is the curriculum's first required metric for project 2; lesson 109 is how a portfolio manager judges whether a signal is worth a team.

Then the Level review and homework `set.alpha.measure`.

**Level AR4: Factor models and risk models** (`lvl.alpha.risk`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 111 | `lsn.alpha.factor-models` CAPM, Fama-French and momentum factors | Regress a strategy on the five Fama-French factors plus momentum and interpret loadings and alpha (recapping 1.7 lesson 28) | A momentum strategy's alpha vanishes once the momentum factor is added. Surprising? (No: it is the factor) | `factor-model-lab`, `regression-lab` | Trap: alpha measured against the market only. Edge: a signal can be valuable even if a factor explains it, if the factor is not already held | N, C, M, S |
| 112 | `lsn.alpha.covariance-estimation` Estimating a covariance matrix | Estimate a sample covariance, explain why it is singular when there are more shares than days, and shrink it (Ledoit and Wolf; recapping 1.4 lesson 95) | 500 shares, 250 days of returns. Rank of the sample covariance matrix? (at most 249) | `covariance-lab`, `matrix` | Trap: inverting a sample covariance for optimisation. Edge: shrinkage towards a constant-correlation target often beats the identity | N, C, S, N |
| 113 | `lsn.alpha.factor-risk-model` Factor risk models: $\Sigma = BFB^\top + D$ | Build a factor covariance and count its parameters against a full sample covariance | 500 shares and 10 factors. Parameters in each? (5,555 against 125,250) | `factor-model-lab`, `matrix` | Trap: idiosyncratic risks treated as correlated after the factors have been removed (the model assumes not; check residual correlations). Edge: industry factors are often the largest | N, X, C, M |
| 114 | `lsn.alpha.pca-factors` Statistical factors by PCA | Extract principal components of returns, interpret the first as the market, and use components for risk (recapping 1.4's PCA) | The first component explains 35% of the variance and all its loadings are positive. What is it? (the market) | `covariance-lab`, `eigen-finder` | Trap: components are stable over time. Edge: their signs and order can swap between windows, so match them before comparing | C, N, O, S |
| 115 | `lsn.alpha.risk-decomposition` Risk contributions | Compute marginal and percentage risk contributions $\frac{w_i(\Sigma w)_i}{w^\top\Sigma w}$ and find concentration | A 60/40 portfolio of shares (volatility 16%) and bonds (6%), uncorrelated. Share of the risk from shares? (94%) | `portfolio-lab`, `covariance-lab` | Trap: capital weights are risk weights. Edge: risk parity sets the contributions equal | N, N, C, M |
| 116 | `lsn.alpha.residual-returns` Residual returns: what is left after factors | Compute residual returns and evaluate a signal by its residual IC | A signal's IC on raw returns is 0.04 and on residual returns 0.01. What has it been forecasting? (mostly factor returns) | `signal-lab`, `factor-model-lab` | Trap: selling factor exposure as alpha. Edge: residual IC is the number a fund that already holds the factors cares about | N, C, O, S |

Transfer: AR4's risk model is what turns a ranking into positions a risk manager accepts, and the factor regression is the "is it just beta?" check of every 3.1 replication.

Then the Level review and homework `set.alpha.risk`.

**Level AR5: From signal to portfolio** (`lvl.alpha.portfolio`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 117 | `lsn.alpha.signal-to-weights` From scores to weights | Map scores to dollar-neutral weights (rank-weighted, top and bottom quantiles, proportional to z-scores) under a gross constraint | Five shares ranked 1 to 5: weights summing to zero with gross exposure 1? ($-\frac13, -\frac16, 0, \frac16, \frac13$) | `position-editor`, `code-cell` | Trap: forgetting to demean, which leaves a net market bet. Edge: rank weights ignore volatility; dividing by volatility equalises risk | A, N, C, K |
| 118 | `lsn.alpha.mean-variance` Mean-variance optimisation and its instability | Compute $w \propto \Sigma^{-1}\mu$ and show how estimation error drives extreme weights | Two shares, each 20% volatility, correlation 0.95, forecasts 5% and 6%. Optimal weights (unscaled)? ($-1.79$ and $+3.21$; with the second forecast at 5.5% they become $-0.58$ and $+1.92$) | `portfolio-lab` (frontier), `matrix` | Trap: the optimiser's output is the truth (it maximises estimation error). Edge: shrinking forecasts and covariances, or adding constraints, tames it | N, C, S, P |
| 119 | `lsn.alpha.constraints` Constraints and the optimiser | Add position, sector, gross and turnover constraints, solve with a convex optimiser, and read which constraints bind | A 2% position cap binds on 40 names. What does that tell you about the signal? | `code-cell` (cvxpy where shipped), `lagrange-explorer` | Trap: constraints are free (each binding one has a shadow price). Edge: an infeasible set of constraints must fail loudly, never silently relax | C, M, K, N |
| 120 | `lsn.alpha.tcost-aware` Trading towards the target: cost-aware rebalancing | Include costs in the objective, derive a no-trade region, and trade part of the way to the target each period (after Gârleanu and Pedersen) | Trading halfway to the target each day: when does that beat trading fully? | `backtest-lab` (costs), `signal-lab` (net) | Trap: optimise gross, then subtract costs. Edge: the best partial rate depends on how fast the signal decays (lesson 106) | N, C, P, S |
| 121 | `lsn.alpha.capacity` Capacity and market impact | Estimate capacity as the size at which impact costs erase net alpha, using the square-root law (1.7 lesson 44) | At USD 100m, impact costs take 1% of a 4% gross alpha, and they grow with the square root of assets. At what size is net alpha half the gross? (USD 400m) | `execution-lab`, `slider` | Trap: capacity from the average volume of the whole universe (the thin names bind first). Edge: capacity falls when many funds trade the same signal (lesson 127) | N, X, C, M |
| 122 | `lsn.alpha.portfolio-backtest` Running the portfolio through the engine | Run the signal's portfolio through the project 1 engine with costs, borrow and constraints, and reconcile its returns with the IC analysis | The IC analysis promised a Sharpe of 1.1; the engine reports 0.6. Where did it go? (costs, constraints, borrow, the transfer coefficient) | `backtest-lab`, `signal-lab` (net) | Trap: reporting the IC-implied Sharpe as the strategy's. Edge: the gap is itself a result for the note | N, C, O, S |

Transfer: AR5 is the portfolio construction module of a systematic fund; lesson 118 is why ML labs regularise and why risk models shrink.

Then the Level review and homework `set.alpha.portfolio`.

**Level AR6: Honest alpha research** (`lvl.alpha.honest`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 123 | `lsn.alpha.replication-crisis` How often published anomalies replicate | Summarise the evidence (McLean and Pontiff; Hou, Xue and Zhang; Jensen, Kelly and Pedersen) and set a prior for a new signal | Hou, Xue and Zhang tested hundreds of published anomalies with microcaps controlled. Most survive? (No, most failed their tests; Jensen, Kelly and Pedersen reach a kinder verdict with Bayesian multiple-testing corrections [check the figures before citing]) | `card-sort`, `sharpe-lab` | Trap: a published result is a true one. Edge: the two camps disagree mostly about microcaps and the right correction, which is itself the lesson | C, M, S, O |
| 124 | `lsn.alpha.sensitivity` Does it survive other universes, periods and definitions? | Run a pre-registered sensitivity grid and report every cell | Momentum works in large caps from 1990 to 2010 but not after. Is the idea dead or the period unusual? | `signal-lab`, `overfit-lab` (sweep) | Trap: reporting the best cell. Edge: a grid is itself a set of trials (RM4) | C, O, P, S |
| 125 | `lsn.alpha.long-short-legs` The long leg, the short leg and their costs | Split long-short returns by leg and apply borrow costs and short constraints | 80% of an anomaly's return comes from shorting microcaps. Can you trade it? | `signal-lab` (net), `margin-lab` (short) | Trap: the short leg is free. Edge: a long-only version against a benchmark is often the honest implementation | N, C, M, S |
| 126 | `lsn.alpha.signal-log` The research log across signals | Apply Benjamini-Hochberg across the signals tested, report a haircut Sharpe, and keep a log of every signal tried (recapping 1.6 lesson 40) | You tested 40 signals and 6 have $p < 0.05$. If none were real, how many would you expect? (2) | `multiple-testing`, `sharpe-lab` (haircut) | Trap: reporting the 6. Edge: signals built from one idea are correlated, so the effective count is lower | N, C, S, N |
| 127 | `lsn.alpha.crowding-crashes` Crowding and momentum crashes | Explain momentum's crashes after sharp market rebounds and the role of crowding, and size the strategy for them | Momentum lost heavily in the rebound of spring 2009 [check the size of the loss]. What did its short leg hold? (the beaten-down shares that rebounded most) | `drawdown-lab`, `dist-explorer` | Trap: momentum's past volatility says nothing about crash risk (scaling by it helps: Barroso and Santa-Clara). Edge: crowding raises correlation among holders, so many funds sell at once | C, M, S, N |

Transfer: AR6 is the part of project 2 a hiring manager reads first; it is canon-gap P1's replication material applied to one's own research.

Then the Level review and homework `set.alpha.honest`.

**Level AR7: Signals beyond price, and machine learning** (`lvl.alpha.beyond-price`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 128 | `lsn.alpha.fundamentals-lags` Fundamental signals and point-in-time accounting | Compute accruals and gross profitability signals from point-in-time accounting data with release lags | Two firms report the same earnings; one's are mostly accruals, the other's cash. Which do markets tend to over-price? (the accrual-heavy one, after Sloan) | `value-table`, `signal-lab` | Trap: period-end dates in place of release dates. Edge: accounting definitions change over time; document the version | C, N, O, S |
| 129 | `lsn.alpha.earnings-drift` Earnings surprises and post-earnings drift | Compute standardised unexpected earnings and measure the drift after announcements as an event study (RM lesson 45) | Shares with the best earnings surprises keep rising for weeks. Why has it not been arbitraged away? | `time-series-lab`, `plot-2d` | Trap: measuring the surprise against a forecast made after the release. Edge: the drift has weakened since its publication [recalled] | N, O, C, M |
| 130 | `lsn.alpha.alternative-data` Short interest, flows, revisions and text | Describe how short interest, fund flows, analyst revisions and text sentiment become signals, with their availability lags and backfill risks | A vendor's sentiment history starts in 2010 but the product launched in 2018. What should worry you? (backfilled history, built with hindsight) | `card-sort`, `free-response` | Trap: vendor backfill treated as point in time. Edge: a simple word-list sentiment score is a fair baseline for any text model | S, M, S, O |
| 131 | `lsn.alpha.ml-cross-section` Machine learning in the cross-section | Fit a regularised regression or gradient-boosted model to next-month ranks with purged walk-forward folds, and compare with linear signals (recapping 2.1's folds for the developer track) | A boosted model's IC is 0.05 against 0.03 for a linear signal. How do you make sure the gain is not leakage? | `ml-workbench`, `time-cv-lab` | Trap: features scaled or selected on the whole sample. Edge: Gu, Kelly and Xiu report that trees and networks helped on US shares, with small predictive $R^2$ [recalled] | C, M, K, S |
| 132 | `lsn.alpha.combining-many` Combining many signals | Combine dozens of signals by ridge regression on ranks under purged folds, and compare with equal weights | Thirty signals: does a fitted combination beat equal weights out of sample? (Often not by much: the fit's gains are eaten by estimation error) | `signal-lab`, `regularisation-path` | Trap: a combination fitted in sample. Edge: equal weights are a regularised estimator with infinite shrinkage | C, N, O, S |

Transfer: AR7 is the link to 2.1's machine learning and to a researcher's take-home, which is often exactly lesson 131's problem.

Then the Level review and homework `set.alpha.beyond-price`.

**Level AR8: Project 2, alpha signal research** (`lvl.alpha.project`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 133 | `lsn.alpha.project-preregistration` Phase 1: pre-registration and the blueprint | Write a one-page pre-registration (hypothesis, universe, signal definitions, horizon, metrics, held-out period, decision rule) before touching data, then `docs/PLAN.md` (threat class A) | A pre-registration that says "we will try several look-backs". What is missing? (the list, and the rule for choosing among them) | `setup-checklist` (project), `free-response` | Trap: a pre-registration written after a first look. Edge: deviations are allowed if reported as deviations | M, S, O, C |
| 134 | `lsn.alpha.project-build` Phase 2: stages and the starter suite | Plan the stages (data and universe, signals, IC tools, portfolio and engine run, report), run the starter suite and paste results | Which test catches a universe built with future data? (rebuild the universe for a past date and compare its membership with what was knowable) | `setup-checklist` (project) | Trap: research in notebooks with no tests. Edge: the starter suite checks the IC function against a seeded synthetic panel with a known IC | O, C, S, S |
| 135 | `lsn.alpha.project-note` Phase 3: the results note | Write the note: IC, its $t$, decay, quantiles, turnover, Sharpe after costs, deflated Sharpe ratio, capacity, held-out result and what failed | Your momentum works and your reversal fails after costs. Which goes in the note? (both) | `results-note` | Trap: a note about the signal that worked. Edge: a null result reported well is a finished project | M, C, S, O |
| 136 | `lsn.alpha.project-defence` The defence | Answer a viva on the project (universe, survivorship, costs, multiple testing, capacity) at 7 of 10 | "How many signals did you try before this one?" | `viva`, `mock-interview` (project) | Trap: not knowing your own trial count. Edge: "I tried 23; the ledger is in the repository" is the strongest answer | C, S, S, O |

Then the project's milestone pack (section 3.4.2) in place of a homework set.

#### Course OA: Options Analytics (`crs.options-analytics`, 8 levels, 43 lessons)

Project 3: an implied volatility surface and Greeks, "with Black-Scholes, binomial and Monte Carlo pricers compared", on yfinance option chains or Deribit's public API. The course takes 1.7's formulas to real quotes: data cleaning, implied volatility that never fails, smiles and surfaces, no-arbitrage checks and fitting, numerical pricers compared, Greeks in practice, and volatility as a traded quantity. Spine: Hull chapters 20 (Volatility Smiles and Volatility Surfaces) and 21 (Basic Numerical Procedures); Gatheral, The Volatility Surface, chapters 1 to 3 and 11; Glasserman, Monte Carlo Methods in Financial Engineering, chapters 1 to 4; Sinclair, Volatility Trading. Lesson ids carry short sub-prefixes inside `crs.options-analytics` (decision in 0.3).

**Level OA1: Option data** (`lvl.options-analytics.data`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 137 | `lsn.options-analytics.optdata-chain-anatomy` Option chain anatomy | Read expiries, strikes, bids, asks, volume, open interest and the contract multiplier, and compute mids and relative spreads | A quote of 1.20 bid, 1.30 ask. Mid and relative spread? (1.25; 8%) | `option-chain` (read) | Trap: the last trade price is the price. Edge: on Deribit options are quoted in the underlying coin, not dollars | N, S, C, M |
| 138 | `lsn.options-analytics.optdata-cleaning` Cleaning a chain | Discard crossed, zero-bid, stale and very wide quotes by stated rules, and keep a log of what was dropped | Tap every quote you would discard from a chain of 40 | `option-chain` (clean) | Trap: silent filtering, which changes the smile. Edge: deep out-of-the-money quotes with a zero bid carry information about the ask only | M, S, O, N |
| 139 | `lsn.options-analytics.optdata-american-dividends` American exercise and dividends | Identify when early exercise matters for listed equity options (calls before a dividend, deep in-the-money puts) and how it biases implied volatility from a European formula | A deep in-the-money call, strike 50, 30 days left, $r = 5\%$, and a dividend of 2 tomorrow. Might exercise today be optimal? (Yes: the dividend exceeds the 0.21 of interest saved by waiting) | `option-bounds` (american), `binomial-tree` (american) | Trap: treating US single-stock options as European. Edge: index options are usually European [check per product] | C, N, M, S |
| 140 | `lsn.options-analytics.optdata-implied-forward` The implied forward and discount factor | Regress $C - P$ on $K$ across strikes to recover the discount factor and the forward (1.7 lesson 62) | $C - P$ is 10.6, 1.0 and $-8.6$ at strikes 90, 100 and 110. Discount factor and forward? (0.96; 101.04) | `option-chain` (parity), `parity-lab` (forward) | Trap: plugging in an assumed rate and dividend. Edge: American options make the relation approximate; use near-the-money strikes | N, N, C, X |
| 141 | `lsn.options-analytics.optdata-time-conventions` Time to expiry | Compute time to expiry consistently (the expiry's time of day, calendar against trading days) and show its effect on short-dated implied volatility | An option expires in 2 days, over a weekend. Is $T$ equal to $\frac2{365}$, $\frac{2}{252}$ or something else? | `iv-solver`, `value-table` | Trap: whole days only, which distorts the shortest expiries most. Edge: weekends carry little variance; some desks weight time by expected variance | C, N, M, S |

Then the Level review and homework `set.options-analytics.data`.

**Level OA2: Implied volatility numerics** (`lvl.options-analytics.iv`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 142 | `lsn.options-analytics.ivnum-newton` Newton's method with vega | Implement Newton iterations for implied volatility, see quadratic convergence, and see failure where vega is tiny | A share at 100, a 3-month call struck at 150 priced at 30% volatility. Start Newton at 10%: where does the first step land? (above $10^{11}$: vega there is almost zero) | `iv-solver` (algorithm) | Trap: Newton always converges. Edge: started high (100%), where the price is concave in volatility, Newton falls steadily onto 30% | O, N, C, S |
| 143 | `lsn.options-analytics.ivnum-bracketing` A solver that cannot fail: brackets, bounds and Brent | Combine a bracketing method with Newton steps and reject prices outside the no-arbitrage bounds | A put quoted below its intrinsic value. Implied volatility? (none exists: flag the quote) | `iv-solver` (bounds) | Trap: returning a number for every input. Edge: tolerance in price is not tolerance in volatility when vega is small | C, O, N, M |
| 144 | `lsn.options-analytics.ivnum-otm-only` Out-of-the-money options only | Build the smile from out-of-the-money puts below the forward and calls above it, and explain why | Why do practitioners discard in-the-money options when building a smile? (wider spreads, early exercise, and their information is duplicated by parity) | `option-chain` (smile), `vol-surface` (smile) | Trap: averaging call and put volatilities at every strike. Edge: at the forward both are at the money and should agree | C, S, S, O |
| 145 | `lsn.options-analytics.ivnum-american-iv` Implied volatility for American options | Invert a binomial American pricer for implied volatility, and compare with the European approximation | An American put's European-implied volatility is 2 points above its American-implied volatility. Which is right? | `binomial-tree` (american), `iv-solver` | Trap: European formulas for American quotes. Edge: inversion through a tree is slow; vectorise or cache (lesson 146) | N, C, M, S |
| 146 | `lsn.options-analytics.ivnum-vectorise-test` Vectorising over a chain, and testing the solver | Write a vectorised solver with round-trip tests (price to volatility to price) and bound tests | Your solver passes for 99% of a chain and returns NaN for the rest. Which rows, and why? | `code-cell` | Trap: testing only at the money. Edge: property tests across random strikes and expiries find the edge cases | K, T, C, S |

Then the Level review and homework `set.options-analytics.iv`.

**Level OA3: Smiles and surfaces** (`lvl.options-analytics.surface`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 147 | `lsn.options-analytics.surface-skew` Smile and skew: why puts are dear | Plot implied volatility against strike and explain equity skew (crash risk, volatility rising as prices fall, demand for protection) | Why does a 20% out-of-the-money index put cost more in volatility terms than a 20% out-of-the-money call? | `vol-surface` (smile) | Trap: skew is an error in the data. Edge: some commodities and currencies show the opposite skew | C, M, S, O |
| 148 | `lsn.options-analytics.surface-coordinates` Strike, log-moneyness and delta | Convert strikes to log-moneyness $k = \ln(K/F)$ and to deltas, and compare smiles across expiries | Two expiries plotted against strike look different; against $k/\sqrt T$ they nearly overlap. Why? | `vol-surface` | Trap: comparing expiries in strike space. Edge: FX markets quote by delta, not strike | N, C, P, S |
| 149 | `lsn.options-analytics.surface-total-variance` Term structure, total variance and forward volatility | Compute total variance $w = \sigma^2T$ and the forward volatility between two expiries | One-month implied volatility 20%, two-month 25%. Forward volatility for the second month? (29.2%) | `vol-surface` (total-variance, term) | Trap: averaging volatilities instead of variances. Edge: forward variance must not be negative (lesson 154) | N, X, C, M |
| 150 | `lsn.options-analytics.surface-events` Event volatility | Back out the market's expected earnings move from the jump in total variance across the announcement | Normal volatility 25%; a 10-trading-day option spanning earnings is priced at 45%. Implied earnings-day move? (about 7.6%: $0.45^2 \times \frac{10}{252} - 0.25^2 \times \frac9{252} = 0.0058$) | `vol-surface` (event) [mode to add] | Trap: spreading the event over the whole period. Edge: after the event, implied volatility collapses (the "crush") | N, C, S, N |
| 151 | `lsn.options-analytics.surface-sticky` Sticky strike and sticky delta | Predict how the smile moves when spot moves under each rule, and the effect on the hedge ratio | Spot falls 5%. Under sticky delta, what happens to the at-the-money volatility? | `vol-surface`, `greeks-lab` | Trap: the smile stays still while spot moves. Edge: the rule changes delta, so hedges differ between desks | C, M, P, S |
| 152 | `lsn.options-analytics.surface-vix` Variance swaps and the VIX | Compute a variance-swap rate from a strip of out-of-the-money options with weights $\frac{\Delta K}{K^2}$, the VIX method in outline | Why does the strip weight each option by $\frac1{K^2}$? (to make the portfolio's payoff the log contract, whose value is variance) | `vol-surface`, `value-table`, `code-cell` | Trap: the VIX is an average of implied volatilities. Edge: the strip is truncated where quotes run out, which biases it low | X, N, C, S |

Then the Level review and homework `set.options-analytics.surface`.

**Level OA4: No arbitrage on the surface, and fitting** (`lvl.options-analytics.fitting`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 153 | `lsn.options-analytics.surface-butterfly-density` Butterfly arbitrage and the implied density | Compute the risk-neutral density from second differences of call prices (Breeden and Litzenberger, 1.3 lesson 74) and flag negative densities | The second difference of three neighbouring call mids is negative. What trade does that offer? (a butterfly that is paid to exist, 1.7 lesson 63) | `vol-surface` (density, arbitrage) | Trap: noise in mids read as arbitrage (check bid and ask). Edge: the density's left tail is fatter than the lognormal's, which is the skew | N, C, M, P |
| 154 | `lsn.options-analytics.surface-calendar` Calendar arbitrage | Check that total variance does not fall with expiry at fixed log-moneyness, and build the trade when it does | One-month volatility 30%, two-month 20% at the same moneyness. Arbitrage? (Yes: total variance falls from 0.0075 to 0.0067) | `vol-surface` (arbitrage) | Trap: comparing volatilities, not total variances. Edge: an event inside the shorter expiry can make its volatility far higher without arbitrage | N, C, O, S |
| 155 | `lsn.options-analytics.surface-svi` Fitting a smile: SVI | Fit Gatheral's raw SVI, $w(k) = a + b\left[\rho(k - m) + \sqrt{(k - m)^2 + \sigma^2}\right]$, to one expiry by constrained least squares | Drag the five parameters until the curve fits the dots; which parameter tilts the smile? ($\rho$) | `vol-surface` (svi) | Trap: unconstrained fits that create butterfly arbitrage. Edge: SVI's total variance is linear in the wings, which matches Lee's bound | P, S, C, N |
| 156 | `lsn.options-analytics.surface-wings` The wings: Lee's moment formula and extrapolation | Bound the wings' slopes (total variance grows at most like $2\lvert k\rvert$) and extrapolate safely beyond the last quote | A fitted smile's total variance rises with slope 3 in the far wing. Allowed? (No: above Lee's bound of 2) | `vol-surface` | Trap: polynomial extrapolation. Edge: the wings drive the price of variance swaps and far tail options most | N, C, O, S |
| 157 | `lsn.options-analytics.surface-interpolation` Interpolating across expiries | Interpolate total variance linearly in time at fixed log-moneyness, and compare with interpolating volatilities | Interpolating volatilities between two expiries creates a calendar arbitrage. Why does interpolating total variance not? | `vol-surface` (surface) | Trap: interpolating in strike between expiries. Edge: the very short end needs its own treatment (events, weekends) | C, M, X, S |
| 158 | `lsn.options-analytics.surface-fit-quality` Judging a fit | Choose fit weights (by vega, by inverse spread), and report errors in volatility and in price against bid and ask | A fit misses three quotes by 0.5 volatility points, inside their bid-ask spreads. A good fit? (Yes, if every error is inside the spread) | `vol-surface`, `value-table` | Trap: minimising errors in price, which ignores the wings. Edge: a perfect fit to mids is overfitting the noise | N, C, O, S |

Then the Level review and homework `set.options-analytics.fitting`.

**Level OA5: Three pricers compared, and a fourth** (`lvl.options-analytics.pricers`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 159 | `lsn.options-analytics.pricers-bs-reference` Black-Scholes as the reference implementation | Implement Black-Scholes with a stable normal CDF, handle $T \to 0$ and $\sigma \to 0$, and test put-call parity | Your Black-Scholes returns NaN at expiry. Why, and what should it return? (division by $\sigma\sqrt T$; the intrinsic value) | `code-cell`, `bs-explorer` | Trap: no limits handled. Edge: very deep out-of-the-money prices underflow; compute in logs or use the complementary error function | K, C, N, M |
| 160 | `lsn.options-analytics.pricers-tree-convergence` Tree convergence: odd-even, smoothing and Richardson | Measure the CRR tree's error against $N$, average odd and even $N$, and apply Richardson extrapolation | Errors at 500 and 501 steps are $-0.0040$ and $+0.0035$. Their average? (much closer: about $-0.00025$) | `pricer-compare` (european), `binomial-tree` (converge) | Trap: more steps always mean less error. Edge: placing a node on the strike removes most of the wobble | N, C, P, S |
| 161 | `lsn.options-analytics.pricers-monte-carlo` Monte Carlo pricing and its standard error | Price a European option by simulating terminal prices and report the standard error, which falls like $\frac1{\sqrt n}$ | To halve a Monte Carlo price's standard error, how many more paths? (four times as many) | `pricer-compare` (mc), `sim-runner` | Trap: reporting a Monte Carlo price without its standard error. Edge: for a European option one terminal draw per path is enough; paths are for path-dependent payoffs | N, C, M, N |
| 162 | `lsn.options-analytics.pricers-variance-reduction` Antithetic variates and control variates | Implement antithetic draws and a control variate (the terminal price, or a related closed-form option), and measure the variance reduction | An antithetic estimator uses $Z$ and $-Z$. Why does it help a call more than a straddle? (the call's payoff is roughly monotone in $Z$; the straddle's is symmetric) | `pricer-compare` (mc) | Trap: counting antithetic pairs as independent draws. Edge: a control variate's coefficient estimated from the same paths adds a small bias | C, N, O, S |
| 163 | `lsn.options-analytics.pricers-american-lsm` American options: tree against Longstaff and Schwartz | Price an American put by tree and by least-squares Monte Carlo with a polynomial basis, and compare | The least-squares price is 0.03 below the tree's. Expected? (Yes: the estimated exercise rule is suboptimal, so the estimate is low-biased) | `pricer-compare` (american), `regression-lab` | Trap: regressing on all paths, including out-of-the-money ones. Edge: the same paths used to fit and to price add a high bias | N, C, O, M |
| 164 | `lsn.options-analytics.pricers-finite-differences` A first PDE grid: explicit and Crank-Nicolson | Solve the Black-Scholes PDE on a grid with the explicit and the Crank-Nicolson schemes, and see the explicit scheme's stability limit | Halve the price step of an explicit grid: how many more time steps does stability need? (four times as many) | `pricer-compare` (pde) [mode to add], `code-cell` | Trap: refining the price grid alone. Edge: Crank-Nicolson oscillates near a kinked payoff unless the first steps are smoothed | N, C, O, S |
| 165 | `lsn.options-analytics.pricers-error-vs-compute` Error against compute: choosing a pricer | Plot error against time for formula, tree, Monte Carlo and PDE, and choose by product (European, American, path-dependent) | Which pricer would you use for a barrier option, and why? | `pricer-compare` | Trap: one pricer for everything. Edge: agreement of two independent pricers is the strongest test of both | S, C, P, M |

Then the Level review and homework `set.options-analytics.pricers`.

**Level OA6: Greeks and risk in practice** (`lvl.options-analytics.greeks`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 166 | `lsn.options-analytics.optrisk-bump-greeks` Greeks by bumping: step sizes and noise | Compute finite-difference Greeks, choose the bump size, and use common random numbers in Monte Carlo | Gamma by central difference with a bump of $10^{-6}$ is wildly wrong. Why? (cancellation: the difference of nearly equal prices loses digits) | `greeks-lab`, `code-cell` | Trap: smaller bumps are always more accurate. Edge: fresh random numbers for each bump make Monte Carlo Greeks useless | N, C, S, K |
| 167 | `lsn.options-analytics.optrisk-mc-greeks` Pathwise and likelihood-ratio Greeks | Derive the pathwise delta of a call, $e^{-rT}\mathbb 1\{S_T > K\}\frac{S_T}{S_0}$, and the likelihood-ratio delta for a digital | Why does the pathwise method fail for a digital option? (its payoff jumps, so its pathwise derivative is zero almost everywhere) | `pricer-compare` (mc), `derivation-steps` | Trap: pathwise for discontinuous payoffs. Edge: the likelihood-ratio estimator is unbiased but noisy | X, C, N, M |
| 168 | `lsn.options-analytics.optrisk-greeks-surface` Greeks across strikes and expiries | Map where gamma and vega concentrate across a surface | Where does a book of short-dated at-the-money options keep its risk, and where does a book of long-dated options? (gamma short-dated, vega long-dated) | `greeks-lab` (surface) | Trap: one number for "vega" across expiries. Edge: vega bucketed by expiry is what a risk report shows | P, C, O, S |
| 169 | `lsn.options-analytics.optrisk-risk-report` A position risk report | Build a report with delta, gamma and vega by expiry bucket and a spot-volatility scenario grid | Your book is flat vega in total but long the front month and short the back. Risky? (Yes, to a change in the term structure) | `greeks-lab` (portfolio), `value-table` | Trap: totals hide offsetting buckets. Edge: scenario grids catch the non-linear risks that Greeks miss | M, C, N, S |
| 170 | `lsn.options-analytics.optrisk-pnl-explain` P&L explain with real data | Attribute an options book's daily P&L to Greeks and a residual, and diagnose large residuals (smile moves, vanna, volga, jumps) | Yesterday's residual was 30% of the P&L. What do you look at first? | `pnl-attribution` (path) | Trap: ignoring the residual. Edge: a persistent residual means a missing Greek, not bad luck | N, C, O, S |

Then the Level review and homework `set.options-analytics.greeks`.

**Level OA7: Volatility as a traded quantity** (`lvl.options-analytics.vol-trading`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 171 | `lsn.options-analytics.voltrade-vrp` Implied against realised: the variance risk premium | Compute realised volatility, compare it with implied, and explain the usual gap as an insurance premium | Index implied volatility has been above the volatility later realised most of the time. Free money for sellers? | `time-series-lab`, `vol-surface` | Trap: the premium is free money (it is paid for crash risk). Edge: the premium turns sharply negative in crises | C, N, M, S |
| 172 | `lsn.options-analytics.voltrade-hedged-pnl` The delta-hedged P&L | Derive the daily P&L of a delta-hedged option, $\frac12\Gamma S^2(\sigma_{\text{real}}^2 - \sigma_{\text{impl}}^2)\Delta t$, from Taylor and the theta relation, and check it on a path (1.7 lesson 78) | Long gamma 0.05 per USD on a USD 100 share, implied 20%, and the share moves 2% in a day. Rough P&L from gamma alone? ($\frac12 \times 0.05 \times 2^2 = 0.10$, before theta) | `hedging-sim` (one-path, distribution), `pnl-attribution` | Trap: the hedged P&L depends on direction. Edge: where gamma sits when the moves happen matters, not only the total variance | X, N, C, S |
| 173 | `lsn.options-analytics.voltrade-straddles-gamma` Straddles, gamma trading and vega trading | Distinguish gamma exposure (short-dated) from vega exposure (long-dated) and design a trade for each view | You think volatility will spike next week, but not stay high. Short-dated or long-dated straddle? (short-dated: you want gamma) | `greeks-lab`, `payoff-builder` | Trap: any straddle expresses any volatility view. Edge: calendar spreads isolate the term-structure view | C, M, S, O |
| 174 | `lsn.options-analytics.voltrade-selling-vol` The risk of selling options | Analyse the return distribution of short puts and short straddles (negative skew, a high hit rate) and size them | A short-put strategy made money in 46 of 48 months. Its Sharpe is 1.5. What do you ask? (about the two months, and the tails RM lesson 11 warned of) | `dist-explorer`, `drawdown-lab` | Trap: hit rate and Sharpe describe the risk. Edge: one crash month can erase years of premium | C, N, O, S |
| 175 | `lsn.options-analytics.voltrade-dispersion` Index against single-share volatility: implied correlation | Compute implied correlation from index and constituent volatilities | An index of two equally weighted shares has implied volatility 25%; each share 30%. Implied correlation? (0.39) | `covariance-lab`, `matrix` | Trap: index volatility is the average of the members'. Edge: dispersion trades sell index volatility and buy member volatility to sell correlation | N, X, C, M |

Then the Level review and homework `set.options-analytics.vol-trading`.

(no server, nothing to attack, free to host, readable from the README) | `setup-checklist` (project), `free-response` | Trap: a live server for a portfolio project. Edge: Deribit's data are free to read; check the terms before publishing derived numbers [check] | M, S, O, S |

(round trips on prices generated by your Black-Scholes, across a grid) | `setup-checklist` (project) | Trap: testing on live data only. Edge: the starter suite's fixtures are synthetic chains with planted arbitrage | O, C, M, S |

In the note or out? (in, with the reason) | `results-note` | Trap: a dashboard with no written findings. Edge: the note's numbers must match the build's metrics file | M, S, S, O |
| 179 | `lsn.options-analytics.optproj-defence` The defence | Answer a viva on the dashboard (forward extraction, American quotes, arbitrage checks, pricer choice) at 7 of 10 | "How did you get the forward, and what if the dividend is wrong?" | `viva`, `mock-interview` (project) | Trap: not knowing where your inputs came from. Edge: naming the dashboard's limits (no live updates, American bias) scores well | C, M, S, O |

Then the project's milestone pack (section 3.4.3) in place of a homework set.

#### Course MS: Market Data and Trading Systems (`crs.systems`, 9 levels, 50 lessons)

Project 4, a live market data handler ("websocket ticks into multi-timeframe candles, stored in PostgreSQL or Redis, with latency measured"; in C++ on the developer track), and project 5, a paper trading bot built on projects 1 and 4. The course covers feeds, book reconstruction, candles and clocks, latency and throughput, storage and replay, performance engineering, the order lifecycle and risk checks, and running a system safely. Spine: Kleppmann and Riccomini, Designing Data-Intensive Applications, 2nd edition, chapters on storage and stream processing; Nasdaq TotalView-ITCH 5.0 specification; Bryant and O'Hallaron, CS:APP, chapters 11 and 12; MIT 6.172 lectures for MS6. Lesson ids carry short sub-prefixes inside `crs.systems` (decision in 0.3).

**Level MS1: Feeds and messages** (`lvl.systems.feeds`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 180 | `lsn.systems.feeds-ticks` Ticks: trades, quotes and book levels | Tell top-of-book (L1), price-level (L2) and order-by-order (L3) data apart, and pick the fields a candle and a book need | A candle needs which fields from a trade message? (price, size, exchange timestamp; not the order id) | `feed-sim` (messages), `json-explorer` (feed presets) | Trap: quotes and trades in one stream treated alike. Edge: some feeds send trades that do not update the book (off-exchange prints) | S, M, S, O |
| 181 | `lsn.systems.feeds-formats` Message formats: JSON and fixed-width binary | Parse a JSON and a fixed-width binary message, and compare their size and parsing cost | The same trade as JSON and as a fixed-width record: which is larger, and which parses faster? | `json-explorer`, `code-cell` | Trap: parsing numbers from JSON as floats for prices (use integers in ticks or decimals). Edge: binary formats need an agreed byte order | T, C, M, K |
| 182 | `lsn.systems.feeds-websockets` Websockets: subscribe, heartbeat, reconnect | Write the connection state machine with heartbeats and reconnection with capped exponential backoff and jitter | Reconnect delays of 1, 2, 4 seconds and so on, capped at 30. What is the sixth delay? (30, with jitter) | `feed-sim` (connection), `node-link` | Trap: reconnecting in a tight loop. Edge: jitter stops thousands of clients reconnecting in step | O, N, C, S |
| 183 | `lsn.systems.feeds-sequence-gaps` Sequence numbers, gaps and resynchronisation | Detect gaps and duplicates by sequence number and resynchronise from a snapshot | Messages arrive numbered 1041, 1042, 1045. What do you do? (Flag the gap 1043 to 1044 and resynchronise before trusting the book) | `feed-sim` (book, recovery) | Trap: carrying on after a gap. Edge: a duplicate is harmless only if processing is idempotent | N, O, C, M |
| 184 | `lsn.systems.feeds-validation` Validating at the boundary | Validate every message once at entry (schema, types, bounds) and decide what to do with a bad one | A message carries a negative size. Drop it, log it, or halt? | `json-explorer`, `card-sort` | Trap: validation scattered through the code. Edge: the engineering floor treats every API response as external input | S, M, S, O |
| 185 | `lsn.systems.feeds-public-apis` Public market data APIs and their rules | Use a public feed (Deribit's websocket needs no key for market data [check]) within its rate limits and terms | Your script polls a REST endpoint 20 times a second and gets throttled. What should it do instead? (subscribe to the stream) | `card-sort`, `setup-checklist` | Trap: polling where a stream exists. Edge: a key with trading permission must never be used for market data | C, M, S, O |

Then the Level review and homework `set.systems.feeds`.

**Level MS2: Rebuilding the book** (`lvl.systems.book`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 186 | `lsn.systems.bookbuild-snapshot-deltas` Snapshot plus deltas | Rebuild a price-level book from a snapshot and a stream of level updates | A delta sets size 0 at a level. What does the book do? (removes the level) | `feed-sim` (book), `price-ladder` | Trap: applying deltas that predate the snapshot. Edge: buffer deltas while the snapshot loads, then apply those after its sequence number | O, C, S, G |
| 187 | `lsn.systems.bookbuild-data-structures` Data structures for a book | Choose a structure (a sorted map, an array indexed by tick, heaps) with the complexity of each operation stated | Prices move within 200 ticks of the mid. Which structure gives constant-time updates? (an array indexed by tick) | `code-cell`, `bench-lab` | Trap: a sorted list with $O(n)$ inserts. Edge: the best price must be found fast after its level empties | S, C, N, M |
| 188 | `lsn.systems.bookbuild-l3` Order-by-order books | Maintain an order map plus price levels through add, modify, cancel and execute messages | An execute message names an order id you never saw. Cause? (a missed message: resynchronise) | `feed-sim` (book), `code-cell` | Trap: treating a modify as cancel plus add for priority. Edge: on most venues a size reduction keeps priority (1.7 lesson 32) | O, C, K, S |
| 189 | `lsn.systems.bookbuild-invariants` Book invariants | Test the invariants (best bid below best ask, no negative sizes, levels sum to their orders) on every update | Your book shows a bid above the ask for 3 milliseconds. Bug or market? (in a single-venue book, usually a bug or a missed message) | `code-cell` (pytest), `price-ladder` | Trap: ignoring a crossed book. Edge: a consolidated book across venues can be crossed briefly for real | K, M, C, S |
| 190 | `lsn.systems.bookbuild-derived` Imbalance and the microprice | Compute queue imbalance $I = \frac{Q_b}{Q_b + Q_a}$ and the microprice $I \times \text{ask} + (1 - I) \times \text{bid}$ | Bid 100.00 for 900, ask 100.02 for 100. Microprice? (100.018) | `order-book` (imbalance) | Trap: the mid is the best estimate of the next trade. Edge: imbalance predicts the next mid move only over very short horizons | N, C, X, S |
| 191 | `lsn.systems.bookbuild-replay` Replaying a recorded day | Record messages to disk and replay them deterministically to test the book builder | A bug appears once a day in production. How do you reproduce it? (replay the recorded messages up to the failure) | `feed-sim` (recovery), `setup-checklist` | Trap: testing only on live data. Edge: replay must use recorded timestamps, not the wall clock | O, C, M, S |

Then the Level review and homework `set.systems.book`.

Why? (unsynchronised clocks) | `latency-lab` (clocks) | Trap: wall-clock time for measuring durations. Edge: NTP gives milliseconds, PTP microseconds [recalled] | C, N, M, S |

**Level MS4: Latency and throughput** (`lvl.systems.latency`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 198 | `lsn.systems.latency-percentiles` Latency percentiles and histograms | Compute p50, p99 and p99.9 from a histogram and explain why the mean misleads | Mean latency 2 milliseconds, p99 40. Which matters to a trader? | `latency-lab` (percentiles) | Trap: reporting the mean. Edge: percentiles of percentiles are not percentiles; keep histograms, not summaries | N, P, C, S |
| 199 | `lsn.systems.latency-coordinated-omission` Coordinated omission | Correct latency measurements for coordinated omission | A service answers in 1 millisecond, except for one 1-second stall during 10 seconds of requests sent every millisecond. Naive p99 against corrected p99? (1 millisecond against about 900) | `latency-lab` (omission) | Trap: a load generator that waits for each reply before sending the next. Edge: the corrected numbers are what a user waiting in the queue experiences | N, C, M, S |
| 200 | `lsn.systems.latency-queues` Queues, utilisation and Little's law | Apply Little's law $L = \lambda W$ and the single-server intuition that waiting grows like $\frac{\rho}{1 - \rho}$ | At 90% utilisation, how long is the average wait in the queue relative to the service time? (9 times) | `latency-lab` (queue), `slider` | Trap: a system at 90% load has 10% headroom. Edge: bursts, not averages, fill queues | N, X, C, P |
| 201 | `lsn.systems.latency-backpressure` Backpressure and bounded queues | Choose a policy when a queue fills (block, drop oldest, conflate to the latest value) for each stage | A candle builder falls behind during a burst. Block the feed, drop ticks, or conflate? | `latency-lab` (queue), `card-sort` | Trap: unbounded queues (memory grows until the process dies). Edge: conflation is safe for quotes but never for trades | C, M, S, O |
| 202 | `lsn.systems.latency-concurrency` Concurrency models | Choose asyncio, threads or processes for input-bound and computation-bound stages, given the global interpreter lock | The feed is input-bound and candle building is light. Threads, asyncio or processes? (asyncio) | `node-link`, `card-sort` | Trap: threads to speed up pure-Python computation. Edge: shared state between threads needs a stated lock (engineering floor) | C, S, S, O |
| 203 | `lsn.systems.latency-profiling` Profiling the hot path | Profile, find the hot path, and remove allocations and repeated parsing from it | 70% of the time is spent creating dictionaries for each message. Fix? | `bench-lab`, `code-cell` | Trap: optimising code that is not hot. Edge: measure p99, not only the mean, before and after | N, C, K, M |

Then the Level review and homework `set.systems.latency`.

(one row) | `code-cell` (sql), `schema-builder` | Trap: "exactly once" assumed from the network. Edge: an update that increments a value is not idempotent; one that sets it is | K, C, M, S |

| 210 | `lsn.systems.perf-memory-layout` Memory layout and the cache | Compare arrays of structures with structures of arrays and contiguous storage for scans | Summing one field of a million records: why are the contiguous arrays faster? (cache lines are filled with useful data) | `bench-lab`, `code-cell` | Trap: Python lists of objects for numeric data. Edge: NumPy arrays are structures of arrays already | C, N, O, S |
| 211 | `lsn.systems.perf-ring-buffers` Ring buffers and single-producer queues | Implement a fixed-size ring buffer with power-of-two capacity and masked indices | Capacity 1,024: where does write number 1,029 go? (slot 5) | `code-cell`, `array-strip` | Trap: overwriting unread data when full. Edge: a single-producer, single-consumer ring needs no lock on most platforms [recalled] | N, K, C, M |
| 212 | `lsn.systems.perf-zero-copy` Parsing without allocation | Parse fixed-width binary messages in place (`struct.unpack_from` on a `memoryview`; in C++, copying into a struct with `memcpy`) | Slicing a byte string for every field allocates. What avoids it? | `code-cell`, `bench-lab` | Trap: casting raw bytes to a struct pointer in C++ (undefined behaviour; copy instead). Edge: alignment and byte order | K, C, S, T |

Edge: the 1.9 exit times the binomial pricer in both languages; this lesson is its method | M, C, N, S |

(parsing and book updates; the network wait) | `node-link`, `card-sort` | Trap: rewriting everything in C++. Edge: AddressSanitizer and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer runs belong in the developer's CI | S, M, S, O |

**Level MS7: Orders and the trading system** (`lvl.systems.orders`, 6 lessons)

(the order manager keeps it; the broker's is the truth to reconcile against) | `order-lifecycle` (architecture), `node-link` | Trap: the strategy talks to the broker directly. Edge: the strategy interface should be the backtester's (BT lesson 83) | S, M, C, O |

What state is it in? (filled or partly filled: the cancel lost the race) | `order-lifecycle` (states) | Trap: assuming a cancel request succeeds. Edge: a reject can arrive after an acknowledgement on some venues | O, G, C, S |

Resend? (Only with the same client order id) | `order-lifecycle` (recovery) | Trap: resending with a new id. What do you do first? (stop trading and investigate) | `order-lifecycle` (reconcile-diff) [mode to add] | Trap: overwriting the broker's position with yours. Which checks would have stopped it sooner? | `order-lifecycle` (risk), `card-sort` | Trap: risk checks inside the strategy (they must sit outside it). Edge: the kill switch must work when everything else has failed | M, S, C, O |

Realistic? (no: compare with BT lesson 60) | `paper-trader` (run), `order-lifecycle` | Trap: paper results as live results. Edge: paper trading tests the plumbing more than the strategy | C, O, N, S |

Then the Level review and homework `set.systems.orders`.

What can go wrong? | `node-link`, `card-sort` | Trap: a fixed local time across daylight-saving changes. Edge: half-days and holidays need the exchange calendar | O, C, M, S |

Edge: an alert nobody acts on trains people to ignore alerts | S, M, S, O |

Where do you look first? | `backtest-lab`, `paper-trader` | Trap: blaming the market. Edge: the backtest of the live period must use the data the bot saw, not revised data | M, C, S, N |

Write the timeline's first three lines | `free-response`, `card-sort` | Trap: blaming a person instead of the process. Edge: the near miss is as valuable to write up as the loss | O, S, C, S |

Ready for real money? (No: the null, the costs and the sample size say not yet) | `paper-trader` (null) | Trap: three good months as evidence. Edge: the honest verdict on most indicator bots is "no edge", and saying so is the project's credibility | C, M, N, S |

---

## 3. Homework blueprint per level

### 3.0 Rules for every set

- **Issue, due and marking** follow `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.5: issued when the level's last lesson is done, due 7 days later, no feedback before submission; code items run their visible examples freely and their hidden tests at submission; long-form items are self-marked against the rubric with Bertie's suggested mark and evidence quote beside the learner's.
- **1.7 sizes.** Levels of 4 or 5 lessons get **8 items: 3 at difficulty 3, 3 at difficulty 4, 2 at difficulty 5**, about 1 hour 30 minutes. Levels of 6 or 7 lessons get **10 items: 4 at difficulty 3, 4 at difficulty 4, 2 at difficulty 5**, about 2 hours.
- **2.3 sizes.** Every non-project level gets **6 items: 2 at difficulty 3, 3 at difficulty 4, 1 at difficulty 5**, about 1 hour 30 minutes, because most items are analyses of a seeded synthetic data set from `market-gen` and take longer than a calculation. The four project levels' homework is their project's milestone pack (section 3.4).
- **Inside those counts**, every set has exactly one long-form item (a derivation, proof or written explanation with a 10-point rubric, usually difficulty 4) and exactly one code drill (difficulty 3 or 4). At least half the items are generator slots, so a redo is a fresh variant. At most two choice items per set.
- **Calculators.** 1.7 and 2.3 items allow the in-app scientific calculator; items marked "mental" ban it and rehearse 3.3's formats.
- **Settings.** Each 1.7 set mixes at least three settings (savings, gilts, US shares, an exchange, a commodity, a currency) so that the set tests the idea, not the story. Each 2.3 set uses a fresh seeded universe.
- **Long-form marking.** Every rubric has 4 to 10 points totalling 10 marks, each naming the model-answer lines that evidence it (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.4). 7 of 10 is the bar wherever an outcome cites a rubric.
- **Drills.** Each drill names its hidden tests and at least one known-wrong solution (`wrong/*.py`) that must fail a named test (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.9). 1.7 drills need the standard library (`math`, `fractions`) and at most NumPy, and run on both targets. 2.3 drills declare `needs`; those needing SciPy, statsmodels or scikit-learn hand off from the Artifact if its build lacks them (decision 21).

Below, each level gives its set id, item mix, long-form item, code drill with its hidden tests, and two example problems in full: example A is the long-form item with its model answer and rubric; example B is a difficulty-5 item with its worked answer and its decision point.

### 3.1 Item 1.7 Finance basics: 14 sets

**RB1 Interest and present value** (`set.rates.time-value`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the annuity formula from two perpetuities. Code drill `drl.fin.amortise` (d3): `payment(principal, rate, n)` and `schedule(principal, rate, n)` returning rows of (period, payment, interest, principal, balance). Hidden tests: `payment(200000, 0.00375, 300)` within $10^{-6}$ of 1111.664956; a zero rate gives `principal / n`; the last balance is within $10^{-6}$ of 0; in every row interest plus principal equals the payment within $10^{-9}$; `n < 1` raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: discounting with $(1 + r)^{n}$ in place of $(1 + r)^{-n}$ fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Derive the present value of an annuity of $C$ a year for $n$ years at rate $r$, first payment in one year, as the difference of two perpetuities. (b) Do the same for payments growing at $g < r$. (c) Check (a) for $C = 100$, $r = 5\%$, $n = 10$.
  *Model answer.* (1) A perpetuity of $C$ from year 1 is worth $\frac Cr$ today (lesson 4's shift: $V = \frac{C + V}{1 + r}$). (2) A perpetuity of $C$ starting in year $n + 1$ is worth $\frac Cr$ at year $n$, so $\frac Cr(1 + r)^{-n}$ today. (3) The annuity is the first perpetuity minus the second, because their payments cancel from year $n + 1$ on: $PV = \frac Cr\left[1 - (1 + r)^{-n}\right]$. (4) Growing: the first perpetuity is worth $\frac C{r - g}$; the second pays $C(1 + g)^n$ in year $n + 1$ and grows at $g$, so is worth $\frac{C(1 + g)^n}{r - g}(1 + r)^{-n}$ today. (5) $PV = \frac C{r - g}\left[1 - \left(\frac{1 + g}{1 + r}\right)^n\right]$. (6) Check: $\frac{100}{0.05}(1 - 1.05^{-10}) = 772.17$, equal to the sum of the ten discounted payments.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the perpetuity value with its reason (line 1). 2: the deferred perpetuity, discounted to today (line 2). 2: the difference and why the tails cancel (line 3), the creative step. 3: the growing case (lines 4 and 5). 1: the check (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A £10,000 loan at 6% a year compounded monthly is repaid over 3 years. After 12 payments the rate resets to 9% a year compounded monthly, keeping the original end date. Find the payments before and after the reset, and the extra paid in total.
  *Answer.* Before: $i = 0.005$, $P_1 = 10{,}000 \times \frac{0.005}{1 - 1.005^{-36}} = £304.22$. Balance after 12 payments: $10{,}000 \times 1.005^{12} - P_1\frac{1.005^{12} - 1}{0.005} = £6{,}864.06$. After: $i = 0.0075$ over the remaining 24 months, $P_2 = 6{,}864.06 \times \frac{0.0075}{1 - 1.0075^{-24}} = £313.58$. Extra: $24 \times (P_2 - P_1) = 24 \times 9.3635 = £224.72$ from the unrounded payments (working from the rounded £313.58 and £304.22 gives £224.64, so the key's tolerance is £0.10). Decision point: the new payment amortises the remaining balance over the remaining term; recomputing on the original £10,000 over 36 months at 9% (£318.00) double-counts the principal already repaid.

**RB2 Bonds and yields** (`set.rates.bonds`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the price-yield curve falls and is convex; the pull to par. Code drill `drl.fin.ytm` (d4): `price(coupon, y, n, face=100, freq=1)` and `ytm(price, coupon, n, face=100, freq=1)` by bisection to $10^{-10}$. Hidden tests: `price(5, 0.04, 3)` within $10^{-6}$ of 102.775091; `ytm(101.5, 6, 2)` within $10^{-8}$ of 0.0519111; a par bond's yield equals its coupon rate at both frequencies; `price(4, 0.04, 10, freq=2) == 100` within $10^{-9}$; a non-positive price raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: omitting the face value from the last cash flow fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For a bond paying $c_t \ge 0$ at times $t = 1, \dots, n$ (not all zero), with price $P(y) = \sum_tc_t(1 + y)^{-t}$: (a) prove $P$ is decreasing and convex in $y$; (b) at a constant yield $y$, show the price one year later (just after a coupon) is $P(1 + y) - c_1$, and deduce that a premium bond's price falls towards 100 and a discount bond's rises.
  *Model answer.* (1) $P'(y) = -\sum_t tc_t(1 + y)^{-t-1} < 0$, since every term is non-negative and at least one is positive. (2) $P''(y) = \sum_tt(t + 1)c_t(1 + y)^{-t-2} > 0$, so $P$ is convex. (3) One year on, the remaining flows are $c_2, \dots, c_n$ at times $1, \dots, n - 1$: their value is $\sum_{t \ge 2}c_t(1 + y)^{-(t-1)} = (1 + y)\left[P - c_1(1 + y)^{-1}\right] = P(1 + y) - c_1$. (4) For a level coupon $c$ and face 100, write $P = 100 + \Delta$. The new price is $100 + \Delta(1 + y) + 100y - c$. (5) At a premium ($c > 100y$, $\Delta > 0$) the change in $\Delta$ is $y\Delta + 100y - c$; since $\Delta$ is the present value of the excess coupons, $c - 100y > y\Delta$, so the premium shrinks. The discount case is symmetric. (6) At maturity $\Delta = 0$: every bond ends at par.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: first derivative and its sign (line 1). 2: second derivative (line 2). 2: the roll-forward identity (line 3). 3: the premium and discount argument (lines 4 and 5). 1: the conclusion at maturity (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A 1-year zero rate is 4% (annual). A 2-year 5% annual-coupon bond trades at 101.50 and a 3-year 6% annual-coupon bond at 103.20. Find the discount factors $d_1, d_2, d_3$, the three zero rates, and the forward rate from year 2 to year 3.
  *Answer.* $d_1 = \frac1{1.04} = 0.961538$. From the 2-year bond: $5d_1 + 105d_2 = 101.5$, so $d_2 = 0.920879$, zero rate $d_2^{-1/2} - 1 = 4.21\%$. From the 3-year bond: $6d_1 + 6d_2 + 106d_3 = 103.2$, so $d_3 = 0.867033$, zero rate $d_3^{-1/3} - 1 = 4.87\%$. Forward: $\frac{d_2}{d_3} - 1 = 6.21\%$. Decision point: each bond's early coupons are discounted with the factors already found, shortest first; discounting the 3-year bond at its own yield gives a single rate that is neither $z_3$ nor the forward.

**RB3 Interest-rate risk and swaps** (`set.rates.risk`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: modified duration from the derivative. Code drill `drl.fin.duration` (d3): `macaulay(cfs, y)`, `modified(cfs, y)`, `dv01(cfs, y)` and `convexity(cfs, y)` for annual cash flows. Hidden tests: a 5-year zero at 4% gives 5, 4.807692 and 0.0395157 (per 100 face); a 3-year 5% par bond at 5% has Macaulay duration 2.859410; each analytic value agrees with a central finite difference of the price within $10^{-6}$; an empty cash-flow list raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: modified duration as Macaulay times $(1 + y)$ fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For annual cash flows $c_t$ and yield $y$, define $D_{\text{mac}} = \frac1P\sum_ttc_t(1 + y)^{-t}$. Show that $-\frac1P\frac{dP}{dy} = \frac{D_{\text{mac}}}{1 + y}$, explain why $D_{\text{mac}}$ is a weighted average time, and give the first-order price change for a small $\Delta y$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\frac{dP}{dy} = -\sum_ttc_t(1 + y)^{-t-1} = -\frac1{1 + y}\sum_ttc_t(1 + y)^{-t}$. (2) Dividing by $-P$: $-\frac1P\frac{dP}{dy} = \frac1{1 + y}\cdot\frac{\sum_ttc_t(1 + y)^{-t}}{P} = \frac{D_{\text{mac}}}{1 + y}$, which is $D_{\text{mod}}$. (3) The weights $w_t = \frac{c_t(1 + y)^{-t}}{P}$ are non-negative and sum to 1, because the present values sum to $P$; $D_{\text{mac}} = \sum_ttw_t$ is the average payment time weighted by present value, the fulcrum of lesson 13. (4) Taylor to first order: $\Delta P \approx \frac{dP}{dy}\Delta y = -D_{\text{mod}}P\Delta y$, and DV01 is $D_{\text{mod}}P \times 0.0001$. (5) The approximation's error is second order, which is the convexity term.
  *Rubric (10).* 3: the derivative with the factor $\frac1{1 + y}$ pulled out (line 1). 2: identifying $D_{\text{mod}}$ (line 2). 2: the weights and why they sum to 1 (line 3). 2: the first-order change and DV01 (line 4). 1: the error is second order (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* A £1m liability is due in 6 years. The curve is flat at 5% (annual). Immunise it with 2-year and 10-year zero-coupon gilts: find the present value to invest in each and their face values, then the surplus if the curve shifts to 4% or to 6% immediately.
  *Answer.* The liability's present value is $\frac{10^6}{1.05^6} = £746{,}215.40$ and its duration 6. Weights $w_2 + w_{10} = 1$ and $2w_2 + 10w_{10} = 6$ give $w_2 = w_{10} = \frac12$: £373,107.70 in each, face values £411,351.24 (2-year) and £607,753.13 (10-year). At 4%: assets £790,893.58 against liability £790,314.53, surplus £579. At 6%: £705,467.31 against £704,960.54, surplus £507. Decision point: matching value and duration makes the first-order change zero; the barbell's greater convexity makes it gain on both parallel shifts, but a twist (short rates up, long rates down) could still lose, which is why immunisation is rebalanced.

**EQ1 Shares and how they pay** (`set.equity.shares`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: buybacks at, above and below fair value. Code drill `drl.fin.adjust` (d4): `adjustment_factors(dates, closes, splits, dividends)` and `total_return(closes, dividends, splits)`. Hidden tests: a 2-for-1 split gives a factor of 0.5 on every earlier date; a dividend of 2 on a previous close of 50 gives a factor of 0.96 on every earlier date; with no events every factor is 1; total return on a seeded series matches the stored value within $10^{-12}$; an ex-date not in the price dates raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: applying the dividend factor from the ex-date onwards fails the second test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A firm worth £100m has 10m shares at their fair value of £10. It buys back 1m shares (a) at £10, (b) at £12. Find the value of each remaining share in each case, explain who gains and who loses, and show that a buyback at fair value leaves holders as well off as a dividend of the same total.
  *Model answer.* (1) At £10 it spends £10m: the firm is worth £90m with 9m shares, £10 each, unchanged. (2) At £12 it spends £12m: worth £88m with 9m shares, £9.78 each. (3) The sellers received £12 for shares worth £10: £2m moved from those who stayed to those who sold. (4) Bought below fair value, the transfer runs the other way, from sellers to stayers. (5) A £10m dividend leaves 10m shares worth £9 each plus £1 of cash each: £10 of wealth per share, the same as case (a) for every holder, whether or not they sell. (6) The two differ in tax and in what they signal about management's view of the price, not in value.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: case (a) (line 1). 2: case (b) (line 2). 2: who gains and why, both directions (lines 3 and 4). 3: the dividend equivalence (line 5). 1: what does differ (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* You buy 400 shares at USD 50 with 50% initial margin (USD 10,000 of your own, USD 10,000 borrowed); maintenance margin is 25%. (a) At what price is the first call? (b) The price falls to 30: how much must you deposit to return to maintenance? (c) Instead the price rises 20% in a year and the loan costs 6%: return on your equity?
  *Answer.* (a) Equity over value is $\frac{400P - 10{,}000}{400P} = 0.25$, so $P = \frac{10{,}000}{0.75 \times 400} = 33.33$. (b) At 30: value 12,000, equity 2,000, required $0.25 \times 12{,}000 = 3{,}000$: deposit 1,000 (or more if the broker restores initial margin: state the rule). (c) Value 24,000; repay 10,000 plus 600 interest; equity 13,400: return 34%, against 20% unlevered. Decision point: the call price comes from the fixed loan and the moving market value, not from the purchase price; a 33% fall, not a 75% fall, triggers it.

**EQ2 Risk, return and the market** (`set.equity.portfolios`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the $n$-asset variance and its floor. Code drill `drl.fin.portfolio` (d4): `portfolio_vol(w, cov)`, `min_var_two(s1, s2, rho)` and `tangency(mu, cov, rf)`. Hidden tests: the tangency weights of example B within $10^{-6}$; its Sharpe ratio 0.430577; equal volatilities give a minimum-variance weight of $\frac12$ for any $\rho < 1$; tangency weights sum to 1; a singular covariance raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: tangency computed from $\mu$ in place of $\mu - r_f$ fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* $n$ assets each have volatility $\sigma$ and every pair has correlation $\rho$. Show that the equally weighted portfolio has variance $\sigma^2\left(\rho + \frac{1 - \rho}n\right)$; interpret the two terms; explain the floor as $n \to \infty$ and why $\rho$ cannot be below $-\frac1{n - 1}$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\mathrm{Var}\left(\frac1n\sum_iR_i\right) = \frac1{n^2}\left[\sum_i\mathrm{Var}(R_i) + \sum_{i \ne j}\mathrm{Cov}(R_i, R_j)\right]$. (2) There are $n$ variances $\sigma^2$ and $n(n - 1)$ covariances $\rho\sigma^2$: total $\frac1{n^2}\left[n\sigma^2 + n(n - 1)\rho\sigma^2\right]$. (3) Simplify: $\sigma^2\left[\frac1n + \frac{n - 1}n\rho\right] = \sigma^2\left(\rho + \frac{1 - \rho}n\right)$. (4) The first term is the systematic risk that every asset shares; the second, idiosyncratic risk, shrinks like $\frac1n$. (5) As $n \to \infty$ the variance tends to $\rho\sigma^2$: volatility $\sigma\sqrt\rho$ cannot be diversified away. (6) A variance cannot be negative: $\rho + \frac{1 - \rho}n \ge 0$ gives $\rho \ge -\frac1{n - 1}$ (1.5 lesson 98's validity check).
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the variance of the average (line 1). 2: counting the terms (line 2). 1: the simplification (line 3). 2: interpretation (line 4). 2: the floor (line 5). 1: the bound on $\rho$ (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Two risky assets: expected returns 8% and 12%, volatilities 15% and 25%, correlation 0.3; cash pays 3%. Find the tangency portfolio's weights, expected return, volatility and Sharpe ratio, and compare with each asset's Sharpe ratio.
  *Answer.* Weights proportional to $\Sigma^{-1}(\mu - r_f)$: normalised, 59.1% and 40.9%. Expected return 9.64%, volatility 15.41%, Sharpe 0.431, above both assets' 0.333 and 0.360. Decision point: the best risky portfolio is not the higher-return asset nor the minimum-variance mix, but the one whose line from cash is steepest; diversification raises the Sharpe ratio above both parts.

**MK1 The limit order book** (`set.markets.order-book`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the exit explanation in writing, marked on the exit rubric. Code drill `drl.fin.match` (d4): `submit(book, order)` with price-time priority, returning fills and the updated book; orders are limit, market or immediate-or-cancel, with integer ticks and lots. Hidden tests: the seven-order sequence of example B gives its fills and final book exactly; a market order against an empty side fills nothing and leaves nothing; an immediate-or-cancel remainder is cancelled; a partly filled resting order keeps its place; quantities stay integers. Known-wrong: filling at the arriving order's limit price fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4; this is the exit's explanation rubric).* Explain to an interviewer what a limit order book is, what the bid-ask spread is and why it exists, and when you would use a market order rather than a limit order.
  *Model answer.* (1) A limit order book is the exchange's list of resting limit orders: bids to buy at stated prices and offers to sell, each with a size. (2) Orders are ranked by price, then by time of arrival; the highest bid and the lowest offer are the best prices. (3) An arriving order that crosses the best opposite price trades immediately, at the resting order's price, against resting orders in priority order, walking up or down the book if it is large. (4) The spread is the gap between the best offer and the best bid; the mid is halfway between. (5) It exists because liquidity providers must be paid for processing costs, for the risk of holding inventory and, mostly, for adverse selection: some of the people who trade with them know more. (6) A market order buys certainty of execution now and pays the half-spread plus any impact from walking the book. (7) A limit order saves the spread but may not fill, and fills most often when the price is moving against it. (8) Use a market order when you must trade now or the order is small relative to the depth; use a limit order when you can wait and are not racing information. (9) A one-sided book or an auction changes all this: there is no spread, or one price clears everything.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the book defined (line 1). 2: price-time priority (line 2). 2: what happens to an arriving order, at which prices (line 3). 1: spread and mid (line 4). 2: why the spread exists, with adverse selection named (line 5). 2: market against limit, the trade-off and a rule for choosing (lines 6 to 8). The edge in line 9 earns no mark but lifts a borderline answer.
- *Example B (d5).* An empty book (tick 0.01) receives, in order: A buy 100 at 10.00; B buy 200 at 10.01; C sell 150 at 10.03; D buy 100 at 10.01; E sell 250 at market; F sell 100 at 10.00; G buy 300 at 10.04. List every fill and the final book.
  *Answer.* E fills 200 against B at 10.01 and 50 against D at 10.01. F crosses the best bid: 50 against D at 10.01, then 50 against A at 10.00. G fills 150 against C at 10.03; its remaining 150 rests as a bid at 10.04. Final book: bids 150 at 10.04 (G) and 50 at 10.00 (A); no offers, so no spread. Decision point: F is a limit sell at 10.00 that trades first at 10.01, the resting bid's better price; a limit price is a worst acceptable price, not the trade price.

**MK2 Auctions, venues and the trade cycle** (`set.markets.venues`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: novation and netting by a central counterparty. Code drill `drl.fin.auction` (d4): `uncross(buys, sells, reference)` maximising executed volume, then minimising the absolute imbalance, then choosing the price nearest the reference, and allocating fills by price then time. Hidden tests: lesson 35's book gives (10.03, 2,000); example B's book gives 20.03 and its allocations; an empty side gives `None`; quantities stay integers.
