Known-wrong: the midpoint of best bid and best offer fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Firms A, B and C trade the same bond. Before clearing, A owes B £10m, B owes C £10m and C owes A £5m. (a) Show the gross and net obligations with and without a central counterparty. (b) Explain novation. (c) Explain what happens if B fails in each case, and why a CCP concentrates risk as well as reducing it.
  *Model answer.* (1) Gross obligations sum to £25m. (2) Net positions: A pays £10m and receives £5m, net $-£5$m; B receives £10m and pays £10m, net 0; C receives £10m and pays £5m, net $+£5$m. (3) Novation: each trade is replaced by two trades with the CCP, so every firm faces only the CCP. (4) With a CCP the obligations net to A paying £5m to the CCP and the CCP paying £5m to C: £10m moves in place of £25m. (5) Without a CCP, if B fails, C loses the £10m B owed it while still owing A £5m; with a CCP B's net position is 0, so nothing is lost, and in general a defaulter's losses fall first on its own margin, then on the default fund. (6) Every firm now depends on the CCP's solvency, so the CCP itself is systemically important, which is why it collects margin and is heavily regulated.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: gross and net (lines 1 and 2). 2: novation defined (line 3). 2: the netted flows (line 4). 2: B's failure in both worlds (line 5). 2: concentration of risk (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Before the open: buy 600 at 20.05, buy 500 at 20.03, buy 400 at 20.02; sell 700 at 20.00, sell 300 at 20.02, sell 800 at 20.04. Find the uncrossing price, the executed volume, each order's fill, and the book left for continuous trading.
  *Answer.* Executable volume by price: 20.00 gives 700; 20.02 gives 1,000 (imbalance +500); 20.03 gives 1,000 (imbalance +100); 20.04 and 20.05 give 600. Volume ties at 20.02 and 20.03; the smaller imbalance chooses 20.03. At 20.03: the sells for 700 and 300 fill completely; the buy at 20.05 fills 600 and the buy at 20.03 fills 400 of 500. Left: bids 100 at 20.03 and 400 at 20.02; offer 800 at 20.04. Decision point: maximum volume alone does not decide the price; the rule set's second test does, and the allocation follows price priority, so the 20.05 buyer fills first and in full.

**MK3 Why spreads exist, and what trading costs** (`set.markets.microstructure`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the Glosten-Milgrom quotes. Code drill `drl.fin.spreads` (d4): `classify(trades, quotes)` by the quote rule with the tick rule as fallback; `effective_spread`, `realised_spread` and `roll(prices)`. Hidden tests: lesson 40's trade gives 0.06, $-0.02$ and 0.08; Roll on a seeded bounce series recovers its spread within 5%; a positive autocovariance returns `None`; a trade at the mid falls back to the tick rule. Known-wrong: Roll with $+\mathrm{Cov}$ under the root returns a number for every series and fails the third test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A share is worth $V_H$ or $V_L$ with equal probability. A fraction $\mu$ of traders are informed and buy if the value is high, sell if low; the rest buy or sell with probability $\frac12$ each. A competitive dealer sets zero-expected-profit quotes. Derive the ask and the bid, show the spread is $\mu(V_H - V_L)$, and explain what happens to the quotes after a trade.
  *Model answer.* (1) Zero expected profit on a sale to a buyer means $a = E[V \mid \text{buy}]$; likewise $b = E[V \mid \text{sell}]$. (2) $P(\text{buy} \mid H) = \mu + \frac{1 - \mu}2 = \frac{1 + \mu}2$ and $P(\text{buy} \mid L) = \frac{1 - \mu}2$. (3) Bayes: $P(H \mid \text{buy}) = \frac{\frac12 \cdot \frac{1 + \mu}2}{\frac12 \cdot \frac{1 + \mu}2 + \frac12 \cdot \frac{1 - \mu}2} = \frac{1 + \mu}2$. (4) So $a = V_L + \frac{1 + \mu}2(V_H - V_L)$ and, by symmetry, $b = V_L + \frac{1 - \mu}2(V_H - V_L)$. (5) $a - b = \mu(V_H - V_L)$: the spread grows with the share of informed traders and with how much there is to know. (6) After a trade the dealer's prior moves to the posterior, and the next quotes are computed from it, so quotes drift towards the true value as trades reveal it.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the zero-profit conditions (line 1). 2: the likelihoods (line 2). 2: Bayes' rule (line 3). 2: the quotes and the spread (lines 4 and 5). 2: updating after a trade (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* With $V \in \{100, 110\}$ equally likely and $\mu = 0.3$, a buy arrives, then a sell. Find the quotes before the first trade, after the buy, and after the sell.
  *Answer.* Before: bid 103.5, ask 106.5. After the buy the prior is $P(H) = 0.65$; with likelihoods 0.65 and 0.35, $P(H \mid \text{buy}) = \frac{0.65 \times 0.65}{0.65 \times 0.65 + 0.35 \times 0.35} = 0.775$ and $P(H \mid \text{sell}) = 0.5$: ask 107.75, bid 105.0. After the sell the prior returns to 0.5 and the quotes to 103.5 and 106.5. Decision point: each quote uses the prior after all trades so far; the buy and the sell cancel exactly here because they carry equal evidence in opposite directions.

**DV1 Forwards and futures** (`set.derivatives.forwards-futures`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: $F_0 = S_0e^{(r - \delta)T}$ by arbitrage in both directions. Code drill `drl.fin.margin` (d3): `margin_account(entry, settlements, size, initial, maintenance, side)` returning daily balances and call amounts, topping up to initial margin. Hidden tests: lesson 50's path gives one call of 3,000 after day 2 and a final balance of 11,500; a path that never breaches gives no calls; a short position's balance moves the other way; size and margins must be positive. Known-wrong: topping up only to maintenance fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* An asset pays a continuous yield $\delta$ (dividends reinvested in the asset); the continuous risk-free rate is $r$. Show that no arbitrage requires $F_0 = S_0e^{(r - \delta)T}$, giving the arbitrage if $F_0$ is above and if it is below.
  *Model answer.* (1) Buying $e^{-\delta T}$ units today and reinvesting the yield grows to exactly 1 unit at $T$. (2) If $F_0 > S_0e^{(r - \delta)T}$: borrow $S_0e^{-\delta T}$, buy $e^{-\delta T}$ units, sell one forward. At $T$ deliver the unit for $F_0$ and repay $S_0e^{(r - \delta)T}$: profit $F_0 - S_0e^{(r - \delta)T} > 0$ with no outlay. (3) If $F_0 < S_0e^{(r - \delta)T}$: short $e^{-\delta T}$ units (paying the yield to the lender, which leaves 1 unit owed at $T$), invest the $S_0e^{-\delta T}$ proceeds, buy one forward. At $T$ pay $F_0$ for the unit, return it, collect $S_0e^{(r - \delta)T}$: profit $S_0e^{(r - \delta)T} - F_0 > 0$. (4) Both inequalities fail, so equality holds. (5) The second trade needs the asset to be shortable; where it is not (some commodities), only the upper bound holds, and a convenience yield fills the gap.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the tailing of the holding (line 1). 3: the cash-and-carry trade (line 2). 3: the reverse trade (line 3). 1: conclusion (line 4). 1: the shorting condition (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* Sterling is at 1.2500 dollars. One-year money-market rates (simple, annual) are 4% in dollars and 5% in sterling. A bank quotes a one-year forward at 1.2450 dollars per pound. Find the fair forward and the arbitrage profit per £1m.
  *Answer.* Fair forward: $1.25 \times \frac{1.04}{1.05} = 1.2381$. The quote is too high, so sell pounds forward: borrow USD 1.25m at 4% (owe 1.3m), buy £1m spot, invest at 5% to get £1.05m, sell it forward at 1.2450 for USD 1,307,250. Profit USD 7,250. Decision point: the pound's forward is too expensive, so the trade sells pounds forward and must therefore own pounds at expiry: borrow dollars, not pounds. Note the convention chip: these rates are simple, as money markets quote them.

**DV2 Option payoffs and strategies** (`set.derivatives.payoffs`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: every piecewise-linear payoff is a sum of calls. Code drill `drl.fin.payoff` (d3): `payoff(legs, S)` and `decompose(kinks, slopes)` returning call quantities, with exact `Fraction`s. Hidden tests: example B's payoff decomposes to quantities 2, $-2$, $-1$, 1 at 80, 90, 110, 130; a straddle's payoff at its strike is 0; payoffs at kinks are exact; unsorted kinks raise `ValueError`. Known-wrong: quantities equal to the slopes rather than the changes in slope fail the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $f$ be continuous and piecewise linear with kinks at $K_1 < \dots < K_m$, $f(S) = 0$ and slope 0 for $S < K_1$. Prove that $f(S) = \sum_jq_j(S - K_j)^+$, where $q_j$ is the change in slope at $K_j$, and say what to add if $f$ has a value or a slope below $K_1$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Let $g(S) = \sum_jq_j(S - K_j)^+$. Each $(S - K_j)^+$ is continuous, zero to the left of $K_j$ and of slope 1 to its right. (2) Below $K_1$, $g = 0 = f$. (3) Between kinks, $g$ is linear with slope $\sum_{K_j < S}q_j$, which by the definition of $q_j$ equals $f$'s slope there. (4) Two continuous functions that agree at $K_1$ and have the same slope on every interval agree everywhere. (5) If $f(S) = a + bS$ below $K_1$, add a bond paying $a$ and $b$ forwards (or $b$ shares). (6) So any such payoff is priced from bond, forward and call prices alone, with no model.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the building block's shape (line 1). 1: agreement below $K_1$ (line 2). 3: slopes agree on each interval (line 3), the key step. 2: the uniqueness argument (line 4). 1: bond and forward terms (line 5). 1: the pricing consequence (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A payoff is 0 below 80, rises with slope 2 to 20 at 90, is flat at 20 until 110, falls with slope 1 to 0 at 130, and is 0 after. Calls (same expiry) cost 21.5 at 80, 13.4 at 90, 3.1 at 110 and 0.6 at 130. Replicate the payoff and price it.
  *Answer.* Slopes 0, 2, 0, $-1$, 0 change by $+2$, $-2$, $-1$, $+1$ at 80, 90, 110, 130: long 2 calls at 80, short 2 at 90, short 1 at 110, long 1 at 130. Price $2(21.5) - 2(13.4) - 3.1 + 0.6 = 13.7$. Check the quotes are arbitrage-free: call slopes between strikes are $-0.81$, $-0.515$ and $-0.125$, rising, so the prices are convex and decreasing. Decision point: the quantities are slope changes; matching the payoff's values at a few prices picks the wrong legs.

**DV3 No-arbitrage bounds and put-call parity** (`set.derivatives.parity`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: parity with known dividends, and the American inequalities. Code drill `drl.fin.arbitrage-check` (d4): `violations(chain, S, r, T, tol)` checking parity, the bounds, monotonicity, vertical spreads and butterflies, returning (kind, strikes, size). Hidden tests: example B's chain gives exactly one parity violation, at 105, of 0.729; a clean chain gives an empty list; a planted butterfly violation is found; the tolerance suppresses a violation smaller than it. Known-wrong: parity with an undiscounted strike reports violations at every strike.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A share pays known dividends with present value $D$ before $T$. (a) Derive $C - P = S_0 - D - Ke^{-rT}$ for European options. (b) For American options, prove $S_0 - D - K \le C - P \le S_0 - Ke^{-rT}$ (the first inequality assuming the American put may be exercised early).
  *Model answer.* (1) European: portfolio A is a call plus cash $D + Ke^{-rT}$; portfolio B is a put plus one share. (2) At $T$ both are worth $\max(S_T, K)$ plus the dividends' value carried to $T$ (A's cash $D$ grows to it; B receives the dividends), so their prices agree: $C + D + Ke^{-rT} = P + S_0$. (3) American upper bound: hold an American put plus a share (portfolio X) against an American call plus $Ke^{-rT}$ in bonds (portfolio Y). If Y's call is exercised at $\tau$, Y holds $S_\tau - K + Ke^{-r(T - \tau)} \le S_\tau$, while X, holding its put unexercised, is worth at least $S_\tau$. If it is never exercised, at $T$ Y is worth $\max(S_T, K)$ and X at least $\max(S_T, K)$ plus dividends. So $P + S_0 \ge C + Ke^{-rT}$. (4) American lower bound: an American call plus $D + K$ in cash (portfolio U) against an American put plus a share (portfolio W). If W's put is exercised at $\tau$, W holds $K$ plus the dividends received so far, while U's cash alone has grown past $D + K$. If never, at $T$ W is worth $\max(S_T, K)$ plus the dividends grown, and U at least $\max(S_T - K, 0) + K + De^{rT}$, which is as much. So $C + D + K \ge P + S_0$. (5) Together: $S_0 - D - K \le C - P \le S_0 - Ke^{-rT}$. (6) The band's width, $K(1 - e^{-rT}) + D$, is why apparent parity violations on American quotes must be tested against the band, not the line.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the two European portfolios (line 1). 2: equal payoffs and the identity (line 2). 2: the upper bound, with both exercise cases (line 3). 2: the lower bound, with both cases (line 4). 1: assembly (line 5). 1: the consequence (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A share at 100 pays no dividends; $r = 4\%$ continuous; European options expire in 6 months. Mid quotes: strike 95, call 8.10, put 1.22; strike 100, call 5.10, put 3.12; strike 105, call 2.75, put 6.40. Find any arbitrage, the trade, and its profit today. Is it still there if each quote is 0.05 either side of the mid?
  *Answer.* $S_0 - Ke^{-rT}$ is 6.881, 1.980 and $-2.921$; $C - P$ is 6.88, 1.98 and $-3.65$. Strike 105 is off by 0.729: the put is too dear relative to the call. Sell the 105 put, buy the 105 call, short the share, lend $105e^{-0.02} = 102.921$: cash today $6.40 - 2.75 + 100 - 102.921 = 0.729$, and the positions cancel at expiry. With half-spreads of 0.05 on each option the profit is $0.729 - 0.10 = 0.629$, still positive (before the share's own spread and borrow). Decision point: test every strike and both directions; the call and put spreads (convexity, verticals) are clean here, so the error is in one put, and the parity check is what finds it.

**OP1 Binomial trees** (`set.pricing.binomial`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: risk-neutral probability and the no-arbitrage condition. Code drill `drl.fin.binomial-american` (d4): `american_put(S0, K, u, d, growth, n)` and `crr_american(S0, K, r, sigma, T, n, kind)`. Hidden tests: lesson 69's tree gives 4.925652 within $10^{-6}$; an American call on a share without dividends equals the European call; the American put is never below the European; with $n = 1$ the price equals the replicating portfolio's; $d \ge$ growth or growth $\ge u$ raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: omitting the early-exercise comparison returns the European 4.190111 and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* In a one-step tree the share moves from $S_0$ to $uS_0$ or $dS_0$, and cash grows by $R = e^{r\Delta t}$. (a) Find the replicating portfolio of a claim paying $V_u$ or $V_d$. (b) Show its cost is $\frac1R[qV_u + (1 - q)V_d]$ with $q = \frac{R - d}{u - d}$, and that the real probability of an up move appears nowhere. (c) Show no arbitrage requires $d < R < u$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Hold $\Delta$ shares and $B$ in cash: $\Delta uS_0 + BR = V_u$ and $\Delta dS_0 + BR = V_d$. (2) Subtracting: $\Delta = \frac{V_u - V_d}{(u - d)S_0}$; then $B = \frac{uV_d - dV_u}{R(u - d)}$. (3) Cost $\Delta S_0 + B = \frac{V_u - V_d}{u - d} + \frac{uV_d - dV_u}{R(u - d)} = \frac1R\left[\frac{R - d}{u - d}V_u + \frac{u - R}{u - d}V_d\right]$. (4) The weights are $q$ and $1 - q$; nothing in the derivation used $P(\text{up})$. (5) If $R \ge u$, borrowing the share and lending the cash earns at least the share's best outcome: an arbitrage; if $R \le d$, borrowing cash to buy the share is one. (6) So $d < R < u$, which is exactly $0 < q < 1$: $q$ behaves as a probability.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the two equations (line 1). 2: solving for $\Delta$ and $B$ (line 2). 2: the cost rearranged into $q$-weights (line 3). 1: the real probability absent (line 4). 2: both arbitrages (line 5). 1: the link to $0 < q < 1$ (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* An American put: share 50, strike 52, $u = 1.2$, $d = 0.85$, growth $e^{0.02}$ per step, two steps. Price it, mark where it is exercised, and compare with the European put.
  *Answer.* $q = \frac{e^{0.02} - 0.85}{0.35} = 0.4863$. Leaves: 72, 51, 36.125 with payoffs 0, 1, 15.875. Up node (60): continuation $e^{-0.02}(0.4863 \times 0 + 0.5137 \times 1) = 0.504$, exercise $-8$: hold. Down node (42.5): continuation $e^{-0.02}(0.4863 \times 1 + 0.5137 \times 15.875) = 8.470$, exercise $9.5$: exercise. Root: continuation $e^{-0.02}(0.4863 \times 0.504 + 0.5137 \times 9.5) = 5.024$, exercise 2: hold. American 5.024, European 4.505. Decision point: at the down node the exercise value replaces the continuation value before stepping back; carrying 8.470 back gives the European price.

**OP2 Black-Scholes** (`set.pricing.black-scholes`). 8 items (3 d3, 3 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: $N(d_2)$ is the risk-neutral probability of exercise. Code drill `drl.fin.black-scholes` (d3): `bs_price(S, K, r, sigma, T, kind, div=0.0)` using `math.erf`. Hidden tests: lesson 72's call within $10^{-4}$ of 10.4506; put-call parity within $10^{-12}$; as $T \to 0$ the price tends to intrinsic value; $\sigma \le 0$ or $T < 0$ raises `ValueError`; a dividend yield lowers the call. Known-wrong: $d_2 = d_1 + \sigma\sqrt T$ fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Under the risk-neutral model $S_T = S_0\exp\left[(r - \frac12\sigma^2)T + \sigma\sqrt TZ\right]$ with $Z \sim \mathcal N(0, 1)$, show that $P(S_T > K) = N(d_2)$ and interpret the cash-or-nothing term of the Black-Scholes formula.
  *Model answer.* (1) $S_T > K \iff (r - \frac12\sigma^2)T + \sigma\sqrt TZ > \ln\frac K{S_0}$. (2) $\iff Z > \frac{\ln(K/S_0) - (r - \frac12\sigma^2)T}{\sigma\sqrt T} = -d_2$, where $d_2 = \frac{\ln(S_0/K) + (r - \frac12\sigma^2)T}{\sigma\sqrt T}$. (3) $P(Z > -d_2) = N(d_2)$ by symmetry of the normal. (4) A claim paying $K$ if $S_T > K$ is worth $e^{-rT}KN(d_2)$: the discounted risk-neutral expectation. (5) The call is long an asset-or-nothing claim and short this cash-or-nothing claim, so $Ke^{-rT}N(d_2)$ is what the holder expects to pay, in today's money. (6) $N(d_2)$ is a risk-neutral probability; the real-world chance uses $\mu$ in place of $r$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the event rewritten (line 1). 3: standardising to $Z > -d_2$ (line 2). 1: symmetry (line 3). 2: the digital's price (line 4). 1: the interpretation (line 5). 1: risk-neutral, not real (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Share 100, strike 105, $r = 3\%$, $\sigma = 25\%$, $T = 0.5$. Price a digital call paying 1 if $S_T > 105$, and check the answer against $-\frac{\partial C}{\partial K}$ estimated from two call prices.
  *Answer.* $d_1 = -0.1028$, $d_2 = -0.2795$, $N(d_2) = 0.3899$; digital $= e^{-0.015} \times 0.3899 = 0.3841$. Central difference of Black-Scholes calls at strikes 104.99 and 105.01, divided by $-0.02$: 0.3841. Decision point: a tight call spread is a digital; the identity $-\frac{\partial C}{\partial K} = e^{-rT}P(S_T > K)$ is how desks price and hedge digitals from vanillas, and why a digital near expiry is dangerous to hedge.

**OP3 Greeks, hedging and implied volatility** (`set.pricing.greeks`). 10 items (4 d3, 4 d4, 2 d5). Long-form: the delta-hedged P&L. Code drill `drl.fin.implied-vol` (d4): `implied_vol(price, S, K, r, T, kind)` combining bisection with Newton steps. Hidden tests: lesson 79's call gives 0.200867 within $10^{-6}$; round trips across a grid of strikes and expiries agree within $10^{-8}$; a price below intrinsic returns `None`; the deep out-of-the-money case of lesson 142 converges; at most 100 iterations. Known-wrong: Newton alone from 10% overflows on that case.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* With $r = 0$, a trader is long one option, short $\Delta$ shares, rebalancing over a short step $\Delta t$ in which the share moves $\Delta S$. Show the hedged P&L is about $\frac12\Gamma S^2\left[\left(\frac{\Delta S}S\right)^2 - \sigma^2\Delta t\right]$, where $\sigma$ is the implied volatility, and interpret it.
  *Model answer.* (1) Taylor: $\Delta V \approx \Theta\Delta t + \Delta\cdot\Delta S + \frac12\Gamma(\Delta S)^2$. (2) The short shares lose $\Delta\cdot\Delta S$, so the hedged P&L is $\Theta\Delta t + \frac12\Gamma(\Delta S)^2$. (3) With $r = 0$ the Black-Scholes equation gives $\Theta = -\frac12\sigma^2S^2\Gamma$. (4) Substituting: P&L $\approx \frac12\Gamma S^2\left[\left(\frac{\Delta S}S\right)^2 - \sigma^2\Delta t\right]$. (5) The square of the realised return is the realised variance over the step; the trade makes money when realised variance beats implied, whatever the direction of the move. (6) Summed over the option's life, the P&L is a gamma-weighted difference of realised and implied variance, so it depends on where the share was when the moves happened.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the Taylor expansion (line 1). 1: the hedge cancels delta (line 2). 3: the theta relation from the equation (line 3). 2: the result (line 4). 1: interpretation (line 5). 1: path dependence (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* You are long a 30-day at-the-money straddle (share and strike 100, implied volatility 20%, $r = 0$). Overnight the share rises to 103 and implied volatility to 21%. Using the Greeks at the start (delta 0.0229, gamma 0.1391 per USD, vega 22.87 per unit of volatility, theta $-0.0762$ a day), predict the P&L, then compare with full repricing.
  *Answer.* Delta: $0.0229 \times 3 = 0.069$. Gamma: $\frac12 \times 0.1391 \times 9 = 0.626$. Theta: $-0.076$. Vega: $22.87 \times 0.01 = 0.229$. Predicted $+0.847$. Repricing: 4.5743 to 5.3783, $+0.804$. Residual $-0.043$. Decision point: the residual is not an error; a USD 3 move is large for a 30-day option, and the third-order and cross terms (gamma falling as the share leaves the strike, vega changing with spot) are what the residual measures.

### 3.2 Item 1.7: drills beyond homework, and the Pricing Lab project

**Extra drills** (30 minutes each, outside the sets): `drl.fin.npv-irr` (cash-flow NPV and IRR with multiple-root detection), `drl.fin.bootstrap` (discount factors from a strip of par bonds) and `drl.fin.greeks` (closed-form Greeks against central differences).

**The exit drill** `drl.fin.exit-binomial` (section 4.1.3).

**The Pricing Lab** (`prj.fin.pricing-lab`, 10 h). A small Python package in the learner's repository, under the 1.2 rules (tests, CI): `tvm.py` (present value, annuities, IRR), `bonds.py` (price, yield, duration, convexity, bootstrap), `forwards.py` (cost of carry), `trees.py` (European and American CRR pricers), `bs.py` (prices and Greeks) and `iv.py` (implied volatility), with a `pytest` suite that checks each module against the closed forms, against each other (trees against Black-Scholes, parity in every tree) and against 10 golden values published with the starter suite. It is threat class A (no network, no secrets). Milestones: M1 repository and CI (paste-verify `pytest -q`, 1 h); M2 rates and bonds with tests (3 h); M3 trees with tests (3 h); M4 Black-Scholes, Greeks and implied volatility with tests (2 h); M5 a README in two registers and a one-paragraph note of what the tests found (1 h). The binomial module is what 1.9's exit rewrites in C++, and the whole package is what project 3 (OA) starts from.

### 3.3 Item 2.3 Projects: 37 sets

Each set has 6 items (2 d3, 3 d4, 1 d5): one long-form item, one code drill, and four analysis items on a seeded `market-gen` data set whose true parameters the checker knows. Drills are numbered `drl.proj.*`; their `needs` line says which runtime they require.

#### Course RM

**RM1 Questions and data** (`set.method.questions-data`). Long-form: a pre-registration. Code drill `drl.proj.quality-checks` (d3, needs pandas): `quality_report(df, calendar)` flags runs of 5 or more identical closes, absolute daily returns above 50%, duplicate timestamps and dates missing from the calendar. Hidden tests: a seeded messy CSV with eight planted defects is flagged exactly; a clean file returns an empty report; the report never modifies the input. Known-wrong: `dropna()` before checking hides two of the defects.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Write a pre-registration for "one-week reversal in US large caps".
  *Model answer.* (1) Hypothesis: shares with the lowest returns over the past five trading days outperform those with the highest over the next five, because liquidity providers are paid to absorb temporary order imbalances. (2) Falsifier: a long-short spread whose $t$-statistic is below 2 after costs over the test period, or one that exists only in the cheapest-to-trade decile. (3) Universe: the 500 largest US shares by market capitalisation on each rebalancing date, from point-in-time membership, price above 5 dollars. (4) Signal: minus the 5-day total return, lagged one day; decile portfolios, equal-weighted and value-weighted. (5) Horizon and rebalancing: weekly, positions held five days. (6) Metrics: mean spread and $t$, rank IC and its decay, turnover, Sharpe after costs at 2, 5 and 10 basis points per side, deflated Sharpe ratio with every variant logged. (7) Data: dates of the training period (for example 2000 to 2015) and the held-out period (2016 onwards), opened once. (8) Decision rule: report the held-out result whatever it is; call the hypothesis supported only if the held-out net Sharpe is above 0 with $t > 2$. (9) Variants allowed in advance: 3-day and 10-day windows; anything else is logged as a new trial.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: hypothesis with a mechanism (line 1). 1: a falsifier (line 2). 1: point-in-time universe (line 3). 1: exact signal with the lag (line 4). 1: horizon (line 5). 1: metrics including costs and trial count (line 6). 2: the held-out period fixed in advance and the decision rule (lines 7 and 8). 1: the variants declared (line 9).
- *Example B (d5).* Ten shares are held equally weighted for one year. Eight survive with an average return of 12%; one goes bankrupt (last return $-100\%$) and one is taken over at a 30% gain, both delisting during the year. Your free data has only the eight survivors. Compute the return you would report and the true return, and say what a survivor-only universe does to a momentum backtest.
  *Answer.* Survivors only: 12%. True: $\frac{8 \times 12 - 100 + 30}{10} = 2.6\%$. Bias: 9.4 points. In a momentum backtest the losers most likely to delist are in the short leg, so dropping them removes the short leg's best trades (it looks worse) and inflates any long-only version. Decision point: a delisted share's final return is data, not missing data; setting it to zero or dropping it are both wrong.

**RM2 Measuring performance** (`set.method.performance`). Long-form: why maximum drawdown grows with sample length. Code drill `drl.proj.metrics` (d3, needs NumPy): `sharpe`, `sortino`, `max_drawdown`, `calmar` and `turnover(weights)`. Hidden tests: fixed series with stored answers; a constant series raises on Sharpe; Sortino uses the full-sample downside deviation; turnover counts both sides. Known-wrong: a drawdown measured from the first value rather than the running peak fails the third test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Explain why the maximum drawdown of a strategy with a fixed true Sharpe ratio grows with the length of the record, and what that means for comparing Calmar ratios.
  *Model answer.* (1) Maximum drawdown is the largest fall from a running peak, a maximum over all pairs of dates. (2) A longer record contains more pairs, and more chances of an unlucky run, so its maximum can only stay the same or grow when data are added. (3) For a random walk with drift the expected maximum drawdown rises with the record's length, roughly like its square root for short records, more slowly once the drift dominates. (4) So a 15-year record shows a deeper worst drawdown than a 3-year record of the same strategy. (5) Calmar divides return by maximum drawdown, so it falls with length too; comparing Calmar ratios across records of different lengths compares lengths, not strategies. (6) Compare on equal windows, or compare the distribution of drawdowns from simulation at the same length.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: definition (line 1). 2: monotone in length (line 2). 2: the growth rate (line 3). 1: the example (line 4). 2: the Calmar consequence (line 5). 2: the fix (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A strategy returns 11% a year with 18% volatility; the market returns 8% with 16% volatility; their correlation is 0.6; cash pays 3%. Find beta, alpha, residual volatility and the appraisal ratio, and compare with the Sharpe ratio.
  *Answer.* $\beta = 0.6 \times \frac{18}{16} = 0.675$. Alpha $= 11 - [3 + 0.675 \times 5] = 4.625\%$. Residual volatility $18\sqrt{1 - 0.36} = 14.4\%$. Appraisal ratio $\frac{4.625}{14.4} = 0.32$. Sharpe $\frac{8}{18} = 0.44$. Decision point: the Sharpe ratio counts the market exposure the investor could buy cheaply; the appraisal ratio measures only what the strategy adds.

**RM3 Is it real?** (`set.method.inference`). Long-form: the Sharpe ratio's standard error and autocorrelation. Code drill `drl.proj.block-bootstrap` (d4, needs NumPy): `sharpe_ci(returns, block_mean, n_boot, seed)` by the stationary bootstrap. Hidden tests: the same seed gives the same interval; on IID normal data the interval's width is within 15% of the analytic one; on an AR(1) series with coefficient 0.4 it is wider than the plain bootstrap's; a block mean below 1 raises. Known-wrong: resampling single days passes the IID test and fails the AR(1) test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For IID normal returns with mean $\mu$ and standard deviation $\sigma$, derive $\mathrm{SE}(\widehat{SR}) \approx \sqrt{(1 + \frac12SR^2)/T}$ by the delta method, and explain what positive autocorrelation does to the annualised Sharpe ratio and its standard error.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\widehat{SR} = g(\hat\mu, \hat\sigma^2) = \frac{\hat\mu}{\hat\sigma}$. (2) For normal data $\mathrm{Var}(\hat\mu) \approx \frac{\sigma^2}T$, $\mathrm{Var}(\hat\sigma^2) \approx \frac{2\sigma^4}T$, and the two are uncorrelated. (3) Gradient: $\frac{\partial g}{\partial\mu} = \frac1\sigma$, $\frac{\partial g}{\partial\sigma^2} = -\frac\mu{2\sigma^3}$. (4) Delta method: $\mathrm{Var}(\widehat{SR}) \approx \frac1{\sigma^2}\cdot\frac{\sigma^2}T + \frac{\mu^2}{4\sigma^6}\cdot\frac{2\sigma^4}T = \frac{1 + \frac12SR^2}T$. (5) With positive autocorrelation the variance of a $q$-period sum exceeds $q$ times the one-period variance, so multiplying by $\sqrt q$ overstates the annual Sharpe ratio (Lo's $\eta(q)$ corrects it). (6) The effective number of independent observations is below $T$, so the standard error above is too small; HAC or block-bootstrap errors are needed.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the function (line 1). 2: the sampling variances (line 2). 1: the gradient (line 3). 2: assembly (line 4). 2: annualisation (line 5). 2: the standard error under dependence (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A strategy has annual Sharpe 0.8 from daily data, with skewness $-1$ and kurtosis 8. How many years are needed to be 95% confident (one-sided) that the true Sharpe exceeds 0? Compare with normal returns.
  *Answer.* Per day $\widehat{SR} = \frac{0.8}{\sqrt{252}} = 0.0504$. Minimum track record: $1 + [1 - \gamma_3\widehat{SR} + \frac{\gamma_4 - 1}4\widehat{SR}^2]\left(\frac{1.645}{\widehat{SR}}\right)^2 = 1{,}125$ days, 4.46 years; for normal returns ($\gamma_3 = 0$, $\gamma_4 = 3$) 1,068 days, 4.24 years. Decision point: per-day units throughout; at a daily Sharpe of 0.05 the skew and kurtosis terms are small, so the penalty is modest here; it becomes large for strategies whose per-period Sharpe is large, such as monthly-rebalanced ones.

**RM4 Many trials, one winner** (`set.method.selection`). Long-form: why the deflated Sharpe ratio needs every trial. Code drill `drl.proj.deflated-sharpe` (d4, needs NumPy and the standard library's `statistics.NormalDist`): `expected_max_sr(n_trials, var_trials)` and `dsr(sr, T, skew, kurt, n_trials, var_trials)`, per-period units. Hidden tests: lesson 21's inputs give 0.7592 within $10^{-3}$; with one trial the DSR equals the PSR against 0; the DSR falls as the number of trials rises; inputs in annual units are rejected by a units flag. Known-wrong: a hurdle without the Euler-Mascheroni term overstates the DSR and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Explain what the deflated Sharpe ratio corrects, why $N$ must count every trial (including those only glanced at), and why the variance of the trials' Sharpe ratios enters.
  *Model answer.* (1) A reported Sharpe ratio is usually the best of many; the best of $N$ noise strategies has an expected Sharpe ratio well above 0. (2) The DSR asks whether the observed Sharpe ratio beats that expected maximum, given its own standard error (with skewness and kurtosis). (3) The expected maximum grows with $N$, so leaving out trials lowers the hurdle and overstates significance. (4) Trials one looked at and discarded were selected against just as surely as those run in full, so they count. (5) The hurdle is the expected maximum in units of the trials' spread: trials that vary wildly produce a higher best by chance, so the variance across trials scales the hurdle. (6) Correlated trials count for less: the effective $N$ comes from clustering them (lesson 19).
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the selection problem (line 1). 2: the test (line 2). 2: dependence on $N$ (line 3). 1: discarded trials (line 4). 2: the variance term (line 5). 1: effective $N$ (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Four strategies' mean returns in four time blocks are: block 1: 0.6, 0.1, 0.2, 0.0; block 2: 0.5, 0.3, 0.0, 0.1; block 3: $-0.4$, 0.2, 0.3, 0.2; block 4: $-0.5$, 0.4, 0.1, 0.1. Compute the probability of backtest overfitting by CSCV.
  *Answer.* Six ways to choose two blocks as in-sample. For each, the in-sample best strategy and its out-of-sample rank among four (1 worst, 4 best), $\omega = \frac{\text{rank}}5$ and $\lambda = \ln\frac\omega{1 - \omega}$: blocks {1, 2}: strategy 1, rank 1, $\lambda = -1.39$; {1, 3}: strategy 3, rank 2, $-0.41$; {1, 4}: strategy 2, rank 4, $+1.39$; {2, 3}: strategy 2, rank 4, $+1.39$; {2, 4}: strategy 2, rank 3, $+0.41$; {3, 4}: strategy 2, rank 3, $+0.41$. PBO is the share with $\lambda < 0$: $\frac26 = 0.33$. No split has a tie in the in-sample winner's out-of-sample rank, so no tie-breaking rule is needed. Decision point: strategy 1 looks best on the early blocks and worst later, which is what PBO detects; a single train-test split on blocks 1 and 2 would have chosen it.

**RM5 Validation through time** (`set.method.validation`). Long-form: purging and embargo. Code drill `drl.proj.purged-kfold` (d4, needs NumPy): `purged_splits(t_start, t_end, n_folds, embargo)` yielding train and test indices, where each sample's label spans `t_start` to `t_end`. Hidden tests: no training label interval overlaps any test interval; nothing within the embargo after a test fold trains; every sample is tested exactly once; zero embargo reduces to purging only. Known-wrong: purging only samples whose start lies in the test fold fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Labels are 20-day forward returns. Explain why ordinary time-ordered folds still leak, what purging removes, what the embargo adds, and how long each should be.
  *Model answer.* (1) A sample at day $t$ has a label built from days $t + 1$ to $t + 20$. (2) A training sample at day 495 has a label that uses days 496 to 515; if the test fold is days 500 to 600, that label overlaps test returns, so the model is trained partly on the test period. (3) Purging removes every training sample whose label interval overlaps the test fold's label intervals: here days 480 to 499 before the fold, and, if training data follow the fold, samples whose labels start inside it. (4) Features also have memory (rolling windows, serial correlation), so the samples just after a test fold resemble it; the embargo drops a band after the fold. (5) Purge length is set by the label horizon (20 days here); embargo length by the dependence of features and returns, often a small percentage of the sample. (6) Without both, cross-validated scores are optimistic, by an amount that grows with the label horizon.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: label spans (line 1). 2: the leak illustrated (line 2). 3: purging defined and applied (line 3). 2: the embargo and why (line 4). 1: lengths (line 5). 1: consequence (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Ten years of data are split into 10 groups for combinatorial purged cross-validation with 2 groups tested at a time. How many splits, how many complete backtest paths, and how many years long is each path? Compare with walk-forward on the same data.
  *Answer.* Splits $\binom{10}2 = 45$. Each group is tested in $\binom91 = 9$ splits, so there are $\frac{45 \times 2}{10} = 9$ paths, each covering all 10 years. Walk-forward with a 3-year initial window gives one path of 7 years. Decision point: CPCV gives a distribution of out-of-sample Sharpe ratios (nine paths), but the paths share data, so their spread understates true uncertainty; report them as a distribution, never as nine independent tests.

**RM6 Reproducible research engineering** (`set.method.reproducible`). Long-form: the "Reproducing these results" section of a README. Code drill `drl.proj.manifest` (d3, standard library): `build_manifest(paths)` returning SHA-256 hashes and sizes, and `verify(manifest, root)` returning the changed files. Hidden tests: hashes match stored values; changing one byte is detected; a missing file is reported, not raised; the manifest is written in sorted order. Known-wrong: hashing file names instead of contents passes the first test and fails the second.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Write the "Reproducing these results" section for a backtest repository.
  *Model answer.* (1) Environment: the lockfile and the one command that installs it (`uv sync` or `pip install -r requirements.lock`), with the Python version. (2) Data: the download script, the sources and dates, and the manifest of SHA-256 hashes the script checks; no raw data in the repository. (3) Configuration: the config file that produced the reported numbers, with its path. (4) Command: one command that runs the pipeline end to end and writes `reports/metrics.json` and the figures. (5) Randomness: every seed in the config; any non-deterministic step named. (6) Expected output: the headline metrics with their tolerance ("Sharpe 0.86 ± 0.01 across platforms"). (7) Tests: `pytest -q` and what it covers. (8) Known differences: data revisions after the manifest's date change results; the manifest check reports them.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: environment (line 1). 2: data with hashes (line 2). 1: configuration (line 3). 1: one command (line 4). 1: seeds (line 5). 1: expected output with tolerance (line 6). 1: tests (line 7). 1: known differences (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* Two runs of the same commit give Sharpe 0.74 and 0.71. Their run records differ in: pandas 2.2 against 3.0; data manifest hash; seed source "global" against "config". Rank the likely causes and say how to confirm each.
  *Answer.* (1) The data hash: a revised price series changes results directly; re-run with the old manifest's data. (2) The seed source: a global seed shared with another library changes the random draws; re-run both with the config seed. (3) The pandas version: behaviour changes (copy-on-write, resampling labels) can move numbers; re-run in the old lockfile's environment. Change one factor at a time. Decision point: the three differences are confounded; only a one-at-a-time re-run attributes the 0.03 gap, which is itself within the Sharpe ratio's standard error and would not be noticed without the run records.

**RM7 Writing and defending results** (`set.method.writing`). Long-form: a results note from a metrics file (this rubric is every project's results-note rubric). Code drill `drl.proj.metrics-consistency` (d3, standard library): `check_note(text, metrics, rel_tol)` returning each number in the text that matches no metric, or matches one only outside tolerance. Hidden tests: a note with two planted mismatches; percentages against fractions ("12%" against 0.12); dates and years ignored; an empty note returns no findings. Known-wrong: exact string matching flags "0.86" against 0.8612 and fails the tolerance test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4; the results-note rubric).* From a metrics file for a momentum backtest (gross Sharpe 1.1, net 0.6 at 5 basis points, held-out net 0.3, 37 trials, DSR 0.58, break-even cost 9 basis points, maximum drawdown 31%), write the one-page results note.
  *Model answer.* (1) Question: does 12-1 momentum earn a premium in US large caps after costs? (2) Data: point-in-time universe, sources, dates, held-out period. (3) Method: signal, portfolio, costs, validation scheme, number of trials. (4) Results: net Sharpe 0.6 in sample and 0.3 held out, with standard errors; gross 1.1; drawdown 31%. (5) Uncertainty: DSR 0.58 after 37 trials, below the 0.95 bar, so the evidence is weak. (6) What failed: the 6-1 variant and the value-weighted version; the held-out drop of half. (7) Limitations: survivorship-free but one market; costs assumed, not measured; capacity untested. (8) Next steps: test in other markets; measure costs from MS data.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the question (line 1). 1: data and held-out period (line 2). 1: method with the trial count (line 3). 2: results with uncertainty, held-out first among equals (line 4). 2: the honest verdict from the DSR (line 5). 2: what failed, specifically (line 6). 1: limitations and next steps (lines 7 and 8).
- *Example B (d5).* Find five problems in this note: "Our strategy achieves a Sharpe of 2.3 (2005 to 2024) on S&P 500 constituents. We optimised the look-back window. Costs are negligible for this strategy. It is uncorrelated with the market. Future work: deploy."
  *Answer.* (1) A Sharpe above 2 for a simple rule is a bug until shown otherwise (BT lesson 71). (2) "S&P 500 constituents": today's or point-in-time? (3) The look-back was optimised on the reported period: no held-out result, no trial count. (4) "Costs are negligible": no turnover or break-even cost given. (5) "Uncorrelated with the market": no beta or factor regression shown. Also missing: uncertainty and what failed. Decision point: each claim lacks the number that would test it; the fix is to replace adjectives with the metrics of RM2 to RM5.

**RM8 The empirical finance toolkit** (`set.method.empirical`). Long-form: Fama-MacBeth and cross-sectional correlation. Code drill `drl.proj.fama-macbeth` (d4, needs NumPy): per-period OLS slopes, their mean, standard error and $t$, with optional Newey-West lags. Hidden tests: a seeded panel's results match values stored from statsmodels in CI within $10^{-8}$; with zero lags the error equals the plain standard error of the mean; a period with fewer shares than regressors is skipped and counted. Known-wrong: pooled OLS on the stacked panel gives a $t$ several times larger and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Describe the Fama-MacBeth procedure and explain why its standard error stays valid under correlation across shares on the same date, but not to correlation of the slopes over time.
  *Model answer.* (1) Each period, regress the cross-section of returns on characteristics: one vector of slopes per period. (2) Average the slopes over the $T$ periods. (3) Standard error: the standard deviation of the $T$ slope estimates divided by $\sqrt T$. (4) Shares on one date share shocks, so their errors are correlated; this changes each period's slope, but the procedure uses each period's slope as one observation, so cross-sectional correlation is absorbed into the slopes' variation over time. (5) If slopes are autocorrelated (persistent characteristics, overlapping returns), the $T$ observations are not independent and the standard error is too small: use Newey-West lags on the slope series. (6) Pooled OLS treats $N \times T$ residuals as independent and overstates $t$ by roughly the square root of the effective cross-sectional correlation times $N$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: steps 1 and 2. 2: the standard error (line 3). 3: why cross-sectional correlation is handled (line 4). 2: the time-series caveat and fix (line 5). 1: contrast with pooled OLS (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* For 100 events, abnormal returns on days $-1$, 0 and $+1$ average 0.1%, 0.8% and 0.3%; the cumulative abnormal return over the window has a standard deviation of 3% across events. Compute the mean CAR and its $t$-statistic, and state two conditions for the test to be valid.
  *Answer.* CAR $= 1.2\%$; $t = \frac{1.2}{3/\sqrt{100}} = 4.0$. Conditions: events not clustered in calendar time (otherwise their abnormal returns are correlated and the $t$ is overstated); an estimation window that excludes the event window, so the normal-return model is not fitted on the event itself. Decision point: the standard error comes from the spread of CARs across events, not from the daily volatility of one share.

#### Course BT

**BT1 What a backtest is** (`set.backtest.basics`). Long-form: a lagged position can still leak. Code drill `drl.proj.vector-backtest` (d3, needs pandas): `backtest(opens, closes, signal)` trading at the next open and crediting open-to-close then close-to-close returns. Hidden tests: example B's table; the shuffle-the-future test (changing every price after day $t$ leaves positions up to $t$ unchanged); a signal of all zeros returns zeros. Known-wrong: crediting close-to-close returns from the signal's own close fails the first and second tests.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A vectorised backtest uses `position = signal.shift(1)`, where the signal is a z-score of momentum using the mean and standard deviation of the whole sample. Explain why it still has look-ahead, how big the effect can be, and how to fix it.
  *Model answer.* (1) The shift stops today's return from setting today's position. (2) But the z-score on every date uses the full sample's mean and standard deviation, which include future data. (3) So on each date the signal knows something about the future distribution (for example, that a regime of high returns is coming raises the mean), and positions are scaled with that knowledge. (4) The effect is largest when the series has regimes or trends; it can turn a zero-edge signal into a positive one. (5) Fix: expanding or rolling statistics computed with data up to $t - 1$ only. (6) Test: the shuffle-the-future test fails for the full-sample version and passes for the fix.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: what the shift does (line 1). 3: the leak identified (line 2). 2: the mechanism (line 3). 1: size (line 4). 2: fix (line 5). 1: the test (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Day 1 closes at 102 and the signal turns long at that close. Day 2 opens at 106 and closes at 104; day 3 opens at 103 and closes at 107; the signal turns flat at day 3's close; day 4 opens at 108. Compute the trade's return with correct timing, and the return a backtest that trades at the signal's close would report.
  *Answer.* Correct: buy at day 2's open, 106; sell at day 4's open, 108: $+1.89\%$. Signal-close timing: buy at 102, sell at 107: $+4.90\%$. Decision point: the overnight gap from 102 to 106 is earned only by a trader who could not have known the signal yet; most of the inflated return sits in that gap.

**BT2 Portfolio accounting** (`set.backtest.accounting`). Long-form: first-in-first-out and average cost give the same total P&L. Code drill `drl.proj.ledger` (d4, standard library): a `Ledger` with integer cents supporting buy, sell, mark, dividend and split, and FIFO lots. Hidden tests: example B's sequence gives its cash, position, equity and realised P&L exactly; equity equals cash plus marked positions after every event; a short position pays dividends; selling more than held without a short flag raises. Known-wrong: floats for cash; the invariant test compares in integer cents and fails once accumulated rounding reaches a cent, which the seeded trade list is built to trigger.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove that total P&L (realised plus unrealised) does not depend on whether lots are matched first-in-first-out or at average cost.
  *Model answer.* (1) Total P&L over a period is final equity minus initial equity minus net deposits. (2) Equity is cash plus the market value of the position. (3) Cash depends only on the trades (prices, quantities, fees) and income, not on how lots are matched. (4) The position's quantity and its mark depend only on the trades and the price, not on matching. (5) So equity, and hence total P&L, is the same under any matching rule. (6) The method only splits the total between realised (closed lots) and unrealised (open lots): FIFO realises against the oldest costs, average cost against the mean.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: total P&L from equity (line 1). 1: equity (line 2). 2: cash independent of matching (line 3). 2: position independent (line 4). 2: conclusion (line 5). 1: what does differ (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Start with USD 10,000.00. Day 1: buy 50 at 40.00, fee 1.00. Day 2: buy 50 at 44.00, fee 1.00. Day 3: a 2-for-1 split. Day 4: dividend 0.50 a share. Day 5: sell 120 at 23.00, fee 1.00; mark at 23.50. Find cash, position, equity, and realised P&L (FIFO, before fees).
  *Answer.* After the split the lots are 100 at 20.00 and 100 at 22.00. Cash: $10{,}000 - 2{,}001 - 2{,}201 + 100 + 2{,}759 = 8{,}657$. Position 80 shares, marked at 1,880. Equity 10,537. Realised (FIFO): sold 100 at cost 20 and 20 at cost 22 for 2,760: $2{,}760 - 2{,}440 = 320$. Unrealised: $80 \times 1.50 = 120$. Check: $320 + 120 + 100 - 3 = 537 = 10{,}537 - 10{,}000$. Decision point: the split changes quantities and per-share costs but not lot values; applying it to cash or to realised P&L is the commonest ledger bug.

**BT3 Fills, costs and constraints** (`set.backtest.costs`). Long-form: the break-even cost. Code drill `drl.proj.cost-model` (d3, needs NumPy): `trade_cost(value, adv_value, sigma, half_spread_bps, commission_bps, Y)` with square-root impact and a participation cap. Hidden tests: lesson 62's inputs give 40 basis points of impact; costs scale linearly in value without impact and super-linearly with it; a trade above the cap raises a `CapacityError` carrying the fillable value. Known-wrong: impact proportional to participation fails the second test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Derive the break-even cost per unit traded for a strategy with gross return $g$ per period and turnover $\tau$ per period (units traded, both sides), and explain why it is the most informative single number in a cost section.
  *Model answer.* (1) Net return per period is $g - c\tau$ for a cost $c$ per unit traded. (2) Setting it to zero gives $c^* = \frac g\tau$. (3) For example, gross 6% a year and turnover 10 times a year gives 60 basis points per unit; turnover 100 gives 6. (4) Comparing $c^*$ with realistic costs for the universe (2 to 5 basis points for large caps, 20 or more for small caps) shows at once whether the strategy can exist. (5) It needs no cost model to be trusted, only a measured turnover, and it bounds every cost assumption the reader might doubt. (6) Impact makes $c$ rise with size, so $c^*$ also bounds capacity (lesson 121).
  *Rubric (10).* 1: net return (line 1). 2: the formula (line 2). 2: a worked number (line 3). 2: the comparison (line 4). 2: why it is hard to dispute (line 5). 1: link to capacity (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A strategy has gross alpha 5% a year on USD 50m with impact costs of 0.8% a year, which scale with the square root of assets. Find the asset size that maximises net dollar profit, the net alpha there, and the size at which net alpha is zero.
  *Answer.* Net profit $A(5 - 0.8\sqrt{A/50})$ in percent-dollars; its derivative $5 - 1.2\sqrt{A/50} = 0$ gives $\sqrt{A/50} = 4.17$, $A = $ USD 868m, with net alpha $5 - 3.33 = 1.67\%$. Net alpha is zero at $\sqrt{A/50} = 6.25$, USD 1.95bn. Decision point: the profit-maximising size is less than half the zero-alpha size; a fund running at "capacity" in the second sense earns nothing.

**BT4 Biases you can switch on** (`set.backtest.biases`). Long-form: a bias audit. Code drill `drl.proj.shuffle-future-test` (d4, needs NumPy): `assert_no_lookahead(strategy, prices, t_cut, seed)` perturbing every price after `t_cut` and asserting the positions up to `t_cut` are unchanged. Hidden tests: it catches a same-bar strategy and a full-sample z-score strategy; it passes a correctly lagged strategy; it is deterministic for a seed. Known-wrong: perturbing only the final price misses the z-score leak.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A backtest has five planted defects: today's index members as the universe; unadjusted prices across a 4-for-1 split; the look-back chosen on the reported period; zero costs; fills at the signal's close. Write the audit: for each, how you detect it, its likely direction and size, and the fix.
  *Model answer.* (1) Universe: compare membership on a past date with point-in-time lists; biases returns upwards by dropping failures; fix with point-in-time membership and delisting returns. (2) Split: look for a $-75\%$ one-day return with no news; creates a fake crash (a false short signal, or a huge loss); fix with adjusted returns. (3) Look-back: ask for the trial log; inflates Sharpe by selection; fix with walk-forward selection and a held-out period. (4) Costs: turnover times zero; overstates net returns by turnover times cost; fix with the cost model and report the break-even cost. (5) Fill timing: compare fill prices with the next open; inflates returns by the overnight move after the signal; fix by trading at the next open. (6) Order of work: data defects first (1, 2), then design (3), then implementation (4, 5), because later fixes are meaningless on wrong data.
  *Rubric (10).* 2 each for defects 1 to 5 with detection, direction and fix (lines 1 to 5): 1 mark for detection and direction, 1 for the fix. The ordering in line 6 earns no mark but lifts a borderline answer.
- *Example B (d5).* On a random walk with daily volatility $\sigma$, a rule holds the share on exactly the days its return is positive (it "knows" today's return). Find its daily mean, daily standard deviation and annualised Sharpe ratio.
  *Answer.* Daily return $\max(r, 0)$ with $r \sim \mathcal N(0, \sigma^2)$: mean $\frac\sigma{\sqrt{2\pi}} = 0.399\sigma$; second moment $\frac{\sigma^2}2$; standard deviation $\sigma\sqrt{\frac12 - \frac1{2\pi}} = 0.584\sigma$. Daily Sharpe 0.683, annual $0.683\sqrt{252} = 10.8$. Decision point: pure look-ahead on a series with no edge at all gives a Sharpe ratio of 10.8, independent of $\sigma$; that is why any simple rule above 2 is treated as a bug first.

**BT5 Sizing and risk control** (`set.backtest.sizing`). Long-form: the continuous Kelly fraction and half Kelly. Code drill `drl.proj.vol-target` (d3, needs pandas): `vol_target_weights(returns, target, halflife, cap)` with EWMA volatility lagged one day. Hidden tests: the shuffle-the-future test passes; the cap holds; on IID returns the realised volatility is within 10% of target; the first weights wait for a burn-in period. Known-wrong: unlagged volatility fails the shuffle test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Wealth grows at rate $g(f) = r + f(\mu - r) - \frac12f^2\sigma^2$ when a fraction $f$ is held in an asset with drift $\mu$ and volatility $\sigma$. Derive the Kelly fraction, the growth rate at Kelly and at half Kelly, and explain why practitioners use half Kelly or less.
  *Model answer.* (1) $g'(f) = \mu - r - f\sigma^2 = 0$ gives $f^* = \frac{\mu - r}{\sigma^2}$. (2) $g(f^*) = r + \frac{(\mu - r)^2}{2\sigma^2}$. (3) $g(\frac12f^*) = r + \frac12f^*(\mu - r) - \frac18f^{*2}\sigma^2 = r + \frac34\cdot\frac{(\mu - r)^2}{2\sigma^2}$: three quarters of the excess growth. (4) Volatility of wealth is $f\sigma$: half Kelly halves it, and drawdowns shrink accordingly. (5) $\mu$ is estimated with large error; since $g$ falls fast for $f > f^*$ (at $2f^*$ excess growth is zero), over-betting from an overestimated edge is costly, under-betting cheap. (6) So half Kelly gives up a quarter of the growth for half the risk and a margin against estimation error.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the optimum (line 1). 1: growth at Kelly (line 2). 2: growth at half Kelly (line 3). 1: volatility (line 4). 3: estimation error and asymmetry (line 5). 1: the trade-off stated (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Excess return 6% and volatility 15%. Find the Kelly gearing, the excess growth at Kelly and at half Kelly, the portfolio volatility in each case, and the gearing at which excess growth is zero.
  *Answer.* $f^* = \frac{0.06}{0.0225} = 2.67$. Excess growth at Kelly 8.0%, volatility 40%; at half Kelly 6.0%, volatility 20%; zero excess growth at $2f^* = 5.33$. Decision point: the excess growth at Kelly (8%) exceeds the asset's own excess return (6%) only because of gearing; a 40% volatility account would see drawdowns few investors tolerate, which is the practical case for half Kelly.

**BT6 Strategies to test the engine on** (`set.backtest.strategies`). Long-form: is a 62% hit rate evidence? Code drill `drl.proj.tsmom` (d4, needs pandas): time-series momentum signals (sign of 12-month excess return) with volatility scaling, lagged. Hidden tests: positions on a seeded panel match stored values; the shuffle test passes; an asset with fewer than 12 months of history gets no position. Known-wrong: using the current month's return fails the shuffle test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* An RSI rule wins 62% of its trades over a period in which the market rose 15% a year. Is that evidence of skill? Design the test that would decide.
  *Model answer.* (1) The hit rate depends on the market's drift: in a rising market a long-biased rule wins most trades by luck. (2) It also ignores the size of wins and losses (RM lesson 11). (3) The null: random entries with the same number of trades, the same holding periods and the same long-short mix, on the same dates. (4) Compute the rule's P&L (not hit rate) and the distribution of P&L across, say, 1,000 nulls; the p-value is the share of nulls that do at least as well (lesson 17). (5) Report net of costs, with the trial count if the RSI settings were tuned. (6) Expect "no edge"; if found, test out of sample and in a falling market.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: drift explains hit rate (line 1). 1: size ignored (line 2). 3: a matched null (line 3). 2: the p-value on P&L (line 4). 1: costs and trials (line 5). 1: what to expect and next test (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A stylised carry strategy earns $+0.8\%$ in 95% of months and $-10\%$ in 5%. Find the monthly mean, standard deviation and skewness, and the annualised Sharpe ratio. What does the Sharpe ratio miss?
  *Answer.* Mean $0.95 \times 0.8 - 0.05 \times 10 = 0.26\%$. Standard deviation 2.35%. Skewness $-4.1$. Annualised Sharpe $\frac{0.26}{2.35}\sqrt{12} = 0.38$. It misses that one month in twenty loses more than a year of gains, and that such months cluster in crises, when other assets fall too. Decision point: compute the distribution, not just two moments; skew-adjusted measures (lesson 14) and drawdown statistics expose what the Sharpe ratio hides.

**BT7 Architecture, tests and speed** (`set.backtest.architecture`). Long-form: the engine's component tree in project-forge's branch-chart style. Code drill `drl.proj.event-order` (d3, standard library): a deterministic event queue ordered by (timestamp, sequence number). Hidden tests: events with equal timestamps come out in insertion order; ordering is identical across runs; popping from an empty queue raises; 100,000 events stay in order. Known-wrong: a heap on timestamps alone fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Draw the backtester's component tree as a branch chart, with each component's responsibility, what crosses each boundary, and its test strategy.
  *Model answer.* (1) `data/`: loads cached, adjusted bars and point-in-time universes; emits bar events; tested with golden files and the manifest. (2) `strategy/`: turns bars into target weights; never sees fills or future bars; tested with the shuffle-the-future test. (3) `portfolio/`: turns targets into orders from marked equity; owns the ledger; tested with invariants. (4) `execution/`: turns orders into fills with the fill and cost models; tested against hand-computed fills. (5) `report/`: computes metrics with standard errors and the held-out section from the ledger; tested against `drl.proj.metrics`. (6) `engine.py`: the event loop with deterministic ordering; tested end to end with the buy-and-hold golden test. (7) Boundaries: bars (data to strategy), targets (strategy to portfolio), orders (portfolio to execution), fills (execution to portfolio), ledger (portfolio to report).
  *Rubric (10).* 1 each for the six components with responsibility and test (lines 1 to 6). 4: the boundaries named with what crosses them (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* An engine rebalances 500 shares every day for 20 years, sorting the cross-section each day to rank signals. State the time complexity, estimate the work, and name the change that makes it fastest.
  *Answer.* Per day: computing signals $O(N)$, sorting $O(N\log N)$, orders $O(N)$; over $T$ days $O(TN\log N)$, about $5{,}040 \times 500 \times 9 \approx 2.3 \times 10^7$ basic steps: seconds in NumPy, possibly minutes in a pure-Python loop over objects. The biggest gain: vectorise the cross-sectional ranking across all days at once (one `argsort` on a $T \times N$ array) and keep the event loop only for path-dependent parts. Decision point: profile first; the sort is rarely the bottleneck, object creation per bar usually is.

#### Course AR

**AR1 Universes and signals** (`set.alpha.signals`). Long-form: the skip month and the stories of momentum and reversal. Code drill `drl.proj.momentum-signal` (d3, needs pandas): 12-1 momentum from a monthly price panel with missing values. Hidden tests: lesson 94's share gives 0.20; a share with fewer than 12 months of history gets `NaN`; the signal at month $t$ uses prices up to $t - 1$ only; a split in raw prices is handled by using adjusted prices. Known-wrong: including the latest month fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Explain why 12-1 momentum skips the most recent month, and give the leading explanations for momentum and for short-term reversal.
  *Model answer.* (1) Returns over the last month tend to reverse, so including them dilutes or reverses the momentum signal. (2) Reversal is usually explained as liquidity provision: prices pushed by temporary order imbalances bounce back, paying those who supply liquidity (MK). (3) Bid-ask bounce adds a mechanical reversal at the shortest horizons (Roll, MK lesson 41). (4) Momentum's explanations: behavioural under-reaction to news, then over-reaction; and risk-based stories (exposure to crashes). (5) Neither story is settled; the momentum premium is weaker in some markets and periods and crashes after sharp rebounds (lesson 127). (6) The skip month is therefore a design choice with a reason, and varying it is a trial to log.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the reversal effect (line 1). 2: liquidity provision (line 2). 1: bounce (line 3). 2: momentum's explanations (line 4). 2: honesty about evidence (line 5). 1: the trial point (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Raw (unadjusted) monthly closes for a share: 12 months ago 80, then a 2-for-1 split in month 6, a month ago 44, today 45. Compute 12-1 momentum from raw prices and correctly, and say what a raw-price universe does to the momentum ranking.
  *Answer.* Raw: $\frac{44}{80} - 1 = -45\%$. Adjusted: the price 12 months ago is 40 in post-split terms, so $\frac{44}{40} - 1 = +10\%$. The raw signal puts a winner among the losers. Decision point: every split in raw data creates a false loser (and a reverse split a false winner); signals are always computed from adjusted returns.

**AR2 Cleaning and neutralising** (`set.alpha.neutralise`). Long-form: demeaning within sectors removes sector exposure. Code drill `drl.proj.neutralise` (d4, needs NumPy): residualise a signal on sector dummies and beta by least squares. Hidden tests: the residual has zero exposure to every sector and to beta within $10^{-10}$; a sector with one share gets a zero residual; the residual is unchanged by adding a constant to the signal. Known-wrong: demeaning across the whole cross-section leaves sector exposure and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Show that weights $w_i = s_i - \bar s_{\text{sec}(i)}$ (the signal minus its sector mean) have zero net exposure to every sector, and explain what neutralisation removes and what it keeps.
  *Model answer.* (1) The portfolio's exposure to sector $k$ is $\sum_{i \in k}w_i$. (2) $\sum_{i \in k}(s_i - \bar s_k) = \sum_{i \in k}s_i - n_k\bar s_k = 0$ by the definition of the mean. (3) So every sector's net weight is zero: the portfolio is long and short within each sector. (4) It removes the part of the signal that ranks sectors against each other (a sector bet), which the researcher may not have intended and which a sector factor already prices. (5) It keeps the ranking of shares within each sector. (6) If the sector ranking was the source of the edge, neutralising destroys it; that is a result to report, not to hide.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: exposure defined (line 1). 3: the proof (line 2). 1: interpretation (line 3). 2: what is removed (line 4). 1: what is kept (line 5). 1: the honest caveat (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Three standardised signals have ICs 0.03, 0.025 and 0.02 and correlation matrix with off-diagonals 0.5 (signals 1 and 2), 0.2 (1 and 3) and 0.3 (2 and 3). Find the IC of the equal-weighted combination and of the best linear combination, assuming forecast errors behave like the signals' correlations.
  *Answer.* Equal weights: $\frac{0.075}{\sqrt{\mathbf 1^\top R\mathbf 1}} = \frac{0.075}{\sqrt{5}} = 0.0335$. Best weights proportional to $R^{-1}\mathrm{IC}$: normalised $(0.50, 0.22, 0.28)$, IC $= \sqrt{\mathrm{IC}^\top R^{-1}\mathrm{IC}} = 0.0343$. Decision point: the optimal combination gains only 2% over equal weights, and its weights would be estimated with error; equal weights are the honest default unless the gain is large and stable.

**AR3 Measuring predictive power** (`set.alpha.measure`). Long-form: the fundamental law. Code drill `drl.proj.ic-tools` (d4, needs pandas): per-date rank IC, mean, ICIR, $t$ with optional overlap correction, and decay by horizon. Hidden tests: on a `market-gen` panel with true IC 0.03, the estimate lies within 3 standard errors; rank IC of lesson 104's data is 0.8; overlap-corrected $t$ is smaller than naive for overlapping horizons. Known-wrong: aligning the signal with the same-period return it was built from finds a large IC on a panel whose true IC is 0.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Under Grinold's assumptions (each of $BR$ independent bets a year has forecast alpha $\mathrm{IC} \cdot \sigma \cdot z$ with $z$ standardised), show that the best information ratio is about $\mathrm{IC}\sqrt{BR}$, and say what breadth really counts.
  *Model answer.* (1) For one bet, the expected residual return of a position sized optimally is proportional to its forecast alpha over its residual variance. (2) With alpha $\mathrm{IC}\,\sigma z$ and residual volatility $\sigma$, each bet's contribution to squared information ratio is $\mathrm{IC}^2z^2$. (3) Independent bets add in squared information ratio: $\mathrm{IR}^2 = \sum\mathrm{IC}^2z^2$. (4) With $E[z^2] = 1$ over $BR$ bets, $\mathrm{IR}^2 \approx \mathrm{IC}^2 \cdot BR$, so $\mathrm{IR} \approx \mathrm{IC}\sqrt{BR}$. (5) Breadth counts independent bets a year: correlated shares, or a slow signal rebalanced often, give fewer bets than shares times rebalances. (6) Constraints shrink the transfer coefficient, $\mathrm{IR} = \mathrm{TC} \cdot \mathrm{IC}\sqrt{BR}$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: one bet (line 1). 2: contribution per bet (line 2). 2: additivity (line 3). 2: the law (line 4). 2: breadth (line 5). 1: transfer coefficient (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A signal forecasts 3-month returns and is sampled monthly: mean IC 0.04, standard deviation 0.15, 120 months. Find the naive $t$ and a corrected $t$ allowing for overlap, assuming the overlap gives autocorrelations of $\frac23$ and $\frac13$ at lags 1 and 2.
  *Answer.* Naive $t = \frac{0.04}{0.15/\sqrt{120}} = 2.92$. The variance of the mean is inflated by $1 + 2(\frac23 + \frac13) = 3$, so corrected $t = \frac{2.92}{\sqrt3} = 1.69$, not significant at 5%. Decision point: the 120 monthly ICs share two months of returns with each neighbour, so they hold about 40 independent observations.

**AR4 Factor models and risk models** (`set.alpha.risk`). Long-form: risk contributions sum to total variance. Code drill `drl.proj.factor-cov` (d4, needs NumPy): build $BFB^\top + D$, portfolio variance, factor share and contributions. Hidden tests: example B's numbers; contributions sum to total variance within $10^{-12}$; a singular $F$ is accepted (factor models need not be full rank), a negative $D$ raises. Known-wrong: omitting $D$ fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Show that the contributions $c_i = w_i(\Sigma w)_i$ sum to the portfolio variance $w^\top\Sigma w$, that $\frac{c_i}{\sigma_p}$ is asset $i$'s marginal contribution to volatility times its weight, and explain risk parity.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\sum_iw_i(\Sigma w)_i = w^\top\Sigma w$ directly. (2) $\sigma_p = \sqrt{w^\top\Sigma w}$, so $\frac{\partial\sigma_p}{\partial w_i} = \frac{(\Sigma w)_i}{\sigma_p}$. (3) Hence $w_i\frac{\partial\sigma_p}{\partial w_i} = \frac{c_i}{\sigma_p}$, and these sum to $\sigma_p$ (Euler's theorem for a function homogeneous of degree 1). (4) A contribution can be negative: a hedge reduces total risk. (5) Risk parity chooses weights so every $c_i$ is equal. (6) In a 60/40 portfolio the shares contribute about 94% of risk (lesson 115), so capital weights mislead.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the sum (line 1). 2: the derivative (line 2). 2: Euler (line 3). 1: negative contributions (line 4). 2: risk parity (line 5). 1: the example (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Three shares with exposures to two factors $B = \begin{pmatrix}1.1 & 0.3\\0.9 & -0.4\\1.0 & 0.1\end{pmatrix}$, factor covariance $F = \mathrm{diag}(0.04, 0.01)$, idiosyncratic variances 0.03, 0.02 and 0.05, and weights 0.5, 0.3, 0.2. Find the portfolio's factor exposures, total variance, volatility and the share of variance from factors.
  *Answer.* Exposures $w^\top B = (1.02, 0.05)$. Factor variance $1.02^2 \times 0.04 + 0.05^2 \times 0.01 = 0.04164$; idiosyncratic $0.25 \times 0.03 + 0.09 \times 0.02 + 0.04 \times 0.05 = 0.0113$; total 0.05294, volatility 23.0%; factors 78.7%. Decision point: a long-only portfolio is mostly a market bet, which is why alpha research works with neutralised, residual returns.

**AR5 From signal to portfolio** (`set.alpha.portfolio`). Long-form: mean-variance weights amplify forecast errors. Code drill `drl.proj.rank-weights` (d3, needs NumPy): dollar-neutral rank weights scaled to a gross target with a per-name cap, redistributing the excess. Hidden tests: weights sum to 0 and gross to the target within $10^{-12}$; no weight above the cap; lesson 117's five shares give $-\frac13, -\frac16, 0, \frac16, \frac13$; ties get equal weights. Known-wrong: clipping without redistribution misses the gross target.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For $w = \frac1\lambda\Sigma^{-1}\mu$, show that an error $\epsilon$ in $\mu$ moves the weights by $\frac1\lambda\Sigma^{-1}\epsilon$, and explain why this is large when assets are highly correlated.
  *Model answer.* (1) Weights are linear in $\mu$, so $w(\mu + \epsilon) - w(\mu) = \frac1\lambda\Sigma^{-1}\epsilon$. (2) Write $\Sigma = Q\Lambda Q^\top$: $\Sigma^{-1}\epsilon = Q\Lambda^{-1}Q^\top\epsilon$. (3) Errors along eigenvectors with small eigenvalues are divided by those small eigenvalues. (4) With two assets of equal volatility and correlation $\rho$, the eigenvalues are $\sigma^2(1 \pm \rho)$; the "difference" direction has eigenvalue $\sigma^2(1 - \rho)$, tiny when $\rho = 0.95$. (5) A small error in the difference of forecasts is multiplied by $\frac1{\sigma^2(1 - \rho)}$, producing huge long-short positions (lesson 118). (6) Remedies: shrink forecasts, shrink the covariance (raising small eigenvalues), constrain weights.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: linearity (line 1). 2: eigen-decomposition (line 2). 2: small eigenvalues amplify (line 3). 2: the two-asset case (lines 4 and 5). 2: remedies (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* The same two shares as lesson 118 (volatility 20%, correlation 0.95). Forecasts are 5% and 6%; then the second is revised to 5.5%. Compute $\Sigma^{-1}\mu$ before and after, and the gross weights scaled to a gross exposure of 1.
  *Answer.* Before: $(-1.79, 3.21)$, scaled $(-0.36, 0.64)$. After: $(-0.58, 1.92)$, scaled $(-0.23, 0.77)$. A half-point forecast change cuts the short by two thirds. Decision point: forecasts are never known to half a point, so the unconstrained optimiser's positions are mostly noise; shrink, constrain, or rank.

**AR6 Honest alpha research** (`set.alpha.honest`). Long-form: "what would change my mind". Code drill `drl.proj.bh` (d3, standard library): Benjamini-Hochberg returning rejected indices at level $q$. Hidden tests: example B's p-values reject six at $q = 0.10$; an empty list returns empty; all p-values 1 reject none; ties are handled; order of input preserved. Known-wrong: comparing each p-value with $q$ (no correction) rejects seven.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For a momentum signal you believe in, write the paragraph "What would change my mind": the results, set in advance, that would make you abandon it.
  *Model answer.* (1) A held-out net Sharpe below 0 with the in-sample net Sharpe above 0.5. (2) A deflated Sharpe ratio below 0.5 after counting all trials. (3) A factor-adjusted alpha with $t < 1$ against the momentum factor, meaning the signal adds nothing to the published factor. (4) An effect that exists only in the most expensive decile, or only in the short leg of microcaps. (5) Instability: sub-period Sharpe ratios that differ by more than three standard errors with the recent ones negative. (6) A break-even cost below the measured cost for the universe. (7) Each threshold set before looking, so the paragraph is evidence of method, not a post-hoc excuse.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: held-out criterion (line 1). 1: DSR (line 2). 2: factor alpha (line 3). 1: where it lives (line 4). 1: stability (line 5). 1: costs (line 6). 2: thresholds set in advance (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Ten signals give p-values 0.001, 0.008, 0.012, 0.025, 0.031, 0.040, 0.09, 0.20, 0.45 and 0.70. Which does Benjamini-Hochberg reject at $q = 10\%$? How many would Bonferroni reject at a 10% family-wise level?
  *Answer.* BH thresholds $\frac{iq}m = 0.01, 0.02, \dots, 0.10$: the sorted p-values are below theirs up to the sixth (0.040 against 0.06) and the seventh fails (0.09 against 0.07), so the first six are rejected. Bonferroni: p at or below $\frac{0.10}{10} = 0.01$: two (0.001 and 0.008). Decision point: BH controls the expected share of false discoveries, suiting a screen of many signals; Bonferroni controls the chance of any false discovery, suiting a single claim. Choose by what the result will be used for.

**AR7 Signals beyond price, and machine learning** (`set.alpha.beyond-price`). Long-form: a point-in-time accounting pipeline. Code drill `drl.proj.sue` (d3, needs pandas): standardised unexpected earnings with a seasonal random-walk expectation, available from the announcement date. Hidden tests: stored values on a seeded panel; the signal is `NaN` before the announcement; fewer than 8 quarters of history give `NaN`. Known-wrong: using the fiscal quarter-end date fails the second test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Design the pipeline that turns quarterly accounting data into a point-in-time signal panel.
  *Model answer.* (1) Store each value with three dates: fiscal period end, announcement (release) date, and the date the data vendor recorded it. (2) Use values as first reported, not restated, keeping restatements as later versions. (3) A signal at date $t$ uses only values with a release date (and recording date) before $t$, joined with an as-of join (1.1 lesson 88). (4) Add a margin (for example one day) for release times after the close. (5) Align fiscal years that end in different months by release date, not fiscal period. (6) Test: rebuild the panel for a past date and check that no value released after it appears.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: three dates (line 1). 2: as first reported (line 2). 2: the as-of join (line 3). 1: the margin (line 4). 1: fiscal alignment (line 5). 2: the test (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Two standardised signals with correlation 0.6 have ICs 0.03 and 0.02. Find the combination weights (proportional to $(R + \lambda I)^{-1}\mathrm{IC}$) for $\lambda = 0$, 1 and 10, and their limit.
  *Answer.* $\lambda = 0$: $(0.90, 0.10)$. $\lambda = 1$: $(0.69, 0.31)$. $\lambda = 10$: $(0.61, 0.39)$. As $\lambda \to \infty$ the weights tend to the ICs' proportions $(0.6, 0.4)$. Decision point: with correlated signals the unpenalised fit nearly drops the second signal on a small difference in ICs; ridge pulls towards weights proportional to the ICs, which is more stable out of sample.

#### Course OA

**OA1 Option data** (`set.options-analytics.data`). Long-form: a cleaning policy. Code drill `drl.proj.implied-forward` (d3, needs NumPy): `implied_forward(strikes, calls, puts)` by least squares on $C - P = DF(F - K)$. Hidden tests: lesson 140's quotes give 0.96 and 101.04; noise of 0.01 on each price keeps the estimates within stated tolerance; fewer than two strikes raise. Known-wrong: regressing $K$ on $C - P$ gives a biased slope under noise and fails the second test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Write the cleaning policy for a daily snapshot of an equity option chain.
  *Model answer.* (1) Drop quotes with a zero bid, or flag them for wing extrapolation only. (2) Drop crossed quotes (bid above ask) and quotes whose relative spread exceeds a set threshold (for example 50% of the mid). (3) Drop quotes older than a set age at the snapshot time. (4) Keep out-of-the-money options for the smile; use near-the-money pairs for the implied forward. (5) Drop quotes that violate no-arbitrage bounds after allowing for spreads; log each one. (6) Record counts dropped by rule, by expiry; a rule that drops most of an expiry is reported, not hidden. (7) Fix the thresholds before looking at the results they affect.
  *Rubric (10).* 1 each for lines 1 to 5. 3: the logging (line 6). 2: thresholds fixed in advance (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A share at 60 goes ex a dividend of 2 tomorrow. An American call struck at 50 has 30 days left; $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 25\%$. Should it be exercised today?
  *Answer.* Exercise now: 10. Holding: after the dividend the share is about 58, and the call is then worth about its Black-Scholes value with 30 days left, 8.23 (no further dividends). Since $10 > 8.23$, exercise today. Check by the rule of thumb: the dividend (2) exceeds the interest saved by paying the strike later, $50(1 - e^{-0.05 \times 30/365}) = 0.21$, and the time value forgone is small for a call this deep in the money. Decision point: treating this option as European would misprice it and misstate its implied volatility; the early-exercise premium belongs in the data model.

**OA2 Implied volatility numerics** (`set.options-analytics.iv`). Long-form: implied volatility exists and is unique inside the bounds. Code drill `drl.proj.iv-vector` (d4, needs NumPy): vectorised implied volatility over a chain with bracketing and Newton steps. Hidden tests: round trips over a 50-strike, 6-expiry grid within $10^{-8}$; prices outside bounds return `NaN` with a reason code; lesson 142's case converges; it runs a 1,000-option chain in under 1 second on the web target [unverified on phones].
