Known-wrong: a scalar loop passes accuracy and fails the time test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove that for a European call on a share without dividends the Black-Scholes price is strictly increasing in $\sigma$, tends to $\max(S_0 - Ke^{-rT}, 0)$ as $\sigma \to 0$ and to $S_0$ as $\sigma \to \infty$, and conclude that every price strictly between these bounds has exactly one implied volatility.
  *Model answer.* (1) Vega $= S_0\sqrt Tn(d_1) > 0$ for every $\sigma > 0$, so the price is strictly increasing. (2) As $\sigma \to 0$, $d_1, d_2 \to +\infty$ if $S_0 > Ke^{-rT}$ (price tends to $S_0 - Ke^{-rT}$) and to $-\infty$ if below (price tends to 0). (3) As $\sigma \to \infty$, $d_1 \to +\infty$ and $d_2 \to -\infty$, so the price tends to $S_0$. (4) The price is continuous in $\sigma$. (5) By the intermediate value theorem every price in the open interval is attained, and strict monotonicity makes the solution unique. (6) Prices outside the interval (below intrinsic, above the share) have no implied volatility: they are arbitrage or bad data.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: vega positive (line 1). 2: the small-$\sigma$ limit (line 2). 2: the large-$\sigma$ limit (line 3). 1: continuity (line 4). 2: existence and uniqueness (line 5). 1: outside the bounds (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Share 100, 3-month call struck at 150, zero rates, priced at 30% volatility (0.0192). Run Newton's method from 100% and from 10%, and explain the difference.
  *Answer.* From 100%: 58.5%, 44.2%, 36.8%, 32.6%, 30.6%, 30.0%: steady convergence from above. From 10%: vega is about $10^{-13}$, so the first step lands above $10^{11}$. Decision point: the price is convex in volatility at low volatility and concave at high volatility for a far out-of-the-money option, so Newton from below overshoots wildly; bracket first, or start high.

**OA3 Smiles and surfaces** (`set.options-analytics.surface`). Long-form: forward variance and the calendar condition. Code drill `drl.proj.total-variance` (d3, needs NumPy): total variance by expiry at fixed log-moneyness, forward volatilities, and calendar flags. Hidden tests: lesson 149's numbers give 29.2%; lesson 154's give a flag; a flat surface gives forward volatility equal to spot volatility. Known-wrong: averaging volatilities gives 30% for lesson 149 and misses the flag in 154.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Show that the forward implied variance between expiries $T_1 < T_2$ at fixed log-moneyness is $\frac{\sigma_2^2T_2 - \sigma_1^2T_1}{T_2 - T_1}$, and explain why a negative value is a calendar arbitrage.
  *Model answer.* (1) Under Black-Scholes total variance adds over time: the variance from 0 to $T_2$ is that from 0 to $T_1$ plus that from $T_1$ to $T_2$. (2) So $\sigma_2^2T_2 = \sigma_1^2T_1 + \sigma_{12}^2(T_2 - T_1)$. (3) Solve for $\sigma_{12}^2$. (4) A variance cannot be negative, so total variance must not fall with expiry. (5) At fixed log-moneyness (forward-adjusted strikes), a later-dated call is worth at least an earlier one; total variance falling would price it lower. (6) The trade: buy the longer option, sell the shorter, at matched moneyness; it cannot lose at the first expiry.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: additivity (line 1). 2: the identity (lines 2 and 3). 2: non-negativity (line 4). 2: the option-price argument (line 5). 2: the trade (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* An expiry 5 trading days away (before earnings) trades at 30% volatility; one 12 days away (after earnings) at 40%. Assuming the 30% holds on ordinary days, find the market's implied earnings-day move.
  *Answer.* The 12 days are 11 ordinary days and the earnings day. Earnings-day variance $= 0.40^2 \times \frac{12}{252} - 0.30^2 \times \frac{11}{252} = 0.00369$, a standard deviation of 6.1% for the earnings day (of which $(0.40^2 - 0.30^2) \times \frac{12}{252} = 0.00333$, 5.8%, is the excess over an ordinary day; the item accepts 6.1% and gives 5.8% the feedback "that is the excess over an ordinary day; the earnings day also carries its ordinary variance"). Decision point: the event's variance is concentrated on one day; spreading it over the 12 days, as a volatility average does, understates the move and misprices options expiring just after the event.

**OA4 No arbitrage on the surface, and fitting** (`set.options-analytics.fitting`). Long-form: butterflies and the density. Code drill `drl.proj.svi-fit` (d4, needs NumPy; SciPy if shipped): fit raw SVI to one expiry by constrained least squares. Hidden tests: parameters recovered from a synthetic SVI smile within tolerance; the fit's density (Durrleman's condition) is non-negative on a grid; total variance stays positive; Lee's wing bound holds. Known-wrong: an unconstrained fit passes the first test on clean data and fails the density test on noisy data.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Show that a butterfly's price, divided by the squared strike spacing, tends to $e^{-rT}$ times the risk-neutral density, and conclude that call prices must be convex in strike.
  *Model answer.* (1) A butterfly with strikes $K - h$, $K$, $K + h$ pays a tent of height $h$ centred on $K$. (2) Its price is $C(K - h) - 2C(K) + C(K + h)$, the discounted expectation of the tent. (3) Divided by $h^2$, the tent becomes a spike of area 1 centred on $K$ as $h \to 0$. (4) So $\frac{C(K - h) - 2C(K) + C(K + h)}{h^2} \to e^{-rT}f(K)$, which is $\frac{\partial^2C}{\partial K^2}$ (Breeden and Litzenberger). (5) A density cannot be negative, and a tent's payoff is non-negative, so the butterfly's price cannot be negative: $C$ is convex in $K$. (6) A negative second difference in quotes, beyond bid and ask, is an arbitrage (1.7 lesson 63).
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the tent (line 1). 2: its price (line 2). 2: the limit (lines 3 and 4). 3: convexity from non-negativity (line 5). 2: the trading consequence (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Raw SVI with $a = 0.04$, $b = 0.4$, $\rho = -0.7$, $m = 0$, $\sigma = 0.1$ for an expiry of 6 months. Check Lee's wing bounds and positivity, and find the at-the-money implied volatility.
  *Answer.* Wing slopes of total variance: $b(1 + \rho) = 0.12$ on the right and $b(1 - \rho) = 0.68$ on the left, both below 2. Minimum total variance $a + b\sigma\sqrt{1 - \rho^2} = 0.0686 > 0$. At $k = 0$: $w = a + b\sigma = 0.08$, so $\sigma_{\text{impl}} = \sqrt{0.08/0.5} = 40\%$. Decision point: passing Lee's bound and positivity is necessary, not sufficient; the butterfly (density) condition must be checked on a grid as well.

**OA5 Three pricers compared, and a fourth** (`set.options-analytics.pricers`). Long-form: Monte Carlo error and antithetic variates. Code drill `drl.proj.mc-pricer` (d4, needs NumPy): European Monte Carlo with antithetic draws and a control variate, returning price and standard error. Hidden tests: within 4 standard errors of Black-Scholes for 5 parameter sets; antithetic standard error below plain for a call; the control-variate standard error below both; seeded reproducibility. Known-wrong: counting antithetic pairs as $2n$ independent draws understates the standard error and fails a coverage test over 200 seeds.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Show that a Monte Carlo estimate's standard error is $\frac{s}{\sqrt n}$, and that antithetic variates reduce variance exactly when $\mathrm{Cov}(f(Z), f(-Z)) < 0$.
  *Model answer.* (1) The estimate is the mean of $n$ IID payoffs with variance $s^2$, so its variance is $\frac{s^2}n$. (2) Halving the error needs four times the paths. (3) Antithetic: average $\frac12[f(Z) + f(-Z)]$ over $\frac n2$ pairs, using $n$ draws. (4) Its variance per pair is $\frac14[2s^2 + 2\mathrm{Cov}(f(Z), f(-Z))]$, so over $\frac n2$ pairs the estimator's variance is $\frac{s^2 + \mathrm{Cov}}n$. (5) This beats $\frac{s^2}n$ exactly when the covariance is negative. (6) For a call the payoff rises with $Z$, so $f(Z)$ and $f(-Z)$ move oppositely; for a straddle the payoff is symmetric in $Z$, so the covariance is positive and antithetics hurt.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the standard error (line 1). 1: the square-root law (line 2). 2: the antithetic estimator (line 3). 3: its variance (line 4). 1: the condition (line 5). 1: call against straddle (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* The CRR prices of an at-the-money call (share 100, $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$, one year), averaged over odd and even steps, are 10.450079 at 250/251 steps and 10.450334 at 500/501. Apply Richardson extrapolation assuming error proportional to $\frac1N$, and compare with Black-Scholes, 10.450584.
  *Answer.* $2(10.450334) - 10.450079 = 10.450589$, an error of $5 \times 10^{-6}$, against $2.5 \times 10^{-4}$ for the 500-step average. Decision point: extrapolation works only once the odd-even wobble is removed; applied to raw prices it amplifies the wobble.

**OA6 Greeks and risk in practice** (`set.options-analytics.greeks`). Long-form: the pathwise delta of a call. Code drill `drl.proj.bump-greeks` (d3, needs NumPy): central-difference delta, gamma and vega with chosen bump sizes, against closed forms. Hidden tests: agreement within $10^{-5}$ for delta and vega and $10^{-4}$ for gamma at the default bumps; a bump of $10^{-9}$ for gamma is rejected by a guard; Monte Carlo bumps use common random numbers. Known-wrong: fresh random numbers per bump give a gamma error above 100%.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Under $S_T = S_0\exp[(r - \frac12\sigma^2)T + \sigma\sqrt TZ]$, derive the pathwise estimator of a call's delta and explain why it fails for a digital option.
  *Model answer.* (1) Delta is $\frac{\partial}{\partial S_0}E[e^{-rT}(S_T - K)^+]$. (2) Exchange derivative and expectation (allowed because the payoff is continuous and differentiable almost everywhere): $E\left[e^{-rT}\frac{\partial(S_T - K)^+}{\partial S_T}\cdot\frac{\partial S_T}{\partial S_0}\right]$. (3) $\frac{\partial S_T}{\partial S_0} = \frac{S_T}{S_0}$ and $\frac{\partial(S_T - K)^+}{\partial S_T} = \mathbb 1\{S_T > K\}$. (4) So the estimator is $e^{-rT}\mathbb 1\{S_T > K\}\frac{S_T}{S_0}$, averaged over paths. (5) A digital's payoff jumps at $K$: its pathwise derivative is zero everywhere except at the jump, where it is undefined, so the exchange fails and the estimator returns 0. (6) Use the likelihood-ratio method (differentiate the density instead) or smooth the payoff.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: definition (line 1). 3: the exchange and its condition (line 2). 2: the two derivatives (line 3). 1: the estimator (line 4). 2: the digital's failure (line 5). 1: the remedy (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* For a central difference $\frac{f(x + h) - f(x - h)}{2h}$ with truncation error about $\frac{h^2}6\lvert f'''\rvert$ and rounding error about $\frac{\epsilon\lvert f\rvert}h$, find the bump that minimises the total, and its size for double precision.
  *Answer.* Minimise $\frac{h^2}6\lvert f'''\rvert + \frac{\epsilon\lvert f\rvert}h$: the derivative $\frac h3\lvert f'''\rvert - \frac{\epsilon\lvert f\rvert}{h^2} = 0$ gives $h = \left(\frac{3\epsilon\lvert f\rvert}{\lvert f'''\rvert}\right)^{1/3}$, of order $\epsilon^{1/3} \approx 6 \times 10^{-6}$ in relative terms; for a share at 100, a bump of about $10^{-3}$. Decision point: gamma (a second difference) has rounding error $\frac{\epsilon\lvert f\rvert}{h^2}$, so its best bump is larger, of order $\epsilon^{1/4}$; one bump size for every Greek is wrong.

**OA7 Volatility as a traded quantity** (`set.options-analytics.vol-trading`). Long-form: the variance risk premium. Code drill `drl.proj.realised-vol` (d3, needs NumPy): close-to-close realised volatility over windows, annualised, and its standard error. Hidden tests: a constant-volatility simulated series recovers its volatility within 2 standard errors; window boundaries are left-closed; fewer than 2 returns raise. Known-wrong: dividing by $n$ rather than $n - 1$ fails a small-window test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Explain why index implied volatility has usually exceeded the volatility later realised, why that is not free money for option sellers, and how a seller should size the trade.
  *Model answer.* (1) Buyers of index puts pay for insurance against crashes, when their other assets also fall; sellers demand a premium for bearing that risk. (2) So implied variance exceeds expected realised variance on average: the variance risk premium. (3) The seller's returns are mostly small gains and occasional very large losses, concentrated in crises (lesson 174). (4) A historical Sharpe ratio computed over calm years overstates the trade's quality, because the losses are rare and may be missing from the sample. (5) Size by the loss in a stress scenario (for example a 1987-style or 2008-style move), not by volatility, and consider buying far wings to cap losses. (6) The premium is compensation for real risk; it is earned only by those who survive the crashes.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: insurance demand (line 1). 2: the premium (line 2). 2: the return shape (line 3). 1: the Sharpe illusion (line 4). 2: sizing (line 5). 1: conclusion (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* An index weights three shares 0.5, 0.3 and 0.2, with implied volatilities 30%, 25% and 35%; index implied volatility is 22%. Assuming one common correlation, find the implied correlation.
  *Answer.* $\sigma_I^2 = \sum_iw_i^2\sigma_i^2 + \rho\sum_{i \ne j}w_iw_j\sigma_i\sigma_j$: $0.0484 = 0.0330 + \rho \times 0.0540$, so $\rho = 0.285$. Decision point: implied correlation comes from variances, not from averaging volatilities; a dispersion trader who thinks realised correlation will be lower sells index volatility and buys the members'.

#### Course MS

**MS1 Feeds and messages** (`set.systems.feeds`). Long-form: the connection state machine with failure behaviours. Code drill `drl.proj.backoff` (d3, standard library): `backoff_delays(base, cap, attempts, seed)` with full jitter, and `SequenceChecker` reporting gaps and duplicates. Hidden tests: delays never exceed the cap; with the jitter removed they are 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 30; a gap from 1042 to 1045 reports 1043 and 1044; a duplicate is reported once and not reapplied. Known-wrong: jitter added after the cap exceeds it.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Design the feed connection's state machine (states, transitions, and the failure behaviour for each external interaction), in the engineering floor's terms.
  *Model answer.* (1) States: disconnected, connecting, subscribing, snapshotting, live, resynchronising, stopped. (2) Connect failure: retry with capped exponential backoff and jitter; after N failures alert and keep retrying. (3) Heartbeat missed: treat as disconnect. (4) Sequence gap: enter resynchronising, buffer deltas, fetch a snapshot, apply buffered deltas after the snapshot's sequence number, return to live. (5) Invalid message: log with its raw bytes (no secrets), count it, drop it; if the rate exceeds a threshold, resynchronise. (6) Downstream queue full: apply the stage's policy (block, drop oldest, conflate) and record it (lesson 201). (7) Shutdown: flush in-progress candles as provisional, close cleanly. (8) Every transition is logged with a reason.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: states (line 1). 2: connect failure (line 2). 1: heartbeat (line 3). 2: gap recovery (line 4). 1: invalid messages (line 5). 1: backpressure (line 6). 1: shutdown (line 7). 1: logging (line 8).
- *Example B (d5).* Messages arrive in the order 1041, 1042, 1045, 1043, 1044, 1046. What does a handler with no reordering do, and what does one with a reorder buffer of 3 messages and a 50-millisecond wait do, if the out-of-order messages arrive within 10 milliseconds?
  *Answer.* Without reordering: at 1045 it sees a gap, resynchronises (a snapshot, perhaps hundreds of milliseconds), then receives 1043 and 1044 as stale and drops them. With the buffer: it holds 1045, receives 1043 and 1044 within the wait, applies 1043, 1044, 1045, 1046 in order, and never resynchronises. Decision point: on transports that can reorder (UDP multicast across lines), a small reorder buffer avoids needless resynchronisation; on TCP websockets reordering does not occur, so a gap means loss and resynchronisation is right.

**MS2 Rebuilding the book** (`set.systems.book`). Long-form: the data-structure choice. Code drill `drl.proj.l2-book` (d4, standard library): apply a snapshot and deltas with sequence checks; best bid and ask; invariants. Hidden tests: a recorded fixture of 10,000 messages ends in the stored book; deltas older than the snapshot are ignored; a crossed book after any message raises; size 0 deletes the level. Known-wrong: applying buffered deltas from before the snapshot fails the second test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Choose a data structure for a price-level book and justify it with complexities for add, update, delete and best price.
  *Model answer.* (1) Operations: update a level's size (most frequent), delete a level, insert a new level, read the best bid and ask (every message). (2) A sorted map (balanced tree): $O(\log n)$ for insert, update and delete; best price $O(1)$ with a cached iterator or $O(\log n)$. (3) An array indexed by price in ticks from a base: $O(1)$ update, insert and delete; best price needs a scan when the best level empties, bounded by the distance to the next level. (4) A hash map of levels plus two heaps: $O(1)$ update, $O(\log n)$ insert, lazy deletion. (5) Choice: for liquid instruments trading within a few hundred ticks of the mid, the array (cache-friendly, constant time), with recentring when prices drift; for wide or sparse books, the sorted map. (6) The choice is benchmarked (lesson 213) on recorded data, not assumed.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: operations (line 1). 2: sorted map (line 2). 2: array (line 3). 1: heaps (line 4). 3: the choice with its condition (line 5). 1: benchmark (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* An order-by-order feed (tick 0.01) sends: add 1 buy 300 at 100.00; add 2 buy 200 at 100.00; add 3 sell 400 at 100.02; add 4 buy 500 at 99.99; add 5 sell 100 at 100.03; execute 300 against order 1; cancel order 3; modify order 2 down to 150. Give the final book, the spread, the mid and the microprice.
  *Answer.* Bids: 150 at 100.00 (order 2), 500 at 99.99 (order 4). Asks: 100 at 100.03 (order 5). Spread 0.03, mid 100.015. Imbalance $\frac{150}{250} = 0.6$; microprice $0.6 \times 100.03 + 0.4 \times 100.00 = 100.018$. Decision point: the cancel of order 3 removes the whole best ask level, so the best ask jumps to 100.03 and the spread widens; a size reduction (order 2) keeps its place on most venues.

**MS3 Time and candles** (`set.systems.candles`). Long-form: a watermark policy. Code drill `drl.proj.candles` (d3, needs pandas): ticks to one-minute OHLCV and VWAP with left-closed bars, and a roll-up to any multiple. Hidden tests: example B's ticks; the roll-up equals bars built directly from ticks; a minute with no trades gives no bar; times are timezone-aware in UTC. Known-wrong: right-closed bars put the 10:01:00.000 tick in the 10:00 bar.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Choose a watermark and finalisation policy for one-minute candles, weighing latency against completeness.
  *Model answer.* (1) A bar can be finalised only once no more of its ticks will arrive; ticks can arrive late. (2) A watermark of $w$ seconds after the bar's end finalises it then; later ticks are late. (3) Small $w$: fast bars, more late ticks; large $w$: complete bars, slower consumers. (4) Measure the lateness distribution (receive minus exchange time) and set $w$ at, say, its 99.9th percentile. (5) Late ticks: amend and publish a correction downstream (including roll-ups), or drop and count; never amend silently. (6) For research archives amend; for a live signal, consumers must accept corrections or use provisional bars.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the problem (line 1). 2: watermark (line 2). 2: the trade-off (line 3). 2: setting it from data (line 4). 2: late-tick handling (line 5). 1: by consumer (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Ticks (time, price, size): 10:00:05.120, 100.00, 10; 10:00:31.400, 100.05, 5; 10:00:59.999, 99.98, 20; 10:01:00.000, 100.02, 10; 10:01:30.500, 100.10, 5; 10:01:45.000, 100.04, 15. Build the two one-minute bars with VWAP and the two-minute roll-up. Then a late tick (exchange time 10:00:40.000, 100.20, 2) arrives at 10:02:03 and is amended in: what changes?
  *Answer.* 10:00 bar: O 100.00, H 100.05, L 99.98, C 99.98, V 35, VWAP 99.9957. 10:01 bar: O 100.02, H 100.10, L 100.02, C 100.04, V 30, VWAP 100.0433. Roll-up: O 100.00, H 100.10, L 99.98, C 100.04, V 65, VWAP 100.0177. Amended 10:00 bar: H 100.20, V 37, VWAP 100.0068, and the roll-up's high becomes 100.20, its volume 67 and its VWAP 100.0231. Decision point: the tick at exactly 10:01:00.000 starts the second bar; and an amendment must reach the roll-up too.

**MS4 Latency and throughput** (`set.systems.latency`). Long-form: coordinated omission. Code drill `drl.proj.percentiles` (d3, standard library): percentiles from a log-bucketed histogram, and a coordinated-omission correction given the intended send interval. Hidden tests: lesson 199's scenario gives about 1 and about 900 milliseconds for p99 before and after; buckets keep 2 significant figures; an empty histogram raises. Known-wrong: averaging percentiles across intervals fails a merge test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Explain coordinated omission and how to correct for it.
  *Model answer.* (1) A load generator that waits for each reply before sending the next request stops sending while the system stalls. (2) So a 1-second stall produces one slow sample instead of the thousand slow requests that real users, arriving on schedule, would have suffered. (3) Percentiles from such data understate the tail by orders of magnitude (lesson 199). (4) Correction: send on a fixed schedule regardless of replies and measure from the intended send time; or, after the fact, back-fill the samples a stalled period should have produced (as HdrHistogram's correction does). (5) For a feed handler, measure from exchange or intended times, not from when the handler got round to reading. (6) Report percentiles with the method stated.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the mechanism (line 1). 2: the effect (line 2). 1: the size (line 3). 3: the corrections (line 4). 1: feed handler (line 5). 1: reporting (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Messages arrive at 8,000 a second; each takes 0.1 millisecond to process on one thread. Find the utilisation, the mean wait in the queue, the mean time in the system and the mean number in the system (single server, random arrivals); then the mean wait during a burst at 9,500 a second.
  *Answer.* Utilisation $\rho = 0.8$; mean queue wait $\frac\rho{1 - \rho} \times 0.1 = 0.4$ ms; time in system 0.5 ms; by Little's law $L = 8{,}000 \times 0.0005 = 4$ messages. At 9,500 a second, $\rho = 0.95$ and the wait is 1.9 ms, nearly five times longer for 19% more traffic. Decision point: latency is non-linear in load; plan capacity for bursts, with headroom well below 100%.

**MS5 Storage and replay** (`set.systems.storage`). Long-form: why "set" upserts are idempotent and "increment" ones are not. Code drill `drl.proj.upsert` (d3, standard library `sqlite3`): a candle table with key (symbol, interval, start) and `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`. Hidden tests: replaying a message log twice gives an identical table; a late amendment updates in place; queries are parameterised (an injection string is stored as data). Known-wrong: string-formatted SQL fails the injection test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Explain at-least-once delivery, why "set" upserts are idempotent and "increment" upserts are not, and how to make a volume total safe under replays.
  *Model answer.* (1) Networks and restarts mean a message may be delivered more than once; exactly-once delivery is not available end to end. (2) An operation is idempotent if applying it twice equals applying it once. (3) "Set the candle's values to these" is idempotent: a replay writes the same values. (4) "Add this tick's volume" is not: a replay double-counts. (5) Safe totals: store ticks keyed by their exchange trade id and compute volume from the set, or record the last applied sequence number with the candle and skip any message at or below it, in one transaction. (6) Test by replaying the same log twice and comparing tables.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: at-least-once (line 1). 1: definition (line 2). 2: set (line 3). 2: increment (line 4). 2: the remedies (line 5). 1: the test (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Fifty symbols each print 200,000 trades a day, stored at 40 bytes a trade. Find the raw storage per day and per year of 252 trading days, and the effect of columnar compression if it achieves 5 times [assumption: typical for such data, measured in the lesson].
  *Answer.* $50 \times 200{,}000 \times 40 = 400$ MB a day; 100.8 GB a year; about 20 GB compressed. Decision point: a laptop holds the compressed archive easily, a database row store with indexes may need several times the raw size; choose Parquet for history and the database for the live window.

**MS6 Performance engineering** (`set.systems.performance`). Long-form: memory layout and the cache. Code drill `drl.proj.ring-buffer` (d3, standard library): a fixed-capacity ring buffer with power-of-two capacity, an overwrite-or-reject policy and size tracking. Hidden tests: write number 1,029 in a capacity-1,024 buffer lands in slot 5; reject mode refuses when full; overwrite mode advances the read index; a capacity that is not a power of two raises. Known-wrong: masking without the power-of-two check accepts a capacity of 1,000 and fails the last test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Explain why summing one field of a million records is faster in a structure of arrays than in an array of structures, and what this means in Python and C++.
  *Model answer.* (1) Memory moves to the processor in cache lines (typically 64 bytes). (2) In an array of structures each record carries every field, so reading one field brings the others into cache unused. (3) In a structure of arrays the field's values are contiguous, so every byte fetched is used and the hardware prefetcher streams them. (4) In Python a list of objects is worse still: each element is a pointer to a separate object, scattered in memory. (5) NumPy arrays are contiguous structures of arrays, which is part of why vectorised code is fast. (6) In C++, choose the layout by access pattern: per-message processing favours structures; column scans favour arrays.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: cache lines (line 1). 2: AoS waste (line 2). 2: SoA streaming (line 3). 1: Python lists (line 4). 2: NumPy (line 5). 1: C++ choice (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A benchmark reports Python times (milliseconds) 52, 31, 30, 30, 31, 29, 30, 30, 31, 30 and C++ times 0.40, 0.31, 0.30, 0.30, 0.31, 0.30, 0.29, 0.30, 0.30, 0.31; the Python timer wraps file loading and the computation, the C++ timer only the loop. Summarise each fairly and say whether "C++ is 100 times faster" is supported.
  *Answer.* Discard the first (warm-up) run of each. Python median 30 ms; C++ median 0.30 ms; ratio 100. But the timers measure different work, so the ratio is not supported: time the same region (the loop) in both, or both end to end. Decision point: the comparison's validity depends on what is timed, not on how many runs; report the median and a high percentile of the same region, with the build flags.

**MS7 Orders and the trading system** (`set.systems.orders`). Long-form: the order state machine and the cancel-fill race. Code drill `drl.proj.order-state` (d4, standard library): an order state machine applying execution reports idempotently by execution id. Hidden tests: a duplicate fill is ignored; a fill after a cancel request leads to filled or partly filled, not cancelled; illegal transitions raise; quantities never exceed the order. Known-wrong: applying fills by quantity without execution ids double-counts a replayed fill.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Draw the order lifecycle's states and legal transitions, and explain how a cancel request and a fill can cross, and how the system must resolve it.
  *Model answer.* (1) States: pending new, new (acknowledged), partly filled, filled, pending cancel, cancelled, rejected, expired. (2) Legal: pending new to new or rejected; new to partly filled, filled, pending cancel or expired; partly filled to partly filled, filled or pending cancel; pending cancel to cancelled, partly filled or filled. (3) A cancel request travels to the venue while a fill travels back: the venue may fill before it sees the cancel. (4) The system must treat pending cancel as still live and accept fills in that state. (5) The final state comes from the venue's reports (cancelled with the filled quantity so far, or filled), never from the request. (6) Every report carries an execution id, so a replayed report is ignored.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: states (line 1). 2: transitions (line 2). 2: the race (line 3). 2: handling (lines 4 and 5). 2: idempotency (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* After a crash, local records show: order A buy 100, filled 100; order B buy 50, acknowledged, no fills; order C sell 30, cancel requested. The broker shows A filled 100, B filled 50, C filled 30. Local position +100. Find the broker's position and the steps to take before trading resumes.
  *Answer.* Broker position $100 + 50 - 30 = +120$. Steps: stop trading (kill switch); import the missing execution reports for B and C by execution id; set B and C to filled; recompute the position (+120) and check it against the broker's; check risk limits on the new position; record the incident; only then resume. Decision point: the broker's records are the truth for what happened; overwriting them with local state, or resuming before reconciling, turns a crash into a loss.

**MS8 Running a system safely** (`set.systems.operations`). Long-form: a post-mortem. Code drill `drl.proj.risk-gate` (d3, standard library): pre-trade checks (maximum order size, price collar against a reference, position limit, message rate) returning every failed check. Hidden tests: an order breaching two checks reports both; the rate limit uses a sliding window; the kill switch rejects everything once set; the gate is pure (no side effects on rejection). Known-wrong: returning on the first failure reports one check.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Write the post-mortem for: "After a network reconnect at 14:32:05 the bot re-sent 40 orders in one second; 12 filled; the position limit was breached for 3 minutes until a person stopped it."
  *Model answer.* (1) Summary in two sentences, with the impact (position, P&L, duration). (2) Timeline with exact times: reconnect, re-sends, fills, breach, detection, stop. (3) Cause: on reconnect the bot replayed unacknowledged orders with new client ids, so the broker treated them as new. (4) Why the safeguards failed: the position check ran in the strategy, which still held the old position; the rate limit was per order type, not total. (5) Detection: a person noticed the P&L chart; no alert fired. (6) Fixes: reuse client ids on retry (lesson 217); move risk checks outside the strategy (lesson 219); a total rate limit and a position alert. (7) Blameless: the process allowed it; no person is the cause.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: summary (line 1). 2: timeline (line 2). 2: cause (line 3). 2: failed safeguards (line 4). 1: detection (line 5). 1: fixes (line 6). 1: blameless framing (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Over a month, the backtest of the bot's rules returned 4.0% and paper trading 1.0%. Investigation finds: costs not in the backtest, 1.2%; fills worse than the backtest's model, 0.9%; data revised after the bot traded, 0.4%; a stale signal after a restart, 0.5%. Is the gap explained? What would you change?
  *Answer.* $1.2 + 0.9 + 0.4 + 0.5 = 3.0$ points, the whole gap. Changes: put the measured costs and fill model into the backtest; backtest the live period on the data the bot actually saw (recorded by MS5); fix the restart to recompute signals before trading. Decision point: a gap fully explained by named causes is a success of the monitoring; the bot's remaining edge (the backtest net of these) is what the results note reports.

### 3.4 Item 2.3: the projects and their milestone packs

#### 3.4.0 Rules for every project

- **Built on the learner's machine, in Git, published on GitHub** (widget catalogue decision 7). The app holds the brief, the starter suite (offered through `downloads` in the Artifact and as a public template on the web app; open item 7), the milestone checks and the results note.
- **Project-forge's phases are the milestones.** Phase 1: `docs/PLAN.md` with the eleven sections of `blueprint.md` (scope and non-goals; signal for a hiring manager; component tree; language per component; data; interfaces; threat class and its mandatory blocks; failure modes; limitations; repository tree; execution order), reviewed against the adversarial pass. Phase 2: stages from the plan's execution order, each closed by pasted `pytest -q` output and a short `STATE.md` update. Phase 3: README in two registers, `docs/DECISIONS.md`, the results note, and the pre-push gate (history-wide secrets scan, dependency audit, clean-clone test run, licence).
- **Evidence kinds** (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.8): `paste-verify` for test output and gate output, `repo-check` for the repository URL (web app only), `self-report` for the README (attested). "Finished" is `ASSESSMENT.md` 11.4's definition: repository URL, passing tests, README, and a results note at 7 of 10 with its `sections-complete` and `metrics-consistent` predicates passing.
- **Proposed note predicates** [our choice; change request with open item 6]: backtest and alpha notes add `trial-count`, `deflated-sharpe`, `held-out` and `costs-break-even`; the options note adds `pricer-agreement` and `arbitrage-report`; the market-data note adds `latency-method`; the bot note adds `null-comparison`. These carry canon-gap P1's checklist into 2.3 whether or not P1 is accepted.
- **Hours** (section 1.2.4): 70 per project, split roughly as Blueprint 8, Build 46 to 48, README and note 10, gate 2, defence 2; each project's table gives its split.
- **Marking.** The milestone pack's long-form items (the `PLAN.md` review and the results note) are self-marked with Bertie's suggested marks (`tutor-draft`); the defence uses `viva`.

#### 3.4.1 Project 1: backtesting engine (`prj.proj.backtester`, kind `backtest`)

**Brief.** An event-driven engine in Python (the developer track may write the core in C++ with Python bindings, as its own choice) with point-in-time universes, corporate actions, a ledger in integer cents, next-open fills, spread, commission and square-root impact costs, volume caps, borrow costs, volatility-targeted sizing, a locked held-out period, walk-forward selection, a trial ledger, and a report with Sharpe, Calmar and maximum drawdown with standard errors, the break-even cost and the deflated Sharpe ratio. Run on at least two strategies from BT6 and one deliberately overfit strategy. Threat class A.

| M1 `PLAN.md` passing the adversarial pass (`lsn.backtest.project-blueprint`) | Blueprint | self-marked rubric at 7 of 10; file in repository | 8 |

| M3 Ledger and portfolio with invariants | Build | paste (`test_ledger_*`, `test_invariants_*`) | 10 |
| M4 Execution: fills, costs, impact, caps | Build | paste (`test_execution_*`) | 10 |
| M5 Strategy interface, sizing, shuffle-the-future test | Build | paste (`test_lookahead_*`) | 8 |

| M8 Defence | | `viva` at 7 of 10 | 2 |

- *Milestone pack example A (the M1 review item, long-form).* "Review this `PLAN.md` for the engine and list its gaps in order of severity." Model answer: no test strategy for the execution model (severity 1: the component most likely to be wrong); no failure behaviour for a missing or malformed price file (2); costs unspecified (3); no held-out period or trial ledger in scope (4); data licence and caching unstated (5); class A stated but the dependency and publication blocks skipped silently (6). Rubric (10): 2 each for the first four gaps, 1 each for the last two.
- *Milestone pack example B (an M3 check).* The starter suite's `test_invariants_random_trades` runs 10,000 seeded random trades, splits and dividends and asserts, after every event, that equity equals cash plus marked positions in integer cents and that position quantities equal the sum of fills. A ledger in floats fails it once accumulated rounding reaches a cent, which the published seed is chosen to trigger (CI keeps a float ledger in `wrong/` to prove it).

#### 3.4.2 Project 2: alpha signal research (`prj.proj.alpha`, kind `alpha`)

**Brief.** Cross-sectional 12-1 momentum and 1-week or 1-month reversal across a few hundred US shares, from a point-in-time universe (with the survivorship limits of free data stated, and checked against Open Source Asset Pricing's portfolios where possible). Report rank IC with its $t$ and decay, quantile spreads, turnover, Sharpe after costs (run through project 1's engine, or a vectorised equivalent with the same cost model), factor-adjusted alpha, capacity, the deflated Sharpe ratio, and the held-out result. Pre-registration first. Threat class A.

#### 3.4.3 Project 3: options analytics dashboard (`prj.proj.options`, kind `options`)

**Brief.** From cached daily snapshots of yfinance option chains on a few US shares or an index ETF, or Deribit's public option data, build a static site: cleaned chains with logged drops; implied forwards; implied volatility smiles and a surface in log-moneyness and total variance; calendar and butterfly arbitrage reports; an SVI fit per expiry with errors against bid and ask; Black-Scholes, CRR (with Richardson), Monte Carlo (with variance reduction) and Crank-Nicolson pricers compared for accuracy and speed; Greeks across the surface; American early-exercise effects. Starts from the 1.7 Pricing Lab. Threat class A (the download script is the only network call, needs no key, and runs separately from the build).

Rubric (10): 2 terms, 2 snapshot timing, 2 caching and manifest, 3 failure behaviours, 1 volume.

#### 3.4.4 Project 4: live market data handler (`prj.proj.market-data`, kind `market-data`)

**Brief.** Subscribe to a public websocket feed (Deribit's public channels, or a crypto exchange's public trades stream [check terms]); validate and sequence-check messages; rebuild a top-of-book or L2 book; build multi-timeframe candles with a stated watermark; store candles in PostgreSQL (or Redis for the live window) with idempotent upserts; record raw messages for replay; measure latency percentiles without coordinated omission; alert on staleness. In C++ on the developer track (curriculum 2.3), with sanitisers and benchmarks in CI. Threat class B (database credentials; any API key), so secrets load from the environment, `.env` is ignored from the first commit and `.env.example` is committed, and logs never carry credentials.

Model answer: disconnect (backoff with cap and jitter; resynchronise; bars in progress marked provisional); sequence gap (resynchronise from a snapshot); invalid message (log, count, drop, threshold); database down (buffer to disk up to a bound, then alert and stop accepting, never drop silently); queue full (stated policy per stage); clock skew (exchange time for bars, monotonic time for latency); shutdown (flush, close). Rubric (10): 1 each for the seven behaviours, 3 for stating what state each leaves behind.

**Brief.** Built on projects 1 and 4: the backtester's strategy interface, the handler's live candles, an order manager with the lifecycle state machine and client order ids, pre-trade risk checks outside the strategy, a kill switch, reconciliation on restart, structured logs and alerts, and a paper broker (a broker's paper API, or a simulator using the MK fill rules). Strategy: the curriculum's EMA, RSI or Supertrend rules as a baseline, judged against a random-entry null with matched turnover (RM lesson 17). Paper only. ## 4. Topic exam blueprint and the exit tests

### 4.1 Item 1.7: `exm.fin`

#### 4.1.1 The paper

| Property | Value |
|---|---|
| Kind | `topic` (`ASSESSMENT.md` 3.1) |
| Duration | 90 minutes, one clock, the 15-minute pause budget of `ASSESSMENT.md` decision 3 |
| Items | 12 questions, 100 marks, marked per part |
| Difficulty mix, by marks | 20 at d2, 50 at d3, 20 at d4, 10 at d5, checked per form by CI within 5 points |
| Format mix, by marks | Long-form 27 (the three exit slots); numeric 62; single choice 4; point or curve 3; set selection 4 |
| Aids | The in-app scientific calculator, except on Q4, Q5(b) and Q7 (written answers). A formula card with $N(\cdot)$ values is not given: the calculator has the normal CDF |
| Pass | **70 of 100**, and **7 of 10 on each exit slot** (Q4, Q5 and Q7: `slotMin` 0.7, `ASSESSMENT.md` decision 28) |
| Forms | At least 3; 69 of 100 marks from generator slots; each long-form slot draws from an authored pool of at least 6 items |
| Practice | One practice paper of the same shape from the practice pool, never the exam reserve |
| Resit | 7-day cool-down and a remediation set on the failed skills (`ASSESSMENT.md` 3.8); an exit slot below 7 can be met in a later sitting or an exit-item sitting without resitting the whole paper |
| Help | None: Bertie is away app-wide (`AI-TUTOR.md` 2.12); written slots are marked afterwards against the rubric, self-marked with Bertie's suggested marks |

#### 4.1.2 Slots

| Slot | Levels | Content | Format | Difficulty by part | Marks |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | RB1 | (a) a present value, annuity or perpetuity; (b) a loan payment, NPV or IRR, with one convention change | N, N | (a) d2 4, (b) d3 4 | 8 |
| Q2 | RB2, RB3 | (a) price a coupon bond; (b) bootstrap a discount factor or a forward rate; (c) a DV01 hedge or a convexity-corrected price change | N, N, N | (a) d2 3, (b) d3 3, (c) d4 4 | 10 |
| Q3 | EQ1, EQ2 | (a) a margin-call price or an ex-dividend price; (b) a two-asset volatility, Sharpe ratio or CAPM return | N, N | (a) d2 3, (b) d3 3 | 6 |
| **Q4** | MK1, MK3 | **Exit slot A.** Explain a limit order book and the bid-ask spread: how the book is ordered, what happens to an arriving order, what the spread is and why it exists (the MK1 rubric, lines 1 to 5) | Long-form | d3 | 10 |
| **Q5** | MK1 | **Exit slot B.** (a) Given a book, the average fill and slippage of a market order; (b) explain when you would use a market order and when a limit order, with the costs and risks of each (the MK1 rubric, lines 6 to 9, rescaled) | N, long-form | (a) d4 3, (b) d3 7 | 10 |
| Q6 | MK2, MK3 | (a) effective and realised spreads or an auction's uncrossing price; (b) Glosten-Milgrom quotes or Roll's spread | N, N | (a) d3 3, (b) d4 4 | 7 |
| **Q7** | DV3 | **Exit slot C.** Derive put-call parity for European options on a share without dividends by a no-arbitrage argument, then state the trade if $C - P > S_0 - Ke^{-rT}$ (the DV3 rubric, European part, extended with the trade) | Long-form | d3 | 10 |
| Q8 | DV1 | (a) a futures margin account; (b) a forward price with income or a covered-interest-parity arbitrage | N, N | (a) d2 3, (b) d3 4 | 7 |
| Q9 | DV2, DV3 | (a) sketch a strategy's payoff; (b) a bound violation and the profit locked in | P, N | (a) d2 3, (b) d4 3 | 6 |
| Q10 | OP1 | (a) the risk-neutral probability; (b) a two-step European price; (c) the American put's exercise decision at one node | N, N, N | (a) d2 2, (b) d3 4, (c) d4 2 | 8 |
| Q11 | OP2, OP3 | (a) which statement about $N(d_2)$ is right; (b) a P&L explained by delta, gamma, theta and vega; (c) which option to buy for a stated volatility view | C, N, C | (a) d2 2, (b) d4 4, (c) d3 2 | 8 |
| Q12 | DV3, OP3 | A quoted chain with bid and ask: (a) select every arbitrage (parity, convexity, bounds); (b) the cheapest trade for one of them and its profit after spreads | set, N | d5 4 and 6 | 10 |

**Totals.** Marks 100. By difficulty: d2 $4 + 3 + 3 + 3 + 3 + 2 + 2 = 20$; d3 $4 + 3 + 3 + 10 + 7 + 3 + 10 + 4 + 4 + 2 = 50$; d4 $4 + 3 + 4 + 3 + 2 + 4 = 20$; d5 10. Long-form $10 + 7 + 10 = 27$. Generator slots (Q1 to Q3, Q5(a), Q6, Q8 to Q10, Q11(b) and Q12) carry 69 marks.

**Coverage.** Every form tests all 14 levels except at most one of MK2 and EQ1 (rotated through Q3(a) and Q6(a)), meeting `ASSESSMENT.md`'s 80% rule. By course: RB 18, EQ 6, MK 27, DV 28, OP 21 (Q12 split evenly between DV and OP). MK is weighted above its lesson share (21%) because two of the three exit slots sit there.

**Worked example of one slot (Q7, d3).** Model answer: (1) Portfolio A: one European call and a zero-coupon bond paying $K$ at $T$, cost $C + Ke^{-rT}$. (2) Portfolio B: one European put and one share, cost $P + S_0$. (3) At $T$, if $S_T \ge K$: A is worth $(S_T - K) + K = S_T$; B is worth $0 + S_T = S_T$. If $S_T < K$: A is worth $0 + K = K$; B is worth $(K - S_T) + S_T = K$. (4) Equal payoffs in every state, and neither can be exercised early, so with no arbitrage their prices are equal: $C + Ke^{-rT} = P + S_0$. (5) If $C - P > S_0 - Ke^{-rT}$, A is dear and B cheap: sell the call, buy the put, buy the share, borrow $Ke^{-rT}$; the cash taken in today is $C - P - S_0 + Ke^{-rT} > 0$ and the position is worth exactly zero at $T$. Rubric (10): 2 for the portfolios (lines 1 and 2); 3 for the payoffs in both cases (line 3); 2 for the no-arbitrage step and the European condition (line 4); 3 for the trade, its direction and why it is riskless (line 5). CI checks the identity with SymPy and the trade's payoff in both cases.

#### 4.1.3 The 1.7 exit, rebuilt in the app

| Part | Curriculum line | Gate (in the app) | Confirm (logged) | Taught in | Runs on |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `1.7.exit` | Explain a limit order book, the bid-ask spread, and market against limit orders; derive put-call parity; price a European option on a binomial tree in Python | `exm.fin` at 70% with `slotMin` 0.7 on Q4, Q5 and Q7; capstone drill `drl.fin.exit-binomial` passing every hidden test | None: no public paper sets these tasks; the explanation slots are also offered as an optional spoken `viva` rehearsal | MK1, MK3 (29 to 34, 40, 45); DV3 (60, 61, 63); OP1 (64 to 70) | Both (standard library and NumPy) |

```ts
// exit data for 1.7, using ASSESSMENT.md 2.10's ExitPart; no new instrument kinds
{ id: '1.7.exit',
  text: 'Explain a limit order book, the bid-ask spread, and market against limit orders; derive put-call parity; price a European option on a binomial tree in Python',
  gate: [ { kind: 'exam', exam: 'exm.fin', pass: 0.7, slotMin: { q4: 0.7, q5: 0.7, q7: 0.7 } },
          { kind: 'drill', drills: ['drl.fin.exit-binomial'] } ],
  confirm: [],
  estHours: 2 }
```

**The exit drill `drl.fin.exit-binomial`** (d4, needs NumPy; a standard-library reference also passes). The learner writes `price_tree(S0, K, u, d, R, n, kind)` (an explicit tree with gross growth $R$ per step) and `price_crr(S0, K, r, sigma, T, n, kind)`, both European, both overwriting one array by backward induction. Hidden tests:

1. **Exact tree.** `price_tree(Fraction(4), 5, Fraction(2), Fraction(1, 2), Fraction(5, 4), 3, 'call') == Fraction(288, 125)` and the put gives `Fraction(108, 125)`, with $q = \frac12$.
2. **Parity in every tree.** For 20 seeded trees, call minus put equals $S_0 - KR^{-n}$ exactly in `Fraction`s, and within $10^{-12}$ in floats.
3. **Convergence.** `price_crr` with $n = 2{,}000$ is within $2 \times 10^{-3}$ of Black-Scholes for four cases (an at-the-money call and put with $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$, $T = 1$; a put struck at 110 on 100 with $r = 3\%$, $\sigma = 30\%$, $T = 0.5$; a call struck at 40 on 50 with $r = 0$, $\sigma = 40\%$, $T = 2$); measured errors are about $10^{-3}$ or less.
4. **One step.** With $n = 1$ the price equals lesson 64's replication, $\frac{20}3$.
5. **Inputs.** $d \ge R$, $R \ge u$, $n < 1$ or $\sigma \le 0$ raise `ValueError`.
6. **Memory.** $n = 20{,}000$ runs within the drill's memory limit, which a full $O(n^2)$ tree exceeds.
7. **Time.** $n = 2{,}000$ in under 1 second on the web target (the reference takes about 0.01 s in CPython with NumPy) [unverified on an iPhone in the Artifact; arch-decision's E8 measurement decides whether this test runs there].

Known-wrong solutions: discounting once by $R$ instead of $R^n$ (fails tests 1 and 2); weighting by a "real" probability of one half (fails test 3); storing the whole tree (fails test 6); a recursion without memoisation (fails test 7). The C++ rewrite of 1.9's exit is timed against this function.

**Order and test-out.** A learner may sit `exm.fin` before the 1.7 lessons to test out (`ASSESSMENT.md` 7.3), and the exit drill can be attempted at any time; both are needed for the exit part.

### 4.2 Item 2.3: `exm.proj.methods`

#### 4.2.1 The paper

| Property | Value |
|---|---|
| Kind | `topic` (`ASSESSMENT.md` 3.1); a change request, because `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.8 lists 2.3 with no topic exam (decision 18, open item 2) |
| When | Unlocks when the learner's second project is finished; closes the topic together with the exit |
| Duration | 90 minutes, one clock |
| Items | 11 questions, 100 marks: Section A, methods (9 questions, 70 marks); Section B, defence of the learner's own finished projects (3 written questions, 30 marks) |
| Difficulty mix, by marks | 20 at d2, 50 at d3, 20 at d4, 10 at d5 |
| Format mix, by marks | Long-form 40 (Q5 and Section B); numeric 52; single choice 5; set selection 3 |
| Aids | Calculator; no notes; no access to the learner's repository during the paper (Section B is written from memory, as at interview) |
| Pass | **70 of 100** |
| Forms | At least 3; Section A's numeric slots are generator slots (52 marks); Section B draws from a pool of 6 prompts per question with variants for each project kind |
| Help | None during the paper; long-form answers are marked afterwards against the rubric (self-marked with Bertie's suggested marks and evidence quotes) |

#### 4.2.2 Slots

| Slot | Levels | Content | Format | Difficulty by part | Marks |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | RM2 | (a) Sharpe and Sortino from a short series; (b) beta, alpha and the appraisal ratio | N, N | (a) d2 4, (b) d3 4 | 8 |
| Q2 | RM3 | (a) the Sharpe ratio's standard error and interval; (b) a minimum track record; (c) what positive autocorrelation does to $\sqrt{12}$ | N, N, C | (a) d2 3, (b) d3 3, (c) d2 2 | 8 |
| Q3 | RM4 | (a) the expected best of $N$ noise strategies; (b) a deflated Sharpe ratio; (c) PBO from a small performance matrix | N, N, N | (a) d3 3, (b) d4 4, (c) d3 3 | 10 |
| Q4 | RM5 | (a) select the training samples to purge and embargo; (b) CPCV splits and paths | set, N | (a) d3 3, (b) d2 3 | 6 |
| Q5 | BT4, RM1 | Audit a described backtest with planted defects: find them, give each one's direction and fix (the BT4 rubric) | Long-form | d3 | 10 |
| Q6 | BT3, AR5 | The break-even cost and the capacity of a strategy with square-root impact | N | d4 | 6 |
| Q7 | AR3 | (a) a rank IC; (b) an IC $t$-statistic with overlapping horizons; (c) an information ratio from IC and breadth | N, N, N | (a) d2 3, (b) d3 3, (c) d2 2 | 8 |
| Q8 | OA3, MS4 | (a) forward volatility between two expiries and a calendar check; (b) a queue's waiting time and Little's law | N, N | (a) d3 4, (b) d3 4 | 8 |
| Q9 | MS4, MS3 | (a) a percentile from a latency histogram; (b) which measurement suffers coordinated omission | N, C | (a) d2 3, (b) d3 3 | 6 |
| B1 | RM5, RM7 | Describe the held-out design of one of your finished projects (dates or data, the lock, how often it was opened) and its result against in-sample; for a systems project, the replay test and the live run | Long-form | d3 | 10 |
| B2 | RM3, RM4, BT4 | Name the biggest threat to the validity of one project's headline number, the test you ran against it, and what it showed | Long-form | d4 | 10 |
| B3 | RM4, RM7 | "Your result is the best of the things you tried." Answer with your trial count, your correction (deflated Sharpe ratio, haircut, or for options and systems projects the agreement between independent methods) and what failed | Long-form | d5 | 10 |

**Totals.** Marks 100. By difficulty: d2 $4 + 3 + 2 + 3 + 3 + 2 + 3 = 20$; d3 $4 + 3 + 3 + 3 + 3 + 10 + 3 + 4 + 4 + 3 + 10 = 50$; d4 $4 + 6 + 10 = 20$; d5 10. Long-form 40.

**Section B rubric (each question, 10).** 3: the specific facts of the learner's project stated (dates, counts, numbers), consistent with their finished results note, which the marker sees beside the answer; 3: the method explained (why the test addresses the threat); 2: the result stated honestly, including where it was weak; 2: one limitation and the next test. An answer that contradicts the learner's own results note scores at most 4.

**Worked example of one slot (Q6, d4).** "Gross alpha 5% a year at USD 50m with impact costs of 0.8% that scale with the square root of assets, and other costs 1% a year whatever the size. (a) Net alpha at USD 50m. (b) The size that maximises net dollar profit. (c) The size at which net alpha is zero." Answer: (a) $5 - 0.8 - 1 = 3.2\%$. (b) Profit $A[4 - 0.8\sqrt{A/50}]$; derivative $4 - 1.2\sqrt{A/50} = 0$ gives $\sqrt{A/50} = 3.33$, $A = $ USD 556m. (c) $\sqrt{A/50} = 5$, USD 1.25bn. Marks: 2, 2, 2. CI recomputes each with SymPy.

#### 4.2.3 The 2.3 exit, rebuilt in the app

| Part | Curriculum line | Gate (in the app) | Confirm (logged) | Taught in | Runs on |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `2.3.exit` | Three finished projects, each with tests and a written results note | Three project records marked finished (`ASSESSMENT.md` 11.4), of three different kinds among `backtest`, `alpha`, `options`, `market-data` and `bot`, each with its note predicates passing (section 3.4.0) | The public repositories (the web app can check the URLs through GitHub's API on request) | RM, BT8, AR8, OA8, MS9 | Both (pastes); repository check on the web only |

```ts
// exit data for 2.3; needs the projectKind list and `distinct` requested in open item 6
{ id: '2.3.exit',
  text: 'Three finished projects, each with tests and a written results note',
  gate: [ { kind: 'project', projectKind: ['backtest', 'alpha', 'options', 'market-data', 'bot'], count: 3, distinct: true,
            notePredicates: ['sections-complete', 'metrics-consistent'] } ],   // kind-specific predicates come from each project's brief
  confirm: [],
  estHours: 210 }
```

**How faithful the rebuild is.** The curriculum's line is met exactly: three projects, each with tests (a pasted `pytest -q` with no failures) and a written results note including what failed. The app adds, as conditions of "finished", what the curriculum asks of every project elsewhere in 2.3 (a README; held-out periods, costs and look-ahead checks for backtests) and canon-gap P1's checklist as note predicates, which a reader would check anyway. The track rule: the planner proposes the three kinds in `projectOrder`; a learner may substitute, keeping three distinct kinds.

## 5. Reading and resource map per level

### 5.0 How to use this map

- **One resource per topic** (curriculum rule 1), in the curriculum's order for 1.7: **Khan Academy Finance and Capital Markets** (interest, bonds, shares), then **Zerodha Varsity** modules on stock markets, futures and options, then **Hull, Options, Futures, and Other Derivatives, 11th edition** (`src.hull-ofod-11e`), with **Harris, Trading and Exchanges** (`src.harris-trading-exchanges`) beside course MK. **MIT 15.401** (`src.mit-15401`) and **Yale ECON 252** (`src.yale-econ252`) are the lecture parallels; each row says which to watch. For 2.3 there is no single textbook (canon-gap G4): the spine is **López de Prado, Advances in Financial Machine Learning** (`src.afml-2018`) for RM and BT, **Grinold and Kahn** (`src.grinold-kahn-2e`) for AR, **Hull chapters 20 and 21 with Gatheral and Glasserman** for OA, and **Kleppmann and Riccomini** (`src.ddia-2e`) with the ITCH specification for MS, plus the papers named.
- **Problems over reading** (rule 2). Rows say what to do and carry hours that become `Level.companion` estimates. 1.7's rows sum to 26.0 h (22.0 watching and reading, 4.0 problems); 2.3's to 47.5 h (39.5 and 8.0). Lectures are budgeted at 1.25 times speed.
- **Hull's problems** are cited by chapter and number only; the Student Solutions Manual (paid) has the worked answers to the practice questions. Varsity is free online; its examples use Indian markets, which the lessons recreate with US and UK numbers.
- **[check]** marks a chapter, lecture, module or section number or title, or a URL path, recalled but not confirmed here. Chapter titles for Hull 11th edition listed in canon-gap section 6 were searched there; the others are [check].
