## 6. Verification approach

Every auto-marked item has two independent implementations of its answer (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 6.2): the TypeScript generator or the author's declared answer, and a Python verifier written from the generator's plain-language semantics without reading the TypeScript, run in CI under `tools/zqverify` with pinned NumPy, SciPy, SymPy, pandas, statsmodels and mpmath, and QuantLib where the widget catalogue's decision 16 uses it for day counts and bond analytics [QuantLib pin unverified]. CI also replays every distractor through the runtime grader and requires it to be marked wrong with its intended diagnosis. This file adds four rules [our choice]:

1. **Two routes to every price.** Every price has a second route in CI: replication against risk-neutral expectation; a tree against Black-Scholes; a closed-form Greek against a central difference; a bootstrap against a direct solve; parity against both legs priced separately. A shared method would share its mistakes.
2. **Exact where the answer is rational.** Trees with rational $u$, $d$ and $R$, ledgers in integer cents, order books in integer ticks and lots, parity and margin arithmetic: all in `fractions.Fraction` or integers, compared exactly.
3. **Known truths for data items.** Every 2.3 data item is generated by `market-gen` from stated parameters (true IC, volatility, autocorrelation, planted defects), and its answer is graded against the estimate's sampling distribution, not against a single number: CI checks that the reference solution lands within 3 standard errors on 1,000 seeds and that every distractor (a leak, a wrong lag) lands outside on at least 99% of them.
4. **Never grade realised P&L** (decision 10). Simulations and games grade decisions against exact expectations; realised outcomes are shown only.

| Family | Items | CI: second implementation | Runtime check | Traps the check must catch |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Time value, loans, real rates | RB1 | Closed forms against a direct sum of discounted flows in `Fraction`s (exact for rational rates); IRR by `brentq` against a polynomial root solve (NumPy) with every real root listed | `numeric` with tolerance in currency (0.01) or rate (0.0001), convention chip parsed | Multiplying by the growth factor; simple discounting; annuity due off by one; one IRR reported for two |
| Bonds and curves | RB2, RB3 | Prices and yields against QuantLib fixtures; bootstraps against a linear solve of the cash-flow matrix; duration and convexity against central differences of price; swaps against two bonds | `numeric`; `matrix` for discount-factor vectors | Coupon bond's yield used as a zero rate; Macaulay for modified; wrong day count |
| Shares, margin, portfolios | EQ1, EQ2 | Adjustment factors against a re-simulation of the corporate action; margin calls by exact inequality solving; portfolio volatility against $\sqrt{w^\top\Sigma w}$; tangency against a numerical maximisation of the Sharpe ratio (SciPy) | `numeric`, `point` on `portfolio-lab` | Volatilities averaged; tangency from $\mu$ not $\mu - r_f$; call at zero equity |
| Order books and auctions | MK1, MK2 | `exchange-sim` (TypeScript) against an independent Python matcher written from the rule set, property-tested with Hypothesis on random order streams (quantities conserved; no trade at a worse price than either limit; price-time priority) | `state` predicates on the book; `numeric` for fills | Filling at the arriving price; pro-rata assumed; ties broken by size |
| Microstructure | MK3 | Glosten-Milgrom quotes by exact Bayes in `Fraction`s against a simulation of $10^6$ trades; Roll's estimator against the spread of the generating process; effective and realised spreads recomputed from the tape | `numeric` | A spread from costs only; Roll with the wrong sign; realised spread forced positive |
| Forwards, futures, hedges | DV1 | Cash-and-carry prices against both arbitrage portfolios' cash flows (each must net to the stated profit); margin accounts against a day-by-day ledger; hedge ratios against OLS on the generated data | `numeric`; `predicate: arbitrage` on `carry-arb` positions | Forecast read as price; undiscounted value of an existing forward; top-up to maintenance |
| Payoffs, bounds, parity | DV2, DV3 | Payoff decompositions checked at every kink and between kinks exactly; arbitrage claims checked by evaluating the proposed position's payoff on a grid of $S_T$ (non-negative everywhere, positive somewhere) and by the linear programme that finds the cheapest super-replication (SciPy `linprog`) | `payoff` and `arbitrage` predicates; `derivation-steps` checks | Undiscounted strikes; an "arbitrage" that loses in some state; slopes for slope changes |
| Trees | OP1, exit drill | Exact rational trees against path-sum formulas $\sum_j\binom nj q^j(1 - q)^{n - j}(\cdot)$; American prices against a second implementation with explicit node storage; CRR against Black-Scholes with the error bound | `numeric` per node; `set` for exercise nodes; `code` | Real probability; one-period discounting; missing early exercise |
| Black-Scholes, Greeks, implied volatility | OP2, OP3, OA2, OA6 | SciPy and mpmath at 30 digits for prices; Greeks against central differences with the step rule of OA6; implied volatility round trips; P&L attribution against full repricing with the residual reported | `numeric`; `expression` for $d_1$ and $d_2$ forms | $d_1$ and $d_2$ swapped; vega per point or per unit; IV returned for prices outside the bounds |
| Performance statistics | RM2, RM3 | Metrics against independent implementations (pandas against NumPy); standard errors against Monte Carlo over $10^4$ seeded series; HAC against statsmodels; bootstrap intervals' coverage checked by simulation | `numeric` with the item's tolerance | Per-period and annual units mixed; one-sided turnover; IID bootstrap on dependent data |
| Selection and overfitting | RM4 | Expected maxima against simulation of $10^6$ maxima; DSR against the paper's worked example and against a simulation of the null; PBO by brute force over every split | `numeric` | Trials undercounted; ties in ranks mishandled |
| Validation splits | RM5 | Purged folds checked by interval-overlap tests on every pair; CPCV counts by enumeration | `set` over sample indices; `numeric` | One-sided purge; embargo omitted |
| Cross-sectional research | AR1 to AR7 | IC, ICIR, Fama-MacBeth and factor regressions against statsmodels on `market-gen` panels with known truths; neutralised exposures checked to be zero; BH against a direct implementation | `numeric` within sampling tolerance; `predicate` on exposures | Same-period returns; pooled $t$; dollar-neutral taken as beta-neutral |
| Surfaces and pricers | OA3 to OA5 | Calendar and butterfly flags against brute-force checks on the generated surface with planted violations; SVI fits against SciPy least squares from several starts; Monte Carlo against Black-Scholes within 4 standard errors over 200 seeds (coverage near 99.99%); PDE against Black-Scholes | `numeric`, `set` of violating strikes | Averaging volatilities; unconstrained fits; antithetic pairs counted twice |
| Systems | MS1 to MS8 | Every system item is checked by running code: book builders and candle aggregators against recorded fixtures with stored outputs; percentiles against NumPy on the raw samples; queue answers against a discrete-event simulation; idempotency by replaying twice; state machines by exhaustive exploration of event orders up to length 6 | `output`, `state`, `numeric` | Right-closed bars; averaging percentiles; increment upserts; resending with new client ids |
| Code drills | every set; the Pricing Lab; projects' starter suites | `zqtest` suites run against `reference.py` on CPython 3.11 and on Pyodide's CPython; every `wrong/*.py` must fail the test it names; drills tagged `micropython-ok` also run there; project starter suites run on CPython with the pinned stack and publish their test names | Visible examples on Run; hidden tests at submission; the first failing test mapped to a diagnosis | Float cash; unseeded randomness; look-ahead (the shuffle-the-future test is in every strategy drill) |
| Long-form items | every set and both papers | CI checks every model-answer line (each equation by SymPy under its assumptions, each number recomputed) and that each rubric point names lines that exist (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 6.8) | Self-marking against the rubric with Bertie's suggested marks and evidence quotes (`AI-TUTOR.md` 2.7) | Rubric points no line evidences |
| Projects | `prj.proj.*` | Paste-verify of `pytest -q` and gate output; `metrics.json` validated against the published schema; `results-note` predicates (`sections-complete`, `metrics-consistent` and the kind-specific ones); repository URL by GitHub API on the web | `setup-checklist` project mode | Notes whose numbers do not match the metrics; a failed gate pasted as passed (the parser checks the summary line) |
| The papers | `exm.fin`, `exm.proj.methods` | CI rules of `ASSESSMENT.md` 3.2 on every form: equal marks, the 20/50/20/10 mix within 5 points, coverage at 80% of levels, at least 50% of marks from generators, reserve capacity for six fresh sittings | Exam engine | A form off the mix; a generator slot with too few fresh points |

**What verification cannot prove** (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 6.13): that a hook engages, that a trap catches the misconception it targets, that a rubric rewards understanding over vocabulary, and, specific to these items, that a market described in words really has the structure the item assumes (a "competitive dealer", "informed traders", "a liquid universe"), or that a project's results note is honest about what it did not try. The first group is handled by the owner's review of each level and by item statistics after release (a trap fewer than 30% fall into, or a distractor nobody picks, is rewritten). For market descriptions every item states its model in one line of metadata ("the dealer earns zero expected profit on each trade"), and the reviewer signs off that the wording forces it. For notes, the defence (`viva`, `exm.proj.methods` section B) is the check: it asks the questions a sceptical reader would, against the learner's own repository and note.

---

## 7. Sample lesson in full: "What is money later worth today?"

**Why this lesson.** Decision 23: every price in 1.7 and 2.3 is a present value or is built from one. A bond is a sum of discounted payments; a forward is priced by carrying money forward; an option is a discounted expectation; a strategy is judged against what its capital could have earned. The lesson also carries the first two errors of finance, comparing amounts paid at different dates as if they arrived together and multiplying by the growth factor to "discount", and it introduces the argument every later price rests on: a promise that sells below its present value is free money for anyone who can borrow, so its price cannot stay there. Stage 0 taught compound interest forwards (0.1 lesson 26, GCSE); this lesson turns it round.

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Id | `lsn.rates.present-value` (course RB, level RB1, lesson 1 of 1.7) |
| Length | 11 screens (1 title, 1 summary, 9 tasks) and a quiz of 4 items; about 11 minutes of screens and 4 of quiz |
| Requires | 0.1 percentages as multipliers and compound interest (0.1 lesson 26, `lsn.propprec.repeated-change`, repeated multiplication by $1 + r$; its skill id is fixed when that lesson's file is written); 0.3 negative powers |
| Introduces | `skl.fin.present-value`, `skl.fin.discount-factor`, `skl.fin.compare-at-one-date`, `skl.fin.price-by-replication`; terms "present value", "discounting", "discount factor" (embargoed for Bertie until screen 5's Check) and "law of one price" (until screen 9's Check) |
| Frame (why it exists) | Money can be lent and borrowed, so money at different dates can be swapped for each other at the going rate. Without a way to move every amount to one date, any comparison of money paid at different times is a guess, and most guesses favour the bigger number paid later |
| Insight (the aha line) | To compare money at different dates, move it all to one date at the rate you can save and borrow at; the present value of a future payment is the deposit today that grows into it, and that deposit is also its price |
| Misconceptions | `mc.fin.face-value-comparison` (the hook's bait), `mc.fin.sum-without-discounting` (the trap's bait), `mc.fin.discount-multiplies`, `mc.fin.simple-discounting`, `mc.fin.discount-once`, `mc.fin.discount-at-last-date`, `mc.fin.discount-today-flow`, `mc.fin.preferences-not-prices`, `mc.fin.pv-is-opinion`, `mc.fin.negative-rate-impossible`, `mc.fin.higher-rate-higher-pv`, `mc.fin.discount-basis` |
| Worked example with a decision point | Screen 7: three payment plans at 5%; ranking by totals puts £11,000 in a year above £10,500 today, and discounting reverses it, because £10,500 grows to £11,025 by then |
| Trap and edge cases | Trap: screen 6 (bait: "£3,000"). Edges: screen 8 (a negative rate gives a discount factor above 1; a payment today is not discounted), screen 9 (a price below present value is an arbitrage) |
| Transfer | Screen 10: a zero-coupon gilt's price is a discount factor, and it implies a rate |
| Sources | Khan Academy Finance and Capital Markets, unit "Interest and debt", the present value videos [check titles]; Hull ch. 4 (Interest Rates), the sections on compounding [check]; MIT 15.401 session 2, "Present Value Relations I" [searched] |
| Lesson-wide tutor guidance | Do not say "present value", "discount" or "discount factor" before screen 5's Check; never state 10,816, 9,985.21, 2,775.09 or 92.16 before the screen that asks for it; prefer "what date are you comparing them at?" and "what would you need to put in the bank today to end up with exactly that?" to statements |

Throughout, the timeline is `cashflow-timeline`: years along the bottom, each payment an arrow above its year with its amount; the rate sits in a chip ("4%, annual"). Arrows are drawn in ink with text labels; no colour carries meaning (design research 11).

---

**Screen 1. Title** (`title`)

- **Heading:** What is money later worth today?
- **Prose:** "A pound today and a pound in two years are not the same thing. This lesson finds the exchange rate between them."
- **Art:** Bertie holding two envelopes, one stamped with a calendar, his moustache tilted in suspicion.
- **Action:** Continue.

---

**Screen 2. The hook** (`problem`, id `hook`)

- **Prose:** "You have won a prize. Take £10,000 today, or £10,800 in two years. Your savings account pays 4% a year, added once a year."
- **Widget:** `cashflow-timeline` with years 0, 1 and 2; prize A as an arrow at year 0 labelled "£10,000", prize B as an arrow at year 2 labelled "£10,800"; the chip "4%, annual". Nothing is draggable yet.
- **Question:** "Which prize is worth more?" (`choice`)

| Option | Correct? | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|---|
| A. £10,000 today | yes | | "Right, though most people pick B. The next screen shows why A wins." |
| B. £10,800 in two years: it is more money (the bait) | no | `mc.fin.face-value-comparison` | "£10,800 is more pounds, but later pounds. Compare them at the same date: what does £10,000 become by year 2?" |
| C. They are worth the same: £10,800 is 8% more, and 4% for two years is 8% | no | `mc.fin.simple-discounting` | "Interest is added on interest. Two years at 4% grows money by more than 8%." |
| D. It depends on when you want to spend it | no | `mc.fin.preferences-not-prices` | "You can move money between dates at 4%, so one prize can beat the other whatever your plans." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Suppose you take A and save it. How much would you have when B pays out?" Point: highlights year 2 on the timeline. Step: "£10,000 grows by 4% in year 1 and by 4% again in year 2. Compare the result with £10,800."
- **Why?** Yours: "You compared the two prizes at the same date." Then: "Money today can be saved, so it is worth more than the same money later. The question is how much more."
- **Tutor.** Focus: comparing at one date. Notes: most learners choose B, and C is common among those who remember simple interest. Avoid: the words "present value" and "discount".

---

**Screen 3. Grow it** (`explore`, id `grow`)

- **Prose:** "Put prize A in the account. Step through the years and watch the interest arrive."
- **Widget:** `compounding-lab` (blocks) linked to the timeline: a stack showing £10,000, with a year stepper; each step adds an interest block of 4% of the whole stack and moves the stack's arrow one year along the timeline. Prize B's arrow stays at year 2 for comparison.
- **Continue when:** the stepper has reached year 2 (`continueWhen: grow/step == 2`).
- **What the learner sees:** year 1, £10,400 (a £400 block); year 2, £10,816 (a £416 block, because the £400 also earned interest). At year 2 the stack's arrow stands beside B's arrow, £16 taller.
- **Why?** (shown on Continue) "At year 2, prize A is £10,816 and prize B is £10,800. Prize A wins by £16, measured at year 2."
- **Tutor.** Focus: growth by repeated multiplication. Notes: some learners expect £10,800 at year 2 (simple interest); point to the second block.

---

**Screen 4. Turn it round** (`ladder`, id `deposit`, constraint "Find the deposit that grows into £10,800")

- **Prose:** "Now run the bank backwards. How much would you need to deposit today to have exactly £10,800 in two years?"
- **Widget:** `cashflow-timeline` with a draggable arrow at year 0 (its amount set by dragging or typing, snapping to pennies), and a live readout of what it grows to at year 2: "Grows to £[amount × 1.0816]". Prize B's arrow is fixed at year 2.
- **Question:** "Deposit today:" (`numeric-input`, pounds, tolerance £0.01)
- **Correct:** £9,985.21 ($\frac{10{,}800}{1.04^2}$).

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| £10,000 | `mc.fin.simple-discounting` | "£10,000 grows to £10,816, not £10,800. The deposit must be a little smaller." |
| £10,384.62 | `mc.fin.discount-once` | "That grows to £10,800 in one year. You have two years of interest to undo." |
| £11,681.28 | `mc.fin.discount-multiplies` | "That multiplies by 1.04 twice. To undo growth, divide." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "Watch the readout: adjust the deposit until it grows to exactly £10,800." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Growing multiplies by 1.04 each year. What undoes a multiplication?" Point: highlights the readout and the year-0 arrow. Step: "Divide £10,800 by 1.04 to go back one year, then by 1.04 again: $\frac{10{,}800}{1.04 \times 1.04}$."
- **Why?** Yours: "You divided by 1.04 twice: $\frac{10{,}800}{1.04^2} = £9{,}985.21$." Then: "In today's money, prize B is worth £9,985.21 and prize A £10,000: the same verdict, reached at year 0."
- **Tutor.** Focus: undoing growth. Notes: learners who drag rather than divide reach the answer by search, which is fine; ask them afterwards what calculation the readout is doing.

---

**Screen 5. Name it** (`generalise`, id `rule`)

- **Prose:** "What you found has a name. The present value of $C$ paid in $t$ years is the deposit that grows into it, at rate $r$."
- **Rule (shown):** $PV = \dfrac{C}{(1 + r)^t} = C \times (1 + r)^{-t}$. The multiplier $(1 + r)^{-t}$ is the **discount factor**, and moving money back in time is **discounting**.
- **Widget:** `derivation-steps` with two lines given and one blank: "Grow for $t$ years: $PV \times (1 + r)^t = C$." "So: $PV = $ [blank]."
- **Question:** "Complete the line. Then: the 2-year discount factor at 4% is?" (`expression-input` for the blank; `numeric-input` to 4 decimal places for the factor)
- **Correct:** $\frac{C}{(1 + r)^t}$ (any equivalent form, such as $C(1 + r)^{-t}$); 0.9246.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| $C(1 + r)^t$ | `mc.fin.discount-multiplies` | "That grows $C$ forward. Solve the first line for $PV$: divide both sides by $(1 + r)^t$." |
| $\frac C{1 + rt}$ | `mc.fin.simple-discounting` | "That would undo simple interest. Compound growth needs the power $t$." |
| 1.0816 | `mc.fin.discount-multiplies` | "That is the growth factor. The discount factor is its reciprocal." |
| 0.92 | rounding | "Give four decimal places: $\frac1{1.0816} = 0.9246$." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "The first line says the deposit grows into $C$. Which side has $PV$ on it?" Point: highlights $(1 + r)^t$ in line 1. Step: "Divide both sides by $(1 + r)^t$. For the factor, compute $1 \div 1.04^2$."
- **Why?** Yours: "Discounting divides by the growth factor: $PV = C(1 + r)^{-t}$." Then: "£1 in two years is worth £0.9246 today at 4%. Every later price in this item multiplies by factors like this one."
- **Tutor.** Focus: the formula as a description of screen 4's steps. Notes: say "you did this on the last screen" rather than introducing the formula as new.

---

**Screen 6. A stream of payments** (`trap`, id `stream`, trap `mc.fin.sum-without-discounting`, bait option A)

- **Prose:** "A lottery pays £1,000 a year for three years, the first in one year. The rate is still 4%."
- **Widget:** `cashflow-timeline` with three £1,000 arrows at years 1, 2 and 3, and a running "Total of arrows: £3,000" label.
- **Question:** "What is the lottery worth today?" (`choice`)

| Option | Correct? | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|---|
| A. £3,000: three payments of £1,000 (the bait) | no | `mc.fin.sum-without-discounting` | "That adds pounds from three different years as if they all arrived today. Bring each one back to year 0 first." |
| B. £2,775.09 | yes | | "Yes. Each payment has its own discount factor, then the present values add." |
| C. £2,884.62: £3,000 divided by 1.04 | no | `mc.fin.discount-once` | "The later payments are further away. Each needs its own number of years." |
| D. £2,666.99: £3,000 divided by $1.04^3$ | no | `mc.fin.discount-at-last-date` | "Only the last payment is three years away. The first is one year away." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Which year is each payment in?" Point: highlights each arrow in turn with its year. Step: "$\frac{1{,}000}{1.04} + \frac{1{,}000}{1.04^2} + \frac{1{,}000}{1.04^3}$."
- **Why?** Shown: "$961.54 + 924.56 + 889.00 = £2{,}775.09$." Then: "Present values can be added because they are all in today's pounds. Amounts at different dates cannot."
- **Tutor.** Notes: most learners choose A; the screen exists to be failed once. Avoid: the word "annuity" (lesson 4 introduces it).

---

**Screen 7. Choosing a payment plan** (`problem`, id `plans`, the worked example with a decision point)

- **Prose:** "A different bank pays 5% a year. You are offered three plans. Plan A: £3,000 a year for four years, from year 1. Plan B: £11,000 in one year. Plan C: £10,500 today."
- **Widget:** `tvm-timeline` (pv) with three rows, one per plan, each row's arrows on a shared year axis; a `value-table` beside it with a "Total" column (shown: £12,000, £11,000, £10,500) and a "Value today" column the learner fills, one cell per plan; the chip "5%, annual".
- **Question:** "Fill in each plan's value today, then rank the plans from most to least valuable." (`parts`: three `numeric` cells, tolerance £1; then `ordering`)
- **Correct:** A £10,637.85; B £10,476.19; C £10,500.00. Ranking A, C, B.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| Ranking A, B, C | `mc.fin.face-value-comparison` | "That is the order of the totals. Compare the values today: C's £10,500 beats B's £10,476.19." |
| B's value £11,000 | `mc.fin.face-value-comparison` | "Plan B's £11,000 arrives in a year. Bring it back to today: divide by 1.05." |
| C's value below £10,500 (for example £10,000.00, which is $\frac{10{,}500}{1.05}$) | `mc.fin.discount-today-flow` | "Plan C is paid today: its value today is its amount. Only future payments are discounted." |
| A's value £12,000 | `mc.fin.sum-without-discounting` | "Plan A's four payments sit in four different years. Discount each one." |
| A's value £9,872.43 (all £12,000 discounted by $1.05^4$) | `mc.fin.discount-at-last-date` | "Only the last payment is four years away; the first is one year away." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Which plans have payments in the future, and how far?" Point: highlights plan B's single arrow at year 1. Step: "B: $\frac{11{,}000}{1.05} = 10{,}476.19$. A: $3{,}000 \times \left(\frac1{1.05} + \frac1{1.05^2} + \frac1{1.05^3} + \frac1{1.05^4}\right)$. C needs no discounting."
- **Why?** Yours: "You ranked by value today: A £10,637.85, then C £10,500, then B £10,476.19." Then: "The decision point is B against C. B pays £500 more, but a year later; £10,500 saved at 5% is £11,025 by then."
- **Tutor.** Focus: one date for every comparison. Notes: learners often get A right by its large total and B against C wrong. Reps: comparing at year 1 instead (C grows to £11,025; B is £11,000) gives the same ranking, which shows the choice of date does not matter, only that there is one.

---

**Screen 8. When rates go below zero** (`ladder`, id `negative`, constraint "The rate is negative")

- **Prose:** "In 2019 some government bonds in Europe paid negative yields [check the year and the bonds]. Suppose the one-year rate is $-0.5\%$."
- **Widget:** `compounding-lab` (simple) with the rate slider extended below zero, showing £1 shrinking to £0.995 over a year, beside a discount-factor readout that is hidden until Check.
- **Question:** "What is the one-year discount factor?" (`numeric-input`, 4 decimal places)
- **Correct:** 1.0050 ($\frac1{0.995}$).

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| 0.9950 | `mc.fin.discount-multiplies` | "That is the growth factor. Discounting divides by it: $\frac1{0.995}$." |
| 1.0000, or "undefined" | `mc.fin.negative-rate-impossible` | "The formula still works. A negative rate means money shrinks in the bank, so future money is worth more." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "Compute $1 \div (1 - 0.005)$." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "At $-0.5\%$, what does £1 deposited today become in a year?" Point: highlights the £0.995 in the stack. Step: "To have £1 in a year you must deposit $\frac1{0.995}$ today."
- **Why?** Yours: "A discount factor above 1: money later is worth more than money now." Then: "This happens when holding cash costs something, as in 2019 for large holders. A payment due today always has a discount factor of exactly 1."
- **Tutor.** Focus: the formula needs no positive rate. Notes: some learners refuse negative rates as "impossible"; point to the bank account shrinking on screen.

---

**Screen 9. Why it is a price** (`derive`, id `price`, leap: naive "present value is only my estimate of what a promise is worth to me"; fails "a promise sold for less hands a riskless profit to anyone who can borrow at 4%"; points to "the present value is the price the market must charge: the law of one price")

- **Prose:** "A bank offers to sell you a promise: £10,800 paid in two years, for £9,900 today. You can borrow and lend at 4%."
- **Widget:** `derivation-steps` (order mode) with five cards to arrange, beside a `cashflow-timeline` that draws each card's cash flows as it is placed: "Borrow £9,900 today at 4%." "Pay the £9,900 for the promise." "In two years, receive £10,800." "Repay the loan: £9,900 × $1.04^2$ = £10,707.84." "Keep the difference." One distractor card: "Deposit £9,900 of your own money."
- **Question:** "Put the steps in order, leaving out any that are not needed. How much do you keep in two years?" (`ordering` with `exclude`, then `numeric-input`, pounds)
- **Correct:** borrow, pay, receive, repay, keep (the distractor excluded); £92.16.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| The distractor card included | `mc.fin.pv-is-opinion` | "You need none of your own money. The loan pays for the promise, and the promise repays the loan." |
| £900 | `mc.fin.face-value-comparison` | "The loan charges interest. You repay £10,707.84, not £9,900." |
| £85.21 | timing | "£85.21 is the saving measured today. In two years it is worth £85.21 × 1.0816 = £92.16." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "Compute £10,800 minus £9,900 × 1.0816." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Could you buy the promise without any money of your own?" Point: highlights the "Borrow" card. Step: "Borrow £9,900, buy the promise, and in two years the £10,800 repays £10,707.84 of loan."
- **Why?** Yours: "You kept £92.16 with no money and no risk." Then: "Everyone who can borrow would do this, so a promise cannot sell below its present value for long. That is the law of one price, and it sets every price in this item."
- **Tutor.** Focus: replication. Notes: this is the creative step of the lesson; if a learner asks "why would the bank do that?", the answer is that it would not for long, which is the point. Avoid: "arbitrage" before the Check (lesson 47 names it formally).

---

**Screen 10. The price of £100 later** (`problem`, id `gilt`, transfer)

- **Prose:** "The UK government sells promises like this: a zero-coupon gilt pays £100 in two years. Its price today is £92.46."
- **Widget:** `bond-lab` (cashflows, with the yield hidden) showing one £100 arrow at year 2 and the price £92.46 at year 0; a `numeric-input` for the rate.
- **Question:** "What yearly rate, added once a year, does this price imply?" (`numeric-input`, percent, tolerance 0.01 points)
- **Correct:** 4.00% ($\left(\frac{100}{92.46}\right)^{1/2} - 1$).

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| 8.15% | per-period confusion | "That is the growth over both years together. The question asks for the rate a year." |
| 4.08% | `mc.fin.simple-discounting` | "That halves the two-year growth, as simple interest would. Compounded, 4.08% grows £92.46 to £100.15, not £100." |
| 3.77% | `mc.fin.discount-basis` | "That divides the £7.54 discount by the £100 face value and halves it. A rate grows the price: $(100/92.46)^{1/2} - 1$." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "The price is the present value: $\frac{100}{(1 + r)^2} = 92.46$. Solve for $r$." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "The price is a present value. What does $\frac{100}{(1 + r)^2}$ have to equal?" Point: highlights the price label. Step: "$(1 + r)^2 = \frac{100}{92.46} = 1.0816$, so $1 + r = 1.04$."
- **Why?** Yours: "The gilt's price is 100 times the 2-year discount factor, 0.9246, which means a rate of 4%." Then: "Markets quote prices, and rates are read from them. Lesson 7 builds bonds with coupons from exactly these pieces."
- **Tutor.** Focus: a price is a discount factor. Notes: the number deliberately matches screen 5's factor; ask the learner whether they noticed.

---

**Screen 11. Summary** (`summary`)

- **Ideas:**
  1. "Compare money at different dates only after moving it to one date, at your saving and borrowing rate."
  2. "The present value of $C$ paid in $t$ years is $C(1 + r)^{-t}$: the deposit today that grows into it. Present values add; future amounts do not."
  3. "A present value is a price. A promise selling below it hands a free profit to any borrower."
- **Trap:** "Adding or comparing amounts paid in different years as if they all arrived today."
- **Card (saved to the Notebook):** $PV = C(1 + r)^{-t}$; discount factor $d_t = (1 + r)^{-t}$; at 4%, $d_1 = 0.9615$, $d_2 = 0.9246$; a negative rate gives $d_t > 1$; a payment today has $d_0 = 1$.

---

### 7.1 Lesson quiz (4 items, 1 mark each, no hints, one attempt, Why? after each)

| # | Item | Type | Correct | Distractors and their misconceptions | Why? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | "What is £5,000 paid in three years worth today, at 6% a year added once a year?" | N (pounds, tolerance £0.01) | £4,198.10 | £4,237.29 (`mc.fin.simple-discounting`, $\frac{5{,}000}{1.18}$); £5,955.08 (`mc.fin.discount-multiplies`); £4,716.98 (`mc.fin.discount-once`) | "$5{,}000 \times 1.06^{-3} = 4{,}198.10$. Three years of growth are undone by three divisions." |
| 2 | "Value today of £1,200 in one year and £1,200 in two years, at 5%?" | N | £2,231.29 | £2,400 (`mc.fin.sum-without-discounting`); £2,176.87 (`mc.fin.discount-at-last-date`); £2,285.71 (`mc.fin.discount-once`) | "$\frac{1{,}200}{1.05} + \frac{1{,}200}{1.05^2} = 1{,}142.86 + 1{,}088.44$. Each payment has its own factor." |
| 3 | "At 3% a year, which is worth most today? (a) £1,000 now; (b) £1,025 in one year; (c) £1,060 in two years; (d) £1,090 in three years" | C | (a) | (d), the largest amount (`mc.fin.face-value-comparison`); (c) (`mc.fin.simple-discounting`: 6% simple over two years makes it look like £1,000); (b) (a slip) | "Today's values: £1,000, £995.15, £999.15, £997.50. Only (a) is worth a full £1,000." |
| 4 | "Select every true statement. (a) At a positive rate every discount factor for a future date is below 1. (b) The 4-year discount factor is the square of the 2-year one, at a constant rate. (c) A present value is the sum of the face amounts. (d) At a higher rate, a future payment's present value is higher." | M | (a) and (b) | (c) (`mc.fin.sum-without-discounting`); (d) (`mc.fin.higher-rate-higher-pv`) | "$(1 + r)^{-4} = \left[(1 + r)^{-2}\right]^2$. A higher rate means more growth to undo, so a lower present value." |

**Marks and mastery.** Each item scores 1 mark; 3 of 4 marks the lesson Mastered (`ASSESSMENT.md` decision 26). Wrong items join the Redo queue at 7 and 30 days. Two of four items are choice items (50%), offset across course RB, which stays at 34% (section 0.4).

- **Checks.** Every distractor in sections 7 and 7.1 is replayed through the grader and must be marked wrong with its stated diagnosis (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 6.6); the leak lint confirms no tutor field contains an accepted answer; copy lints run on every learner-facing string (sentences at most 20 words, at most 60 words of prose per screen, Why? at most 3 sentences, no banned words, British spelling).
- **What a reviewer must judge.** Whether screen 2's option D is fair (a learner with a liquidity need might reasonably prefer cash now, which still makes A the answer); whether screen 8's historical example is current and correctly dated [check].
